# Unit u16 briefs: Part XVI sector deep dives, Chapters 77 to 83

Version 1.1, October 3, 2026 (revision round 1 after the blueprint review; see the revision log at the end). Author: brief writer for unit u16. Binding on the writers of Chapters 77 to 83. This revision applies the rulings in `ownership-resolutions.md`, the Case Bible annexes (`case-bible-annex-p.md` 4.14; `case-bible-annex-tr.md` T.13, T.14, T.16, T.17, R.2, R.3, R.9) and the four blueprint review reports; a writer follows this file without consulting them.

## Conventions used in every brief in this file

- Output is LaTeX (decision D-008). File names are `chapters/NN-slug.tex` as fixed in `architecture.md`. Labels follow the style sheet scheme (sec:N.M, ssec:N.M.K, ex:N.K, exh:N.K, cl:N.K, exr:N.K, eq:N.K, fw:slug).
- Every sector chapter covers, in this order of priority: industry economics; how the asset works (adding only what Chapters 11 and 12 do not teach); revenue models; key risks; the contract set; typical financing terms; modeling specifics; landmark deals and failures. The order of sections in each brief serves teaching, not the checklist.
- Cross-references to other chapters are given by chapter number and, where the architecture fixes it, by the owned concept. Other unit briefs (u01 to u15) were being written in parallel; writers resolve section-level anchors against the anchor registry before drafting and report any anchor that does not exist.
- Financing terms. Every number about market terms comes either from a fact sheet (cited by slug) or is an indicative range under decision D-011. Each D-011 range in these briefs is marked "(indicative, D-011)" and must be printed as "indicative", with the market and period, framed by its drivers, and signed off by the domain-expert reviewer. Ranges that a fact sheet lists under "Do not state" appear here only with the D-011 label and the instruction to present them as practitioner consensus, never as verified data. Writers may narrow or drop a D-011 range if the reviewer will not sign it; they may not widen it into a precise figure.
- Running-case numbers are never invented. Where a figure is needed it is cited as "from Case Bible ledger: ID" or as a Case Bible input with its section. Small installments marked "(small)" in Case Bible Part 6 print no running-case numbers other than Case Bible inputs.
- All worked-example numbers below were computed in Python by the brief writer (script retained in the session scratchpad). Writers recompute every number before printing; the numbers auditor checks them.
- Real-case facts come only from the named fact sheets. Each fact sheet's "Do not state" list binds the chapter.
- Revision round 1 conventions. Every worked example names a place, a market, a date and its parties; parties marked "(fictional)" are invented and must never be presented as real organizations, and real places and markets are used only as settings. All new or changed numbers in this revision were recomputed in Python on October 3, 2026 (scripts in the session scratchpad); the expected results are stated with each example and exercise. Every Tier 2 and Tier 3 calculation exercise lists its inputs and its key answers so that the writer and the numbers auditor can check the solution. Headings follow style sheet 3.1 and 4.1: sentence case, serial comma, no dashes, no colon except "Case P:", "Case T:", "Case R:" and "Walkthrough:", and no heading shared with another chapter apart from the fixed element headings (Practitioner's notebook, Judgment drill, Exercises, Solutions to exercises). The section titles in each locked table of contents below supersede the anchor registry's titles for these chapters (central change listed in the revision log); labels and numbers are unchanged except where a new label is marked "(new, appended)".
- Sector placement of the second-wave fact sheets. Each chapter's "Real cases and fact sheets" list names, for every sheet, the subsections where it is used and the angle. A sheet named there is binding: the writer teaches its facts at that place, within its "Do not state" list.
- Ownership in Part XVI (ruling R-101, D-029): each chapter owns only the sector-specific mechanics, structures, frameworks and terms listed under "Owned". Asset physics stays with Chapters 11 and 12, contract forms with Chapters 17 to 28, instruments with Chapters 29 to 34, structuring with Chapters 35 to 38, modeling method with Chapter 45 and life-cycle practice with Chapters 61 to 65; these chapters cite them. Terms whose glossary home is Chapter 12 (ruling R-026) are used without bold and cross-referenced to their Chapter 12 home.
- Target lengths are floors for the core teaching of each sector, not caps (standards review defect 7).

# Chapter 77. Refining, petrochemicals, and manufacturing finance

Rulings applied:
- R-113 and D-028: chapter title "Refining, petrochemicals, and manufacturing finance" (parenthetical removed; serial comma). The gigafactory remains the chapter's manufacturing case in the text.
- R-101 and D-029: the chapter owns only the items in architecture.md's Owns note for Chapter 77; asset physics (Chapter 12), EPC (Chapters 22 and 23), sponsor support instruments (Chapter 26) and sizing (Chapters 35 to 37) are cited.
- R-054: the industrial form is called a "processing agreement (industrial tolling)" throughout; power tolling stays with sec:18.7 and liquefaction tolling with ssec:21.2.3.
- R-050 and D-022: multi-unit completion tests are a sector application. The definition of financial completion is cited to ssec:26.5.2 and lenders' completion tests in practice to sec:61.11 (fw:completion-test-matrix); "reliability run" is cited to its home ssec:22.4.2.
- R-103: the bank price deck is owned by Chapter 75 (ssec:75.3.2); this chapter cross-references it (CR-u16-1 closed).
- R-073: §45X changes are cited to sec:67.10 (US credit constraints after 2025) and t-us-tax-credits; no law detail is taught here.
- R-105: the Case P beat follows Case Bible Part 6 row 77, with the inputs of Annex P 4.14.
- Standards review defect 3 (template headings): sec:77.6, sec:77.7, sec:77.8, ssec:77.8.1, ssec:77.8.2, ssec:77.8.3 retitled; defect 1 (colon heading): ssec:77.7.4 retitled; defect 4 (unlocated examples): all five examples located; defect 6 (exercise inputs): Tier 2 and Tier 3 inputs and answers added; coverage defect 4 and 5 (second-wave sheets): refinery-pf and us-battery-doe placed.

## 77.1 Header

- Chapter: 77
- Title: Refining, petrochemicals, and manufacturing finance
- File: `chapters/77-industrial-projects.tex`
- Target length: 9,000 to 10,000 words
- Dominant lens: a senior project finance banker who has run ECA-heavy industrial financings and sat on the credit committee for a manufacturing deal, with the independent engineer's view of multi-unit completion testing.

## 77.2 Purpose and capability statement

Purpose. Industrial plants turn a bought input into a sold output, and they earn the spread between the two. That makes them the hardest assets in the book to fit into project finance, whose tools were built for contracted, availability-style revenue. This chapter teaches the reader to see a refinery, a petrochemical complex and a battery-cell gigafactory as spread or margin businesses; to identify who carries margin risk, ramp-up risk and supply-chain risk in each; to recognize the structures that make such plants financeable (integrated sponsors, feedstock and offtake arrangements, processing fees, sponsor completion guarantees released only on demanding tests, ECA anchoring); and to explain why Sadara and Northvolt Ett, both financed by sophisticated lender groups, ended up where they did.

What you will be able to do:
- Compute a refining crack spread, a cracker cash margin and a factory's breakeven yield, and say which number a lender should size against (Capabilities 4, 13).
- Rank the ways an industrial sponsor can protect a margin, from merchant sale to processing fee, and show how each moves debt capacity (Capabilities 2, 5).
- Read and test a multi-unit completion test and the sponsor guarantee it releases (Capabilities 3, 10).
- Explain, from the Sadara, Duqm, Northvolt and US DOE battery-plant records, why sponsor-supported industrial debt still carries market and ramp-up risk (Capabilities 13, 15).

## 77.3 Concepts owned and assumed

Owned (taught in full here):
1. Spread (margin) business as a revenue type: revenue equals product value less feedstock cost; why lenders treat it as the riskiest revenue class short of pure merchant power.
2. Refining economics at financier depth: crack spreads (3-2-1 as the teaching benchmark), gross refining margin, capture rate, configuration and the Nelson complexity index (credited to W. L. Nelson, as an established measure), crude differentials, turnarounds.
3. Petrochemical economics: feedstock advantage (ethane against naphtha), co-product credits, cash cost margin per tonne of ethylene, integration with refining and with feedstock suppliers, the capacity-wave cycle.
4. Manufacturing (gigafactory) economics: nameplate, throughput ramp, yield and scrap, unit cost per good unit, breakeven yield, learning curves and product-price deflation.
5. Industrial customer contracts: supply agreements with volume commitments, capacity reservation fees, customer prepayments; why order books are not take-or-pay.
6. Processing agreements (industrial tolling) in industrial plants: the processor earns a fee per tonne and the toller owns input and output (R-054; power tolling is owned by sec:18.7, liquefaction tolling by ssec:21.2.3; this is the industrial form and the glossary home of "processing agreement", ssec:77.5.2).
7. Framework 77.1, the margin protection ladder (original).
8. Technology licenses and process performance guarantees from licensors; the gap between a licensor's guarantee and the lenders' loss.
9. Multi-unit completion tests for industrial complexes as a sector application (R-050): envelopes of units, reliability runs (term home ssec:22.4.2), product specification tests, and the financial completion test (definition ssec:26.5.2; practice and release sec:61.11 and sec:61.12) that releases sponsor guarantees.
10. Feedstock supply and product marketing agreements with integrated sponsors (sponsor marketing, lifting, marketing fees).
11. Industrial supply-chain and geopolitical risk as a credit risk (single-route feedstock and product logistics; dependence on one country's equipment and materials).
12. Industrial policy finance in manufacturing: public lenders and guarantors (EIB, ECAs, national guarantee offices) and conditional commitments drawn on milestones; the difference between commitments and funding.
13. Modeling specifics for industrial plants: joint price decks for inputs and outputs, margin correlation, yield and throughput ramp curves, inventory-heavy working capital, turnaround downtime.

Assumed (with the teaching chapter):
- Oil, gas and LNG chains, and processing plants in outline: Chapter 12.
- Real versus nominal prices and indexation: Chapter 5. Probability and P-values, correlation: Chapter 9.
- EPC and its limits: Chapter 22. EPCM, split contracts, wraps, interface agreements: Chapter 23.
- Sponsor support and completion guarantees as instruments, and the definition of financial completion: Chapter 26 (ssec:26.5.2). Lenders' completion tests in practice and release of support: Chapter 61 (sec:61.11, sec:61.12). Reliability run: ssec:22.4.2. Equity bridge, standby and cost-overrun facilities: Chapter 31.
- Federal loan programs and public lenders as a lender type: Chapter 29.
- ECAs, DFIs, A/B loans, content rules: Chapter 29. Islamic structures and sukuk with conventional tranches: Chapter 33. Intercreditor voting and ECA majorities: Chapter 53.
- CFADS and DSCR: Chapter 35. Sizing, sculpting, tenor and merchant tails: Chapter 36. Reserves, sweeps, lock-ups, hedging: Chapter 37.
- Working capital and inventory modeling: Chapter 41. Scenarios and Monte Carlo implementation: Chapter 43. Ramp-up modeling mechanics (traffic ramp-up method, reused here for throughput): Chapter 45.
- Bank price decks for commodities: Chapter 75 (ssec:75.3.2), per R-103.
- Insolvency regimes (US Chapter 11, Swedish bankruptcy referenced only): Chapter 64.
- Tax-credit monetization in the US (for the §45X reference): Chapter 32; US credit constraints after 2025: sec:67.10 (R-073); law detail dated in t-us-tax-credits.

Forward references: Chapter 78 (mining) for the commodity-price lender's toolkit applied to a depleting asset, one sentence in the close. Chapter 88 for evaluating new industrial-policy structures (one sentence in 77.11).

## 77.4 Locked table of contents

Opening (no heading). Northvolt Ett: the "Snowball" facilities of July 28, 2020 (about USD 1.6 billion, first and second lien, EIB and three ECA-covered tranches); four years later, on November 21, 2024, a Chapter 11 filing with about USD 30 million of cash, enough for about a week, and a plant at 16 GWh of capacity against a 60 GWh target. The opening poses the puzzle: the lenders had financed a factory with the tools built for a power plant. Source: northvolt.

What you will be able to do (unnumbered).

77.1 Plants that earn a spread (sec:77.1)
- 77.1.1 Revenue that is a difference between two prices (ssec:77.1.1). Defines the spread business with a two-line illustration: a 1 USD/bbl move in crude with products unchanged against a 1 USD/bbl move in both. Contrasts with Chapter 18 capacity payments in one paragraph (cross-reference, no re-teaching).
- 77.1.2 Three plant types and what they share (ssec:77.1.2). Refinery, cracker complex, cell factory: scale, sponsors, why each sits at the edge of project finance. Uses Sadara (approximately USD 20 billion project cost, 26 units) and Northvolt Ett as the two poles.
- 77.1.3 Who sponsors industrial plants and why they borrow non-recourse (ssec:77.1.3). Integrated strategics (Aramco and Dow at Sadara); national oil companies (OQ and Kuwait Petroleum Europe, 50/50 at the Duqm refinery, whose parent OQ kept lending subordinated shareholder loans of RO 115.0 million in 2021, RO 73 million in 2022, RO 331 million in 2023 and RO 167 million in 2024, the last repayable at OQ8's discretion; source refinery-pf); state industrial policy (EIB, KfW, national debt office guarantees; the US DOE's ATVM loans to automaker battery joint ventures, source us-battery-doe); customers as investors (Volkswagen; Scania as DIP lender later). Four lenses: the sponsor wants leverage and off-balance-sheet risk; the lenders want the sponsor's integration; the host wants jobs and industrial policy; the EPC and technology licensors want their scope fenced.

77.2 Refining economics (sec:77.2)
- 77.2.1 Crack spreads and the gross refining margin (ssec:77.2.1). Example 77.1. Equation 77.1 (3-2-1 crack). Excel implementation.
- 77.2.2 Configuration and complexity (ssec:77.2.2). Hydroskimming, cracking and coking configurations; Nelson complexity index as a measure of conversion capability; light-heavy crude differentials as the coking refinery's margin source. Exhibit 77.1 (TikZ process flow: crude unit, vacuum unit, cracker, coker, hydrotreaters, product blending).
- 77.2.3 Capture rate and why realized margins trail the benchmark (ssec:77.2.3). Yield slate, crude slate, product discounts, own fuel and losses; turnarounds every few years remove a month or more of throughput (no specific frequency stated as fact; writers describe it as a multi-year cycle).

77.3 Petrochemical economics (sec:77.3)
- 77.3.1 Feedstock advantage (ssec:77.3.1). Example 77.2 (ethane against naphtha cracker).
- 77.3.2 Integration and co-products (ssec:77.3.2). Why Sadara's mixed-feed cracker and aromatics plant needed Aramco feedstock agreements and Dow technology and marketing; integration as a credit substitute.
- 77.3.3 The capacity-wave cycle (ssec:77.3.3). Margins collapse when a wave of new capacity arrives; a lender's tenor straddles at least one trough. Explains why price decks for petrochemicals are run through a full cycle rather than off a forward curve.

77.4 Factory economics (sec:77.4)
- 77.4.1 Throughput, yield, and scrap (ssec:77.4.1). Example 77.4. Equation 77.2 (unit cost per good unit) and Equation 77.3 (breakeven yield).
- 77.4.2 Learning curves against price deflation (ssec:77.4.2). The race between cost-down and price-down; a factory that ramps on schedule can still fail if product prices fall faster than its cost curve. Stegra comparator paragraph here (t-frontier-data).
- 77.4.3 Customer contracts and the difference between orders and take-or-pay (ssec:77.4.3). Supply agreements, volume bands, specification and qualification, capacity reservation fees and prepayments. Northvolt's reported USD 55 billion of orders against its inability to deliver on time and to specification. Contrast: the captive cell plants financed by the US DOE (us-battery-doe): Ultium Cells (GM and LG Energy Solution, USD 2.5 billion ATVM loan, late November 2022), BlueOval SK (Ford and SK On, up to USD 9,633,040,000, December 13, 2024, with Ford guaranteeing 50%) and StarPlus Energy (Stellantis and Samsung SDI, USD 7.54 billion including USD 688 million capitalized interest, December 2024, with Stellantis guaranteeing 49% of drawn debt). Each plant sold almost all output to its sponsoring automaker under sponsor guarantees and support agreements, so the loans were sponsor credit dressed as project loans; when US EV demand expectations fell in 2025, the exits were sponsor-funded prepayment (Ultium, May 2025, with a USD 1.8 billion GM loan at 5.7%), joint-venture dissolution with Ford assuming USD 3,805,040,000 of DOE debt at 4.814% to July 15, 2040 under corporate-style covenants (closing May 20, 2026), and continuation (StarPlus). Do not state that DOE forced or cancelled any of the loans, the original loans' interest rates, or what happened to the roughly USD 4.0 billion of BOSK advances Ford did not assume.

77.5 Protecting the margin (sec:77.5)
- 77.5.1 The margin protection ladder (ssec:77.5.1). Framework 77.1 with five rungs (see Frameworks).
- 77.5.2 Processing fees against merchant margins (ssec:77.5.2). Example 77.3 (debt capacity of a urea plant under a processing fee against merchant sale).
- 77.5.3 Who pays for protection (ssec:77.5.3). Each rung transfers upside as well as downside; the sponsor's IRR falls as the ladder rises; the host may supply feedstock below market as its contribution (stated as a structure, without asserting any real price).

77.6 Completion, feedstock, technology, and market risk in process plants (sec:77.6)
- 77.6.1 Completion and ramp-up in multi-unit plants (ssec:77.6.1). Why a 26-unit complex cannot be tested like a single gas turbine; interface between units; partial completion and pre-completion revenues (Sadara's term sheet allowed pre-completion net revenues to pay project costs and fund distributions, subject to conditions). Duqm's schedule: 89% progress at end-2021, 11% behind schedule, mainly because of COVID-19 constraints (OQ's own attribution); inauguration in February 2024 (refinery-pf; the date of first crude or commercial operation is not stated).
- 77.6.2 Feedstock, utilities, and associated facilities (ssec:77.6.2). Single-source feedstock; utilities from third parties (Sadara: Marafiq, SEC); price formulas tied to product prices as a partial natural hedge. Associated facilities financed outside the project company: at Duqm the crude handling and storage at Ras Markaz and the crude terminal sat in other OQ group companies, one of which signed a USD 350 million facility on April 8, 2020 whose repayment starts at the earlier of April 2023 and 12 months after the refinery's commercial operation date, and OTTCO's tank terminal, built larger than OQ8 needed, was impaired in 2024 (recoverable value RO 157.0 million) because no third-party customers came (refinery-pf). Lesson: infrastructure built for one anchor plant carries that plant's volume and timing risk.
- 77.6.3 Technology and licensing (ssec:77.6.3). Licensor process guarantees and their usual remedies (make-good, capped liquidated damages) in principle; the gap to lenders' exposure.
- 77.6.4 Market, customer, and policy risk (ssec:77.6.4). Customer concentration; EV demand slowdown and Asian price competition (Northvolt's own explanation); policy support that can change (US §45X changes under OBBBA, dated October 3, 2026, from t-us-tax-credits and cited to sec:67.10: wind components produced and sold after December 31, 2027 lose §45X, and a phase-out is added for applicable critical minerals from 2031 to 2033; no rates stated). Public lenders' own policy risk: DOE renamed its Loan Programs Office the Office of Energy Dominance Financing and in January 2026 said more than USD 83.6 billion of Biden-era loans and commitments were being restructured, revised or eliminated (us-battery-doe; one sentence, with a forward reference to Chapter 88).
- 77.6.5 Supply chain and geopolitics (ssec:77.6.5). Sadara's production halt of March 31, 2026, attributed to supply-chain disruption (high confidence for the halt and date; medium for the Hormuz detail, which must be presented as reported); Northvolt's reliance on Chinese suppliers for some materials and machinery.

77.7 Licenses, feedstock, marketing, and construction contracts for a process complex (sec:77.7)
- 77.7.1 The industrial contract map (ssec:77.7.1). Exhibit 77.2 (TikZ contract map for an integrated petrochemical complex, Illustrative: project company; two sponsors; feedstock supplier; technology licensor; EPC or EPCM contractors; marketing agent; utilities supplier; lenders including ECA and sukuk holders; land lessor).
- 77.7.2 Technology license and process guarantee (ssec:77.7.2). Clause 77.1 (process performance guarantee and its remedy, Illustrative) with annotations.
- 77.7.3 Feedstock supply, marketing, and lifting agreements (ssec:77.7.3). Sadara's Dow marketing right outside the Middle East and the 2021 change to pro rata sponsor marketing with fees; what lenders gain and lose when the marketer is a shareholder. Duqm's crude supply and offtake terms are not verified (refinery-pf "Do not state"); the writer says only that crude reaches the refinery through Ras Markaz.
- 77.7.4 EPC packages, EPCM, and the wrap for a process complex (ssec:77.7.4). Cross-reference to Chapter 23; sector point only: a refinery or cracker is commonly built by several contractors under an EPCM or multiple lump-sum packages, so the sponsor guarantee carries the completion risk that no single EPC contract holds. Real-case instance: Duqm's three EPC packages (process units; utilities and offsites; crude pipeline, Ras Markaz storage and Duqm port export facilities), with the ECA mix following the contractors' nationalities: UKEF insurance of approximately GBP 700 million tied to Petrofac's scope (SMBC Europe as ECA agent bank), alongside KEXIM, K-SURE and CESCE (refinery-pf). Do not state the 2018 debt total, lender count, Islamic tranche, tenors or pricing, or JBIC participation.
- 77.7.5 Sponsor completion support for industrial plants (ssec:77.7.5). Cross-reference to ssec:26.5.2 for the instrument and the definition of financial completion and to sec:61.11 and sec:61.12 for completion tests and release in practice (R-050); sector point: the guarantee is debt-wide and long (Sadara: about USD 10 billion of guarantees, released at project completion in November 2020, seven years after financial close, per Aramco's 2021 disclosure as reported, medium confidence). Contrast the several (not joint) DOE-loan guarantees pro rata to ownership: Ford 50% of BlueOval SK, Stellantis 49% of StarPlus, each with share retention and subordination of sponsor claims (us-battery-doe), and BlueOval SK's USD 3.1 billion returns of capital to Ford after DOE draws, which raised Ford's exposure when the venture was unwound.

77.8 How industrial plants have been financed (sec:77.8)
- 77.8.1 Financing terms at Sadara, Northvolt Ett, Duqm, and the DOE battery plants (ssec:77.8.1). Exhibit 77.3 (table of verified terms with dates and sources): Sadara 2013 (approximately USD 12.5 billion senior debt; SAR 7.5 billion sukuk at six-month SAIBOR plus 95 bps, 15.75 years, 2.6 times oversubscribed; US Ex-Im approximately USD 4.975 billion direct loan; UKEF USD 700 million guarantee; completion guarantees; 2021 reprofiling from 2029 to 2038 final maturity with principal grace to June 2026 and a new USD 3.7 billion pro rata sponsor guarantee). Northvolt Ett 2020 (about USD 1.6 billion; EIB USD 350 million at LIBOR then SOFR plus 300 bps; Euler Hermes-covered USD 525 million and NEXI-covered USD 150 million at margins of 1.3%; KEXIM USD 100 million direct; first lien to December 31, 2029; second lien USD 255 million at 5% cash or PIK to June 30, 2030; hedging 70% to 110% of projected first-lien debt; swaps at 4.65% to June 30, 2027). Duqm (UKEF insurance of approximately GBP 700 million, May 2019 notice; KEXIM, K-SURE and CESCE named; associated crude handling facility USD 350 million, 10 years, US LIBOR plus a margin, 14 semiannual installments plus a balloon, USD 319.5 million drawn at December 31, 2022). DOE ATVM loans (Ultium USD 2.5 billion, 2022; BlueOval SK up to USD 9.63 billion and StarPlus USD 7.54 billion, December 2024; Ford's assumed note at 4.814% to 2040 with a USD 4 billion minimum Available Liquidity covenant and USD 1 billion cross-default thresholds).
- 77.8.2 Tenor, gearing, and ECA ranges for sponsor-backed process plants (ssec:77.8.2). D-011 indicative only, to be signed off by the domain reviewer: sponsor-guaranteed Gulf petrochemical complexes, 2010 to 2020, senior debt of roughly 50% to 65% of project cost with ECA and public-fund tranches of 12 to 16 years (indicative, D-011); European first-of-a-kind manufacturing plants with public lenders, 2018 to 2024, first-lien tenors of 8 to 10 years and ECA-covered margins well below uncovered commercial margins (indicative, D-011, with Northvolt as the only verified European point; the DOE loans show the US public-lender alternative, priced off federal funding and secured by sponsor guarantees rather than plant economics). Drivers ranked: sponsor credit and guarantee scope first; feedstock integration second; ECA content third; market cycle fourth.
- 77.8.3 Completion-release, sweep, liquidity, and hedging covenants (ssec:77.8.3). Completion-release tests, cash sweeps from margin upside, minimum liquidity (Ford's USD 4 billion Available Liquidity covenant on its assumed DOE note is the corporate-style end point), hedging bands (Northvolt's 70% to 110%), restrictions on expansion before stabilization (the Northvolt lesson), and limits on returns of capital funded from loan draws (the BlueOval SK lesson).

77.9 Modeling an industrial plant (sec:77.9)
- 77.9.1 Joint price decks and margin correlation (ssec:77.9.1). Model product and feedstock prices from one deck; never run independent sensitivities on correlated prices; show with Example 77.1's numbers how a crude rise with lagging product prices compresses margins. Cross-reference Chapter 43 for Monte Carlo implementation.
- 77.9.2 Throughput and yield ramp curves (ssec:77.9.2). Reuse the ramp-up mechanics of Chapter 45 for throughput and yield; two ramp curves multiply, so a modest lag in each compounds (show with Example 77.4).
- 77.9.3 Working capital, turnarounds, and maintenance capex (ssec:77.9.3). Inventory days on crude and product (cross-reference Chapter 41); turnaround downtime as a scheduled availability profile; reserve for turnaround costs (cross-reference Chapter 37 MMRA).

77.10 Sadara and the limits of sponsor support (sec:77.10)
Real case section. Facts from sadara only. Sequence: 2011 JV; 2013 split closing (sukuk first, then main financing); ECA-majority voting; three-envelope completion test with a 60-day reliability run in a 90-day window; completion November 2020; March 2021 reprofiling; 2025 loss of SAR 5.8 billion and accumulated losses of SAR 44.2 billion (medium confidence, single trade-press source; print with attribution); March 31, 2026 halt; Dow's guarantee liability increase of USD 298 million in Q1 2026 and USD 450 million estimated liability at June 30, 2026. Transferable lesson, stated as a mechanism: sponsor support converts a margin business into sponsor credit for the guarantee period and only for the risks the guarantee covers; after release, lenders own the margin, and integration contracts determine whether the sponsors will choose to keep supporting it.

77.11 Northvolt Ett and the factory financed like a power plant (sec:77.11)
Real case section. Facts from northvolt only. Sequence: equity rounds; Snowball facilities; production start December 2021; capacity 16 GWh against 60 GWh; expansion financing of December 2023 (USD 4.5 billion of commitments per the court filing; publicized as USD 5 billion; never drawn; unwound November 14, 2024); suspension September 23, 2024; Chapter 11 on November 21, 2024 with about USD 5.841 billion of funded debt; Scania DIP of USD 100 million; DSRA releases; Swedish bankruptcy March 12, 2025; Lyten agreement August 7, 2025 (completion not verified). Exhibit 77.4 (capital structure at the petition date, from the fact sheet table). One paragraph of contrast with the DOE battery joint ventures (us-battery-doe): there an investment-grade automaker guaranteed its share and took the plant's output, so the 2025 to 2026 demand shock became sponsor charges (Ford's USD 3.2 billion impairment in Q4 2025 and USD 3.6 billion of special charges at closing) rather than lender losses; Northvolt had no such parent. Transferable lesson: in a factory, ramp-up and yield are the credit; a DSRA and ECA cover do not substitute for a stabilized plant; expansion commitments conditioned on milestones are not liquidity; a captive offtake is only as good as the sponsor that stands behind it. One-sentence forward reference to Chapter 88 for evaluating industrial-policy structures.

77.12 Walkthrough: testing a petrochemical complex for completion (sec:77.12)
Task: the IE's completion certificate review for a three-envelope complex. Uses Clause 77.2 (completion test schedule, Illustrative) and Example 77.5. Steps listed under Walkthroughs.

77.13 Case P: Groupe Talmé's fertilizer plan (sec:77.13)
Small installment (see Running case).

77.14 Practitioner's notebook (sec:77.14). Fixed subheadings.

77.15 Judgment drill (sec:77.15). Fixed subheadings.

77.16 A margin needs an owner before it needs a lender (sec:77.16)
Close. Crystallizes: the ladder, the completion test, the commitment-versus-funding distinction. Last paragraph poses the next problem: a mine is also a price taker, but its plant consumes the deposit it stands on; how does a lender size debt against a commodity price and a depleting reserve at the same time (Chapter 78).

77.17 Exercises (sec:77.17). 77.18 Solutions to exercises (sec:77.18). Sources (unnumbered).

## 77.5 Opening

Open on the Northvolt Ett record from the fact sheet: in July 2020 a lender group led by public institutions committed about USD 1.6 billion to a single factory in Skellefteå on terms (ECA cover at 1.3% margins, a DSRA, mandatory hedging, first and second lien) that would look familiar on a gas plant. In November 2024 the parent filed for Chapter 11 with about a week of cash, and rescue liquidity came from releasing reserve cash and from a customer. The scene ends on the question the chapter answers: what did the lenders actually own?

## 77.6 Worked examples

Example 77.1. Crack spread and refinery EBITDA (Illustrative). Setting: Sabine Bend Refining LLC (fictional), a 150,000 bbl/d coastal cracking refinery near Port Arthur, Texas, on the US Gulf Coast; its lenders (a fictional club led by Gulfmark Bank) review the 2025 budget in January 2025. Crude USD 76.80/bbl; gasoline USD 2.24/gal; diesel USD 2.52/gal; 42 gal/bbl. Compute: gasoline USD 94.08/bbl, diesel USD 105.84/bbl; 3-2-1 crack = (2 x 94.08 + 105.84 - 3 x 76.80)/3 = USD 21.20/bbl. Utilization 91.5% gives 50,096,250 bbl a year. Capture rate 83% gives a realized margin of USD 17.60/bbl. Cash opex USD 5.20/bbl. EBITDA = 50.10 million bbl x (17.60 - 5.20) = USD 621.0 million. Re-run at a trough crack of USD 12.85/bbl (realized 10.67; EBITDA USD 273.8 million) and USD 8.10/bbl (realized 6.72; EBITDA USD 76.3 million). Breakeven crack = 5.20/0.83 = USD 6.27/bbl. Teaching point: a 62% fall in the benchmark spread (21.20 to 8.10) cuts EBITDA by 88% (621.0 to 76.3); the lender sizes on the trough, not the average. All numbers verified in Python (revision round 1).

Example 77.2. Ethane against naphtha cracker margins (Illustrative). Setting: two 2024 cracker budgets compared by an ECA underwriter: Calcasieu Olefins LLC (fictional), an ethane cracker in Louisiana, and Straits Olefins Pte Ltd (fictional), a naphtha cracker on Jurong Island, Singapore. Per tonne of ethylene, ethylene price USD 1,040/t. Ethane cracker: 1.24 t ethane per t ethylene at USD 245/t (feed cost USD 303.80), co-product credit USD 62, cash conversion cost USD 168: cash margin USD 630.20/t. Naphtha cracker: 3.20 t naphtha per t ethylene at USD 640/t (feed cost USD 2,048.00), co-product credits (propylene, butadiene, aromatics) USD 1,512, conversion cost USD 214: cash margin USD 290.00/t. At 1.5 million t a year of ethylene each (nameplate): USD 945.3 million against USD 435.0 million. Sensitivities: naphtha up 15% turns the naphtha margin to minus USD 17.20/t; ethane doubling to USD 490/t leaves the ethane margin at USD 326.40/t. Teaching point: feedstock advantage, and why a naphtha cracker's credit depends on co-product markets as much as on ethylene.

Example 77.3. Processing fee against merchant sale for a urea plant (Illustrative). Setting: Caroni Nitrogen Company Ltd (fictional), a gas-based urea plant at Point Lisas, Trinidad and Tobago, seeking debt in March 2024; Atlantic Nitrogen Trading SA (fictional, investment grade) offers a processing agreement (industrial tolling) as the alternative to merchant sales. A 1.21 million t a year plant, project cost USD 1,386.4 million, fixed cash costs USD 41.7 million a year borne by the project company in both cases (gas is supplied and paid for by the toller under the processing agreement; under merchant sale, gas cost is inside the margin). Case A, processing fee USD 162.40/t, 12-year amortizing debt at 7.25%, sized at 1.35x: revenue USD 196.5 million; CFADS USD 154.8 million; debt service USD 114.7 million; annuity factor 7.8379; debt USD 898.8 million (64.8% of cost). Case B, merchant: P50 margin USD 214/t and P90 margin USD 131/t over gas cost; lenders size at the lesser of 1.80x on P50 and 1.25x on P90, 10 years at 7.75%: CFADS USD 217.2 million (P50) and USD 116.8 million (P90); debt service USD 120.7 million and USD 93.4 million, so P90 binds at USD 93.4 million; annuity factor 6.7864; debt USD 634.2 million (45.7%). Difference USD 264.6 million of debt. Writer states the DSCR targets as illustrative and does not present them as market norms.

Example 77.4. Gigafactory ramp, yield, and breakeven (Illustrative). Setting: Elbe Cell GmbH (fictional), a lithium-ion cell plant in Saxony-Anhalt, Germany, financed in 2024 by a fictional lender club with an ECA-covered tranche; its lenders test the ramp plan in January 2025. Nameplate 17.6 GWh a year of cells started (deliberately not Northvolt's 16 GWh); good-cell price USD 98.0/kWh; materials USD 58.0 and variable conversion USD 6.5 per kWh started (scrap consumes materials); fixed cash costs USD 342.7 million a year. Years 1 to 4: throughput 25%, 55%, 80%, 92% of nameplate; yield 72%, 84%, 91%, 94%. Results (recomputed in revision round 1): good output 3.168, 8.131, 12.813, 15.220 GWh; revenue USD 310.5, 796.9, 1,255.7, 1,491.6 million; variable cost USD 283.8, 624.4, 908.2, 1,044.4 million; EBITDA (316.0), (170.2), 4.8, 104.5 USD million; cash cost per good kWh USD 197.76, 118.93, 97.63, 91.13. Breakeven yield: at 55% throughput 101.9% (impossible), at 70% throughput 94.2%, at 80% throughput 90.7%, at 85% throughput 89.2%, at 92% throughput 87.4%. Equations: unit cost = (started x (materials + variable conversion) + fixed)/(started x yield); breakeven yield = (started x (m + v) + F)/(started x price). Teaching point: below a throughput threshold no yield saves the plant; the lender's completion test must test throughput and yield together.

Example 77.5. Reading a reliability-run result against a three-envelope completion test (Illustrative). Setting: Rayong Integrated Petrochemical Company Ltd (fictional), a cracker, derivatives and aromatics complex at Map Ta Phut, Thailand; the independent engineer reports on the reliability run in October 2023 to a lender group led by a fictional Japanese bank with an ECA-covered tranche. Test (Clause 77.2): each envelope must average at least 95.0% of nameplate output over 60 consecutive days within a 90-day window, with off-specification product at most 2.0%; financial completion requires historical DSCR of at least 1.25x for two consecutive quarters. Results: envelope A (cracker) 96.8%, off-spec 1.1%; envelope B (derivatives) 93.9%, off-spec 1.6%; envelope C (aromatics and utilities) 97.4%, off-spec 0.7%; quarter 1 CFADS USD 61.4 million against debt service USD 46.2 million (1.329x, prints as 1.33x); quarter 2 USD 58.9 million against USD 47.0 million (1.2532x, passes at 1.25x whether computed to two or three decimals). Outcome: technical completion fails on envelope B; the financial test passes; the guarantee stays in place; the writer shows the retest options (retest window, partial release by envelope if the clause allows it, and why lenders resist partial release when envelopes depend on each other). Teaching point: a ratio this close to a threshold makes the computation and rounding rule decisive; the clause must say how the ratio is computed and whether it is rounded before comparison (a quarter at 1.2496x would fail if truncated and pass if rounded to two decimals).

## 77.7 Walkthrough

Walkthrough: testing a petrochemical complex for completion (Section 77.12).
1. Read the completion definition and the test schedule (Clause 77.2) before reading any data: list each condition (mechanical completion certificates for every unit, performance runs by envelope, product specifications, environmental permit compliance, financial test, no default, insurance in place, reserve accounts funded).
2. Map units to envelopes and identify interdependencies (cracker feeds derivatives): decide which envelope failures block which releases.
3. Check the test window and run data: continuity of the 60 days, allowable interruptions, how upsets are treated, who witnessed the run (IE).
4. Recompute each envelope's average and off-spec rate from daily logs (Example 77.5 data).
5. Recompute the financial test from the compliance certificate: CFADS and debt service definitions, rounding, and whether pre-completion revenue counts.
6. Write the IE's finding and the facility agent's response: pass, fail, or conditional pass; list retest steps and the long-stop date beyond which the guarantee becomes payable.
7. Draft the two-paragraph credit note the lender sends its committee.

## 77.8 Frameworks

Framework 77.1, the margin protection ladder (original; label fw:margin-ladder). Purpose: rank how firmly an industrial plant's margin is protected and predict the debt it supports. Rungs, from weakest to strongest:
1. Merchant sale of products and spot purchase of feedstock.
2. Sponsor marketing or lifting agreement without price support (the marketer sells; the project company keeps the price risk).
3. Formula-linked feedstock supply, where feedstock price moves with product price (a partial natural hedge) or a sponsor feedstock agreement with volume commitment.
4. Margin floor or offtake with floor price from a creditworthy party (sponsor or customer), or a customer take-or-pay with capacity reservation fee.
5. Processing fee under a processing agreement (industrial tolling) from a creditworthy toller who owns inputs and outputs.
Steps in use: place each revenue stream on a rung; weight by share of revenue; test the credit of the party on each rung; size debt rung by rung (cross-reference the bucket sizing of Chapter 36). Demonstrated on Example 77.3 and in the Case P installment. When to use: any plant whose revenue is a spread.

## 77.9 Real cases and fact sheets

- sadara: anchor case for petrochemicals; lesson: sponsor support, integration and ECA anchoring can finance a mega-complex, but after completion the lenders hold margin and supply-chain risk the contracts did not transfer (2021 reprofiling, 2026 halt). Used in 77.1.2, 77.3.2, 77.6.1, 77.6.2, 77.6.5, 77.7.3, 77.7.5, 77.8.1 and sec:77.10.
- northvolt: anchor case for gigafactory finance; lesson: ramp-up and yield risk, commitments versus funding, expansion before stabilization, rescue liquidity from DSRA releases and a customer. Opening, 77.4.3, 77.8.1, sec:77.11.
- refinery-pf (Duqm refinery, OQ8, Oman; placed in revision round 1): the chapter's verified refining case. Angle: an export refinery that is project-financed in form but carried by national-oil-company sponsors. Placed in ssec:77.1.3 (NOC sponsors and their subordinated shareholder loans), ssec:77.6.1 (schedule slippage), ssec:77.6.2 (associated facilities and the impaired OTTCO terminal), ssec:77.7.4 (three EPC packages and an ECA mix that follows the contractors) and ssec:77.8.1 (Exhibit 77.3 row). Lesson in one line: in a refinery the sponsors' balance sheets, not the debt documents, absorbed delay and margin risk, and facilities built around the anchor plant inherited its volume risk. Do not state the 2018 debt total (reported as about USD 4.6 billion), lender count, Islamic tranche, tenors, pricing, final cost, first-crude or COD date, Kuwait Petroleum's shareholder loans, JBIC participation, or Dangote facts.
- us-battery-doe (Ultium Cells, BlueOval SK, StarPlus Energy; placed in revision round 1). Angle: captive-offtake, sponsor-guaranteed public loans to manufacturing plants as the contrast to Northvolt. Placed in ssec:77.1.3, ssec:77.4.3, ssec:77.6.4 (public-lender policy risk), ssec:77.7.5 (several guarantees, returns of capital), ssec:77.8.1, ssec:77.8.3 and sec:77.11 (contrast paragraph). Lesson in one line: when a plant's only customer is its sponsor, the loan is a sponsor credit, and the exits are sponsor decisions. Respect the sheet's "Do not state" list (no claim that DOE forced or cancelled the loans; no original loan rates; no statement about the unassumed USD 4.0 billion or StarPlus production).
- t-frontier-data: Stegra (Boden, Sweden) as a one-paragraph comparator in ssec:77.4.2 (about EUR 6.5 billion secured by early 2024; a further EUR 1.4 billion round in 2026; on October 1, 2026 a review showed completion costs "significantly higher than assumed" and more capital needed; medium confidence; do not name the chair or the month of the 2026 round).
- t-us-tax-credits: §45X changes (dated, flagged) in ssec:77.6.4 only, cited to sec:67.10.

Open requests (the chapter can be taught without them):
- fact sheet requested: petro-rabigh (optional): Petro Rabigh as a second integrated refining and petrochemicals financing with a reprofiling history.

## 77.10 Running-case installment

Case P (small), story date 2025, Dabakro (place and month: "Groupe Talmé headquarters, Dabakro, May 2025"; writer may choose the month within 2025 and reports it). Character: Mariama Talmé (Chief Executive from 2021), with Tomasz Wierzbicki on a call. Inputs (Annex P 4.14, book inputs): Azotes de Bélanou SA, proposed in 2025 by Groupe Talmé (30%), an unnamed international fertilizer group (40%) and SNHK (30%); ammonia 1,200 t a day and granular urea 2,000 t a day; gas requirement about 46,000 MMBtu a day; capex about USD 1.6 billion; status pre-feasibility. Sombé West cannot supply it alongside Bélanou and SEKA's existing contract; it needs a new discovery or LNG imports, which links it to Chapter 76.

Scene outline: Mariama has the Azotes proposal and wants to know whether the Bélanou template (contract web, ECA and DFI lenders) carries over. Tomasz asks the wrong question first (what the gas costs per kilowatt-month, his verbal habit, which does not apply) and then the right ones: who buys the urea and at what price, and where the gas comes from. Mariama's verbal habit ("Who else has seen this?") is used once, about an approach from a trading house offering a processing agreement. The narration after the box applies Framework 77.1: a merchant export plant, a trader's marketing agreement, or a processing fee; it notes that urea is priced in US dollars, a natural hedge against the cauri that Bélanou lacks (Chapter 59 already taught convertibility). It then shows, with D-013 arithmetic from Case Bible inputs, why gas is the first problem: Sombé West's certified 2P reserves are 1,140 bcf (2017); the Bélanou GSA takes 72,400 MMBtu a day for 22 years with take-or-pay at 80% of ACQ, and SEKA's existing contract takes 38,000 MMBtu a day from 2019 to 2038, together 110,400 MMBtu a day; Azotes would add about 46,000 MMBtu a day, 41.7% more than the two existing contracts combined (46,000/110,400). The reserve coverage figure is printed only from the ledger (P-F61: 2P 1,140 bcf against 559 bcf for Bélanou and 267 bcf for SEKA, coverage 1.38x). Figures displayed: Case Bible inputs (2P reserves, DCQ, take-or-pay level, SEKA quantity, Azotes inputs) and P-F61. No other running-case numbers. State at end: an idea, not a project; the gas question points to the FSRU study of Chapter 76.

## 77.11 Practitioner's notebook (outline)

Checklist: identify the rung of every revenue stream on the margin protection ladder; obtain a joint price deck for inputs and outputs through a full cycle; obtain licensor guarantees and compare caps with lenders' exposure; map unit interdependencies before agreeing envelopes; confirm sponsor guarantee scope (debt-wide or cost-overrun only) and release tests; check single-route logistics for feedstock and products; for factories, obtain customer qualification status and contractual remedies for late or off-spec delivery; separate funded from committed capital.

Red flags: debt sized on an average spread; completion tied to mechanical completion only; release of guarantees on a short reliability run; expansion financing raised before phase one stabilizes; "orders" presented as contracted revenue; a marketer that is also a shareholder with discretion over pricing; DSCR thresholds that pass only by rounding.

Rules of thumb with limits: size spread businesses on a trough-of-cycle margin (limit: a long-term processing fee replaces the cycle); two ramp curves multiply (limit: independent if yield and throughput are driven by different constraints); feedstock linked to product price halves margin volatility only if the link is in the formula, not in a renegotiation clause.

Common mistakes: independent sensitivities on correlated prices; treating licensor LDs as a performance backstop; ignoring working capital build during ramp-up; modeling a factory's price flat in real terms when product prices deflate.

Questions experts ask: Which rung is each dollar of revenue on? Who will fund the plant if margins stay at trough for three years after guarantee release? What is the plant's position on its industry cost curve? Which units, if they fail, stop the others? How much of the funding is committed but conditional?

## 77.12 Judgment drill

The situation: you are the lead arranger for Wabash Cell Manufacturing LLC (fictional), a 30 GWh lithium-ion cell plant near Kokomo, Indiana (Illustrative), April 2026; senior debt requested USD 2,486.3 million; an automaker customer with a 7-year supply agreement for 70% of nameplate; the sponsor asks that its completion guarantee fall away at mechanical completion plus 90 days at 70% of nameplate throughput and 85% yield, arguing the supply agreement makes the plant "contracted"; credit committee in nine days.

Reasoning outline: the supply agreement is not take-or-pay unless volumes, prices and remedies for the customer's non-purchase are fixed and the customer's right to reject is limited to defined specification failures; Example 77.4 shows breakeven yield at 70% throughput of 94.2% in a similar cost structure (and still 89.2% at 85% throughput), so the proposed test can pass while the plant loses cash; the expert asks for a combined throughput-yield-cash test (for example two consecutive quarters at 85% throughput and 92% yield plus a historical DSCR test), a capacity reservation fee or prepayment from the customer, and a sweep until stabilization; trade-off offered: a stepped release of the guarantee in two tranches.

What would change the answer: an investment-grade customer accepting a take-or-pay with capacity fees covering debt service (moves toward release at mechanical completion plus a short run); a proven repeat design by the same sponsor at another site (shortens the test); public lender cover of the ramp-up risk (changes who bears the risk, not whether it exists); a falling cell price curve faster than the cost curve (tightens the test and lowers debt).

## 77.13 Exercise set

Tier 1 (4):
- 77.1: Explain why a spread business carries more risk for lenders than a capacity-payment plant of the same size.
- 77.2: Place five revenue arrangements on the margin protection ladder and justify each placement.
- 77.3: Explain the difference between an order book and a take-or-pay supply agreement, naming three contract terms that decide it, and say why the DOE battery joint ventures were nonetheless lent to as sponsor credits.
- 77.4: State why a debt-wide sponsor completion guarantee was central to Sadara and what risk it did not cover after November 2020.

Tier 2 (5; all inputs and expected answers below, computed in Python):
- 77.5: Scheldt Coast Refining NV (fictional), a 112,000 bbl/d refinery at Antwerp, Belgium, 2024. Inputs: crude USD 81.35/bbl; gasoline USD 2.31/gal; diesel USD 2.68/gal; 42 gal/bbl; capture rate 79%; cash opex USD 6.10/bbl; 365 days; utilization 88.0% and 94.5%. Compute the 3-2-1 crack, realized margin and EBITDA at each utilization. Answers: gasoline USD 97.02/bbl, diesel USD 112.56/bbl; crack USD 20.85/bbl; realized USD 16.47/bbl; throughput 35,974,400 and 38,631,600 bbl; EBITDA USD 373.1 million and USD 400.7 million.
- 77.6: Using Example 77.2's inputs, compute both cracker margins if all co-product credits fall 20%. Answers: ethane cracker USD 617.80/t (co-product credit 49.60); naphtha cracker minus USD 12.40/t (credits 1,209.60). Explain why the naphtha plant's margin falls about 24 times as far in USD per tonne (302.40 against 12.40).
- 77.7: Mohave Cell Works LLC (fictional), Nevada, 2025: nameplate 11.2 GWh started; price USD 104.5/kWh; materials USD 61.3/kWh started; conversion USD 7.2/kWh started; fixed cash costs USD 214.6 million a year. Compute breakeven yield at 60%, 75% and 90% throughput. Answers: 96.1%, 90.0%, 85.9%.
- 77.8: A Gulf polymer plant (Jubail Polymers Two Co., fictional, Saudi Arabia, 2025) has two revenue buckets: a processing-fee bucket with CFADS USD 92.4 million a year sized at 1.30x and a merchant-margin bucket with P50 CFADS USD 61.6 million sized at 2.00x; 12-year level annuity at 7.40%. Compute bucket debt service, total debt and the blended DSCR. Answers: USD 71.08 million plus USD 30.80 million = USD 101.88 million a year; annuity factor 7.7761; debt USD 792.2 million; blended DSCR on total CFADS 1.51x.
- 77.9: Draft a completion test clause for a two-envelope plant that closes the computation and rounding gap identified in Example 77.5 (state the ratio definition, the measurement period, the number of decimals and the rounding rule); annotate each paragraph.

Tier 3 (4):
- 77.10: Using the Northvolt capital-structure table (Exhibit 77.4), rank the claims by priority and explain why rescue liquidity came from the DSRA and a customer, then contrast with how the BlueOval SK unwind allocated debt (us-battery-doe facts only).
- 77.11: Model task (blank workbook, quarterly): Columbia Basin Cells LLC (fictional), Washington State, a 20.0 GWh a year plant. Inputs: quarterly started capacity 5.0 GWh; throughput starts at 20% in quarter 1 and rises 6 points a quarter to a cap of 92%; yield starts at 70% and rises 2 points a quarter to a cap of 94%; price USD 96.0/kWh; materials USD 57.0/kWh started; conversion USD 6.9/kWh started; fixed cash costs USD 98.6 million a quarter. Build the two curves, good output and EBITDA by quarter and find the first quarter of positive EBITDA. Answers: EBITDA quarter 1 (95.3), quarter 6 (66.4), quarter 11 (8.6), quarter 12 6.4 (first positive), quarter 13 onward 22.6 USD million a quarter at steady state; good output in quarter 12 3.956 GWh.
- 77.12: Credit memo: write a one-page recommendation on whether to fund a phase-two expansion before phase one passes completion, using the Northvolt record.
- 77.13: Case P extension: using only Case Bible inputs (1.4 GSA terms; Annex P 1.7.4 and 4.14) and P-F61, list what Azotes de Bélanou must secure (gas source, offtake, sponsor support) before it could be financed and place its possible revenue structures on the ladder. Key facts the answer must use: 110,400 MMBtu a day already contracted from Sombé West; Azotes needs about 46,000 more; coverage 1.38x on the existing contracts (P-F61).

## 77.15 Glossary entries owned

Per the glossary canon (rulings R-026, R-050, R-054): "reliability run" is homed at ssec:22.4.2 and is cross-referenced, not owned. Terms owned here:

| Term | Abbreviation | Definition | Home |
|---|---|---|---|
| spread business | – | A plant whose revenue depends on the difference between the price of what it sells and the price of what it buys, rather than on a contracted payment for capacity or service. | ssec:77.1.1 |
| crack spread | – | The difference between the value of refined products and the cost of the crude oil used to make them, per barrel of crude. | ssec:77.2.1 |
| 3-2-1 crack spread | – | A benchmark crack spread that assumes three barrels of crude yield two barrels of gasoline and one of diesel. | ssec:77.2.1 |
| gross refining margin | GRM | A refinery's product revenue less crude and other feedstock cost, per barrel processed, before operating costs. | ssec:77.2.1 |
| Nelson complexity index | – | A measure of a refinery's conversion capability that weights each secondary unit's capacity by its relative cost against crude distillation. | ssec:77.2.2 |
| capture rate | – | The share of a benchmark margin that a specific plant actually realizes, after its own yields, crude slate, product discounts and losses. | ssec:77.2.3 |
| turnaround | – | A scheduled multi-week shutdown of a refinery or chemical plant for inspection and major maintenance. | ssec:77.2.3 |
| feedstock advantage | – | The cost edge a petrochemical plant gains from a cheaper feedstock, measured as the margin difference per tonne of main product. | ssec:77.3.1 |
| co-product credit | – | The value of secondary products from a cracker or refinery, deducted from feedstock cost when computing the margin on the main product. | ssec:77.3.1 |
| yield (manufacturing) | – | The share of units started that pass final quality tests and can be sold. | ssec:77.4.1 |
| breakeven yield | – | The manufacturing yield at which revenue from good units equals variable and fixed cash costs at a given throughput. | ssec:77.4.1 |
| capacity reservation fee | – | A fixed payment from a customer to reserve a share of a plant's output, owed whether or not the customer takes product. | ssec:77.4.3 |
| margin protection ladder | – | Framework 77.1: a ranking of revenue arrangements for spread businesses by how firmly they protect the project company's margin. | ssec:77.5.1 |
| processing agreement | – | An industrial tolling arrangement in which a toller supplies feedstock, owns the product, and pays the project company a fee per unit processed. | ssec:77.5.2 |
| process performance guarantee | – | A licensor's guarantee that its technology will achieve stated capacity, yields and consumption when built and run to its design. | ssec:77.7.2 |
| envelope (completion testing) | – | A group of interdependent units in a complex that is performance-tested together for completion purposes. | ssec:77.7.5 |

## 77.16 Neighbors

Before: Chapter 76, LNG liquefaction, regasification, and FPSOs (unit u15). It shows how fee-based, tolling-style LNG agreements (Sabine Pass) made liquefaction financeable, how ECA-led mega-financings carried PNG LNG and Ichthys through cost growth, how security force majeure stopped Mozambique LNG, and how FPSO charters are financed. It ends with the gas molecule contracted under long-term SPAs, which sets up Chapter 77's contrast: plants whose molecules are not contracted.

After: Chapter 78, Mining, metals, and critical minerals. It teaches mining economics (cost curves, grade, recovery), the critical-minerals market and public finance for it, the mining contract set and host-government agreements, completion tests and reserve tails, price-deck sizing and streams as construction capital, through Oyu Tolgoi and Cobre Panamá, with a small Case P scene about a bauxite developer seeking power.

## 77.17 Coverage check

- Coverage map P: "refining and petrochemicals"; "manufacturing (gigafactory) finance" (Sections 77.2 to 77.11), each with landmark deals (Sadara and Duqm for refining and petrochemicals; Northvolt Ett and the DOE battery plants for manufacturing).
- Coverage map E: "Islamic project finance structures and how they sit alongside conventional tranches" applied in sector context (Sadara, 77.10; home Chapter 33).
- Coverage map N: "completion tests and the release of sponsor support" applied to multi-unit plants (77.7.5, 77.12; home Chapter 61).
- Coverage map G: "LNG and commodity-linked revenues" extended to refining and petrochemical margins (77.9; home Chapter 45).
- Section 7 candidate cases: "Sadara Chemical" and "Northvolt" (77.10, 77.11).
- Section 7 standard: real cases named, dated, lesson as mechanism.

- R-026: "strip ratio" has its home at ssec:12.3.2 and is used without bold here.
