Example 78.3 recomputed (the version-1.0 CFADS values did not reconcile with Example 78.1).

## 78.1 Header

- Chapter: 78
- Title: Mining, metals, and critical minerals
- File: `chapters/78-mining-and-critical-minerals.tex`
- Target length: 9,000 to 10,000 words
- Dominant lens: a mining project finance banker who has set bank price decks and negotiated completion tests, with the DFI and political-risk specialist's view of host-government agreements.

## 78.2 Purpose and capability statement

Purpose. A mine is a price taker sitting on a depleting asset in a country that can change the rules after the money is spent. This chapter teaches the reader the economics of mining (cost curves, grade, recovery, by-products, cycles), the special features of critical minerals (thin markets, processing bottlenecks, state-directed finance), and the lender's toolkit that answers them: a bank price deck, a reserve tail, a multi-part completion test, sponsor completion support, multi-source structures with DFI and ECA cover, and streams as construction capital. Oyu Tolgoi and Cobre Panamá show what happens when geology and the host government move after financial close.

What you will be able to do:
- Compute a mine's C1 cash cost and all-in sustaining cost per pound with by-product credits, and place it on an industry cost curve (Capabilities 4, 13).
- Size mining debt on a bank price deck, test it on a downside deck, and set the maximum tenor from a reserve tail (Capability 5).
- Draft and test a four-part mining completion test and explain what each part protects (Capabilities 3, 10).
- Judge the true cost and risk transfer of a stream against senior debt, and the protection a host agreement gives (Capabilities 8, 15).

## 78.3 Concepts owned and assumed

Owned:
1. Mining industry economics: the cost curve and quartiles; C1 cash cost; all-in sustaining cost (AISC); by-product credits; capital intensity per annual tonne; grade, strip ratio and recovery as value drivers; grade decline over mine life.
2. Commodity cycles for metals and why majors fund most mines on balance sheet; when limited-recourse finance is used (multi-owner projects, host-country risk sharing, capital beyond the sponsor's appetite).
3. Critical minerals as a finance category: concentration of supply and processing, thin and opaque price discovery, contract-indexed pricing, price floors and state-linked offtake, processing (refining) plants as manufacturing-like credits.
4. Public and policy finance for critical minerals: government direct loans with DSCR sizing tests, cash sweeps and DSRAs (the US DOE Thacker Pass loan), state equity and warrants alongside loans, price floors and guaranteed offtake (US DoD and MP Materials), import-linked lending sized to offtake (US EXIM's Supply Chain Resiliency Initiative, JBIC import and untied loans), home-country sponsor guarantees (JOGMEC), permitting status as finance (EU Critical Raw Materials Act Strategic Projects), DFI and ECA participation, political-risk rationale.
5. Mining host agreements: investment and stability agreements, development plans, fiscal terms (royalty forms, minimum payments, profit-based royalties), state carried interests and their funding.
6. Resource nationalism and the legal foundation of a mining right (approval process risk, as at Cobre Panamá).
7. Sector-specific completion test: Framework 78.1, the four-part completion test (mechanical, physical, metallurgical and operating, financial), with its sponsor guarantee release.
8. The mining reserve-tail requirement and maximum tenor from reserve life (term home of "reserve tail": ssec:45.6.3, R-104), measured both in tonnes and in NPV.
9. Price-deck sizing for mining: base and downside decks (the bank price deck itself is owned by ssec:75.3.2, R-103), correlated by-product prices, TC/RC as a market variable, sizing at higher DSCRs than contracted assets.
10. Streams as construction capital: deposit, ongoing payment, effective cost, and the streamer's exposure to price and concession risk. (The stream and royalty instruments are owned by Chapter 21; this chapter owns their use and cost as project funding.)
11. Debt caps with supplemental senior debt tranches; staged development (open pit then underground).
12. Mining-specific risks: geotechnical and geological (block caving), metallurgical, tailings, water, closure liabilities and care and maintenance cost, captive infrastructure and power.
13. Modeling specifics: mine plan to payable metal chain, price deck by year, FX on local costs, sustaining capital, closure provision and bonding, royalty variants.

Assumed:
- Mines and processing at financier depth, strip ratio and reserves classifications: Chapter 12 (ssec:12.1.1, ssec:12.3.1, ssec:12.3.2; R-025, R-026). Reporting codes (JORC, NI 43-101, S-K 1300) as applied in diligence: Chapter 48, with t-reserves-codes.
- Mining offtake (payables, treatment and refining charges, penalties) and streams and royalties as contracts: Chapter 21.
- Reserves and resources, competent person's reports and the IE's review: Chapter 48.
- E&S standards, community consent, resettlement: Chapter 50.
- Political risk insurance, MIGA, the multilateral halo, the obsolescing bargain, treaty claims: Chapter 60. Investment treaties and arbitration: Chapter 54.
- Country, currency and payment risk: Chapter 59.
- A/B loans, ECA cover, preferred creditor status: Chapter 29. Blended finance: Chapter 34.
- Sponsor completion guarantees as instruments and the definition of financial completion: Chapter 26 (ssec:26.5.2). Completion tests in operation and release of support: Chapter 61 (sec:61.11, sec:61.12).
- Sizing and sculpting: Chapter 36. Reserves and sweeps: Chapter 37. Mining reserves, grades and recoveries in the model, and the term "reserve tail": Chapter 45 (ssec:45.6.3).
- Bank price deck: Chapter 75 (ssec:75.3.2), per R-103.
- Decommissioning reserves and end-of-life: Chapter 65.
- Industrial (processing) plant economics: Chapter 77.

Forward references: none required; a one-sentence pointer to Chapter 88 for evaluating new critical-minerals structures in 78.2.4.

## 78.4 Locked table of contents

Opening (no heading). November 28, 2023: Panama's Supreme Court announces it has declared Law 406, the revised Cobre Panamá concession contract, unconstitutional in its entirety; the mine, which produced 350,438 t of copper in 2022 (38.8% of First Quantum's revenue), moves to preservation and safe management; Franco-Nevada, which had paid a USD 1 billion deposit for a precious-metals stream funded 1:3 alongside the sponsor, writes its stream down by USD 1,169.2 million. The puzzle: a technically sound, environmentally broadly compliant mine lost its right to operate. Source: cobre-panama.

What you will be able to do (unnumbered).

78.1 How mines make and lose money (sec:78.1)
- 78.1.1 Price takers on a cost curve (ssec:78.1.1). Cost-curve exhibit (Exhibit 78.1, Illustrative pgfplots step chart of an industry cost curve with the example mine marked); C1 and AISC defined. Example 78.1.
- 78.1.2 Grade, strip ratio, and recovery (ssec:78.1.2). Why grade is destiny; grade decline; recovery and concentrate grade; how each enters revenue (link to Chapter 45 for the model chain). Strip ratio is used without bold (home ssec:12.3.2).
- 78.1.3 Cycles, capital intensity, and who borrows non-recourse (ssec:78.1.3). Majors' balance sheets; when limited-recourse is chosen: QB2 (Teck's USD 2.5 billion limited-recourse facility, fully drawn by December 31, 2022, with distribution restrictions until completion), Oyu Tolgoi (host-country risk sharing), Reko Diq (Barrick's FY2025 annual information form names non-recourse project finance as a way to mitigate geopolitical risk; do not state that it has closed). Source: t-market-norms. One sentence on the non-project alternative: Sigma Lithium's BRL 487 million, 16-year BNDES loan at 7.45% (August 27, 2024) is secured by a bank letter of credit, not project assets (t-market-norms-2).

78.2 Critical minerals as a finance category (sec:78.2)
- 78.2.1 What makes a mineral critical for a lender (ssec:78.2.1). Supply and processing concentration, thin markets, price spikes and collapses; principles only, no unverified market shares. The EU's 2030 benchmarks as one government's definition of the problem: EU extraction at least 10%, processing at least 40% and recycling at least 25% of annual EU consumption, and no more than 65% of any strategic raw material from a single third country (Regulation (EU) 2024/1252, in force May 23, 2024; t-critical-minerals-policy; do not quote article numbers).
- 78.2.2 Pricing without a deep terminal market (ssec:78.2.2). Contract prices indexed to price-reporting agencies; floor-and-ceiling offtakes; qualification of product by battery makers; why lenders haircut index-linked prices. Real price floor: the US Department of Defense and MP Materials, July 10, 2025: a 10-year NdPr price floor of USD 110/kg, a 10-year commitment that 100% of a new magnet facility's output is bought, USD 400 million of convertible preferred stock plus a warrant (about 15% as converted), a USD 150 million loan for heavy rare earth separation, and a USD 1.0 billion bank commitment letter for the magnet plant (t-critical-minerals-policy). Lesson: a floor from a sovereign moves price risk to the state and makes the state a co-investor; outcomes are not yet known.
- 78.2.3 Processing plants as manufacturing credits (ssec:78.2.3). Hydroxide refineries and separation plants behave like Chapter 77 factories: ramp-up, yield, specification. Cross-reference Framework 77.1.
- 78.2.4 Public money in critical minerals (ssec:78.2.4). US DOE loan to Lithium Nevada (Thacker Pass), announced October 2024 at USD 2.26 billion (USD 1.97 billion principal plus USD 289.7 million capitalized interest) via the Federal Financing Bank; minimum projected DSCR sizing test, cash sweep tied to historical DSCR, DSRA; thresholds redacted (t-market-norms). The September 30, 2025 non-binding agreement on the first USD 435 million draw: USD 182 million of debt service deferred over the first five years, penny warrants for 5% of Lithium Americas and a 5% economic interest in the Thacker Pass joint venture, USD 120 million more into reserve accounts, and an amended GM offtake allowing third-party sales (do not state that these terms were finalized as announced). DOE's USD 996 million loan guarantee to Ioneer's Rhyolite Ridge (January 2025). Import-linked and home-country tools: US EXIM's Supply Chain Resiliency Initiative sizes debt to the share of offtake contracted to US importers and excludes PRC-linked projects (do not state that EXIM has closed any SCRI deal); DFC equity and project development funding (do not state Serra Verde or Aclara amounts); EU Strategic Projects (47 selected on March 25, 2025, EUR 22.5 billion of expected investment, permitting deadlines of 27 months for extraction and 15 months for processing and recycling; RESourceEU of December 3, 2025 with up to EUR 3 billion over 12 months); Japan's JOGMEC (equity, loans, debt guarantees for Japanese companies' projects, the 2012 Olaroz guarantee as its example, and the rare metals stockpile). Teaches what is visible and invisible in public filings and that state equity changes who negotiates a restructuring. One-sentence forward pointer to Chapter 88.

78.3 What a lender needs to know about the mine itself (sec:78.3)
- 78.3.1 From deposit to payable metal (ssec:78.3.1). Exhibit 78.2 (TikZ flow: deposit, mine plan, mill, concentrator, concentrate, smelter, payable metal), cross-reference Chapter 12 for the physical description and reserves classifications (R-025); here only what drives cash flow. One sentence: the reserve statement must be read under the reporting code in force on the deal date (JORC, NI 43-101 or S-K 1300; NI 43-101 under proposed replacement since June 2025, JORC under revision; t-reserves-codes), with diligence cited to ssec:48.3.3.
- 78.3.2 Infrastructure, power, and water (ssec:78.3.2). Captive infrastructure and access contracts; Oyu Tolgoi's obligation under the 2009 Investment Agreement to deliver a domestic power source after the 2018 cancellation of a cross-border power arrangement.
- 78.3.3 Tailings, closure, and care and maintenance (ssec:78.3.3). Closure as a liability that outlives the debt; Cobre Panamá's commission estimate of USD 2.6 to 3.4 billion over 17 to 19 years for full closure from the current state (medium confidence; attribute to the government summary). How regulators secure closure, in one paragraph from t-decommissioning-liabilities: US hardrock guarantees on federal land must cover the cost of a third party reclaiming the site and may require trust funds for long-term water treatment; US surface coal bonds must let the regulator complete reclamation itself, with phased release. Cross-reference Chapter 65 for decommissioning reserves and end-of-life practice.

78.4 Revenue models and funding from the offtake side (sec:78.4)
- 78.4.1 Concentrate, cathode, and metal sales (ssec:78.4.1). Cross-reference Chapter 21 for payables and TC/RCs; this subsection shows where they enter Example 78.1 and that TC/RC is a market variable, not a constant: the annual copper concentrate benchmark agreed with Chinese smelters moved from USD 59.5/t and 5.95 cents/lb (2021) to USD 65/6.5 (2022), USD 88/8.8 (2023), USD 80/8.0 (2024), USD 21.25/2.125 (2025) and USD 0/0 (2026), with spot charges "trading at negative triple digits" in early 2026, because smelter capacity, mostly in China, outran mine supply after losses such as Cobre Panamá's closure (t-mining-offtake). For miners this was a gain (Antofagasta's TC/RC deductions fell from USD 185.3 million in 2024 to USD 20.3 million in 2025); for custom smelters a margin crisis (about 75% utilization, Teck), which is an offtaker credit risk for a project lender. Payables and penalty elements are illustrated from the Escondida technical report (96.2% payable copper in concentrate, 90% gold and silver; arsenic among penalty elements); do not state penalty thresholds or which company led each year's benchmark.
- 78.4.2 Offtake-linked finance and prepayments (ssec:78.4.2). Offtakers (traders, smelters, battery makers) prepaying for future output; security and priority against senior lenders. Three real models from t-untied-resource-finance: (1) Japan's JBIC import loans (capped at the import contract value; Codelco, March 28, 2025, up to USD 466 million JBIC portion, USD 666 million in total) and untied loans (Rio Tinto's Rincon lithium project, March 10, 2026, USD 1,175 million with JBIC's USD 240 million alongside IFC, Export Finance Australia and IDB Invest); (2) a loan paired with a parallel export contract that the borrower said was not a securitization (China Development Bank's USD 10 billion, 10-year loan to Petrobras in 2009 alongside a 150,000 to 200,000 barrel a day Unipec export agreement); (3) proceeds routed to a lender-controlled account with set-off across all the debtor's debts (CDB's 2010 USD 1 billion oil-backed loan to Ecuador, as described by Gelpern and co-authors, 2021). Lesson: who controls the collection account matters more than the label on the loan. Do not state KEXIM or K-sure product terms or JBIC loan rates and tenors.
- 78.4.3 Streams and royalties as construction capital (ssec:78.4.3). Example 78.4; Cobre Panamá stream facts (USD 1 billion deposit funded 1:3, further USD 356 million in 2018 for the KPMC share, full impairment in 2023). Instrument home: ssec:21.5.2.

78.5 Geological, metallurgical, price, fiscal, legal, and social risk in mines (sec:78.5)
- 78.5.1 Geology and geotechnics (ssec:78.5.1). Oyu Tolgoi underground: approved May 6, 2016 at USD 5.3 billion with first production expected in 2020; July 2019 disclosure of difficult ground conditions; definitive estimate December 16, 2020 at USD 6.75 billion and sustainable production by October 2022; later USD 6.925 billion; underground production celebrated March 13, 2023.
- 78.5.2 Metallurgy and ramp-up (ssec:78.5.2). Recovery shortfalls; concentrate quality penalties (arsenic and other deleterious elements, t-mining-offtake); specification for battery-grade products.
- 78.5.3 Price, by-products, and currency (ssec:78.5.3). Correlation of by-product credits; TC/RC swings (the 2023 USD 88/8.8 against the 2026 USD 0/0 benchmark as the sensitivity range; Example 78.1); local-currency costs with USD revenue as a partial hedge (cross-reference Chapter 59); mandatory commodity hedging in a recent financing (Skouries: gold and copper swaps, gold collars, interest-rate swaps and FX forwards; t-market-norms-2).
- 78.5.4 Fiscal terms and resource nationalism (ssec:78.5.4). Cobre Panamá: 2% royalty on negotiable gross production under Law 9 against a March 2023 draft with a USD 375 million minimum annual payment and a 12% to 16% profit-based royalty; Oyu Tolgoi's 2018 tax assessment of approximately USD 155 million taken to UNCITRAL arbitration in 2020 (outcome not stated).
- 78.5.5 The legal foundation of the right to mine (ssec:78.5.5). Cobre Panamá: Law 9 declared unconstitutional in a ruling made public in September 2018 (never "annulled the concession"); legislative approval in three days; protests; the 2023 ruling. Lesson: diligence on how the right was granted, not only on what it says (cross-reference Chapter 49 for legal diligence).
- 78.5.6 The social license to operate (ssec:78.5.6). The SGS audit (361 of 370 commitments fulfilled, 87.7% overall) against national rejection; cross-reference Chapter 50 for the standards.

78.6 Host agreements, carried interests, and the mine contract map (sec:78.6)
- 78.6.1 Host agreements and stability (ssec:78.6.1). Investment agreement, development and financing plan, shareholders' agreement with the state (Oyu Tolgoi 2009, 2011, 2015 documents); stability clauses and their limits (cross-reference Chapter 54). Clause 78.1 (fiscal stability clause in a mining investment agreement) with three variants: sponsor-friendly (freeze), lender-friendly (freeze plus economic equilibrium with lender consent rights), government-friendly (equilibrium only, capped, with periodic review); followed by a landing paragraph dated to "mining investment agreements negotiated 2010 to 2025" stated as practitioner observation (D-011 style) without real-deal attribution.
- 78.6.2 State carried interests and who funds them (ssec:78.6.2). Oyu Tolgoi: Turquoise Hill funded the government's 34% through a carry account loan that reached USD 2.4 billion with interest and was waived in January 2022. Glossary home of "carried interest (state)" (R-083).
- 78.6.3 EPCM, owner's team, and construction packages (ssec:78.6.3). Cross-reference Chapter 23; sector point: mines are rarely built under a single lump-sum EPC, so completion risk sits with the sponsor.
- 78.6.4 Offtake, stream, infrastructure access, and closure bonding (ssec:78.6.4). Exhibit 78.3 (TikZ contract map of a limited-recourse copper-gold mine, Illustrative).

78.7 Structuring and sizing mining debt (sec:78.7)
- 78.7.1 Applying the bank price deck to a mine (ssec:78.7.1). The deck is defined and owned in ssec:75.3.2 (R-103); here, how a mining deck is haircut against consensus and run with correlated by-products; Example 78.3.
- 78.7.2 Reserve tail and tenor (ssec:78.7.2). Example 78.2; Equation 78.1 (maximum tenor from reserve life and required tail). The term "reserve tail" is cited to ssec:45.6.3 (R-104); this subsection sets the mining requirement.
- 78.7.3 The four-part completion test (ssec:78.7.3). Framework 78.1, then Framework 78.2 (the deck-tail-test triangle) tying deck, tail and test together across Examples 78.2, 78.3 and 78.5. Real instance: QB2's several sponsor guarantees ran until final completion, achieved in March 2025 against a June 2025 completion longstop, and were then released (t-market-norms-2).
- 78.7.4 Multi-source structures and debt caps (ssec:78.7.4). Oyu Tolgoi December 15, 2015: USD 4.4 billion from IFC and EBRD (A and B loans), EDC, US EXIM, Efic and 15 commercial banks with a MIGA guarantee of USD 1 billion (expropriation, transfer and inconvertibility, war and civil disturbance, breach of contract, up to 12 years); USD 6 billion debt cap with a USD 1.6 billion supplemental option; reported tranche tenors of 12 to 15 years and MIGA-covered loans at LIBOR plus 2.65% before completion and 3.65% after (medium confidence, single trade-press source; label it). Exhibit 78.4 (reported tranche structure table, labeled with the confidence note). Do not reconcile the USD 1 billion MIGA guarantee with the USD 700 million MIGA-covered tranche.
- 78.7.5 Tenor, gearing, and cover in limited-recourse mining debt (ssec:78.7.5). Verified single-deal evidence first (t-market-norms-2; each is one deal, not a market average): Skouries (Greece, Eldorado Gold, signed April 5, 2023): EUR 480.4 million commercial loan at six-month EURIBOR plus an undisclosed fixed margin, 70% hedged, EUR 200 million from the Greek Recovery and Resilience Fund at 3.04% and 4.06% fixed, a EUR 60 million cost-overrun facility, an equity commitment backstopped by a letter of credit, repayment in 13 semiannual installments from December 31, 2026 to December 31, 2032; QB2: USD 2.5 billion repaid in 17 equal semiannual installments of USD 147 million from June 15, 2023 (final installment June 2031, computed); Aya Gold and Silver (Morocco): EBRD project finance at SOFR plus 5%; Cariboo Gold (Canada, Osisko Development): a USD 450 million private-credit loan of July 21, 2025 at 3-month SOFR plus 0.10% plus 9.50% initially, stepping to 7.50%, with warrants. Lessons: mining debt amortizes over about six to eight years after completion because there is no long offtake; lender type sets price. Then the D-011 indicative ranges, printed as practitioner consensus with reviewer sign-off: limited-recourse base-metal mines with DFI or ECA participation, 2010 to 2025, senior debt of roughly 40% to 60% of development capital, reserve tails of at least 25% to 35% of reserves at final maturity, sizing DSCRs well above contracted infrastructure (t-market-norms lists mining DSCRs and gearing under "Do not state" as verified norms; t-market-norms-2 bars mining margins, gearing and DSCRs as generic norms). Drivers ranked: host-country risk, deposit quality and position on the cost curve, sponsor strength and completion support, commodity, lender type.

78.8 Modeling a mine (sec:78.8)
- 78.8.1 The mine-plan chain (ssec:78.8.1). Tonnes, grade, recovery, payable metal; cross-reference Chapter 45 (which owns the cell-level build); here the lender's checks: plan against reserve statement, recovery against test work, and grade profile against the reserve tail.
- 78.8.2 Price decks, by-products, TC/RCs, and FX in the model (ssec:78.8.2). One scenario selector drives copper, gold, TC/RC and FX together; no independent sensitivities on correlated prices.
- 78.8.3 Closure, sustaining capex, and royalty variants (ssec:78.8.3). Closure provision accrual and bonding; NSR, gross and profit-based royalties and their effect on downside cover (link to Example 78.1).

78.9 Oyu Tolgoi and multi-source mining finance under a demanding host (sec:78.9)
Real case section from oyu-tolgoi. Sequence 2009 to June 30, 2026. Lesson as mechanism: a multi-source structure with DFI and MIGA cover financed a large mine in a frontier country, but geotechnical overruns and host pressure were resolved by sponsor funding and renegotiation (the carry-loan waiver, the 2021 and 2022 funding plans), not by lender enforcement or insurance claims, because creeping pressure rarely triggers a political-risk policy. Do not state that the 2021 to 2022 reprofiling or supplemental debt closed.

78.10 Cobre Panamá and the mine that lost its legal foundation (sec:78.10)
Real case section from cobre-panama. Sequence 1997 to September 30, 2026. Lesson: the stream holder carried full concession risk; fiscal renegotiation, legislative haste and judicial review can end a right to operate regardless of operating compliance; arbitration claims became a bargaining chip that had to be suspended. Do not state that the mine was financed with non-recourse bank project finance; do not merge the USD 20 billion and USD 27 billion claim figures.

78.11 Walkthrough: marking up a mining completion test (sec:78.11)
Task: lenders' counsel and the IE mark up a sponsor's draft completion schedule, then test a result. Uses Clause 78.2 (completion test schedule, Illustrative) and Example 78.5.

78.12 Case P: Hautes-Moraba Bauxite asks Bélanou for power (sec:78.12)
Small installment.

78.13 Practitioner's notebook (sec:78.13). 78.14 Judgment drill (sec:78.14).

78.15 A mine is a wasting asset with a political half-life (sec:78.15)
Close. Crystallizes deck, tail, completion, and the host agreement as the true senior security. Final paragraph poses the next problem: some infrastructure has no commodity price at all, only drivers who may or may not choose to pay; how does a lender finance a road whose revenue depends on people it has never met (Chapter 79).

78.16 Exercises (sec:78.16). 78.17 Solutions to exercises (sec:78.17). Sources.

## 78.5 Opening

The Cobre Panamá ruling of November 28, 2023, told from the stream holder's seat: a USD 1 billion deposit funded alongside the sponsor during construction, a mine at record output a year earlier, and a full impairment by year end. The writer contrasts this with the operating record (SGS audit 2026) and ends on the question: what exactly does a lender to a mine own when the right to mine can disappear?

## 78.6 Worked examples

Example 78.1. C1 and AISC for a copper-gold mine (Illustrative). Setting: Cerro Albarda Copper SpA (fictional), an open-pit copper-gold mine under development in the Atacama region of Chile; its lenders (a fictional club with a DFI and an ECA) review the banking case's first full operating year (2028, in 2024 prices) in December 2024. Ore milled 30.5 Mt a year at 0.58% Cu; recovery 88.4%: copper in concentrate 156,379.6 t; payable 96.5%: 150,906.3 t payable copper = 332.69 million lb. Site costs: mining USD 3.95/t ore, processing USD 8.40/t ore, G&A USD 61.8 million: site total USD 438.5 million. Concentrate at 26% Cu: 601,460 dmt; TC USD 80/dmt and RC USD 0.08/lb payable, the 2024 Chinese benchmark (t-mining-offtake): TC USD 48.1 million, RC USD 26.6 million; freight 1.08 wmt per dmt at USD 61/wmt: USD 39.6 million; TC/RC and freight USD 114.4 million. Gold by-product: 96,300 oz at 92% payable and USD 2,350/oz: credit USD 208.2 million. C1 = (438.5 + 114.4 - 208.2)/332.69 = USD 1.04/lb. Sustaining capex USD 212.4 million; royalty 5% of gross revenue at copper USD 4.15/lb (5% x (332.69 x 4.15 + 208.2) = USD 79.4 million). AISC = (438.5 + 114.4 - 208.2 + 212.4 + 79.4)/332.69 = USD 1.91/lb; margin at USD 4.15/lb = USD 2.24/lb. TC/RC sensitivity: at the 2026 benchmark of USD 0/0 the TC and RC disappear and C1 falls by USD 0.22/lb to USD 0.81/lb; at the 2023 benchmark (USD 88/8.8) C1 rises by USD 0.02/lb. Writer places the mine on Exhibit 78.1 (second quartile in the illustrative curve). Verified in Python (revision round 1).

Example 78.2. Reserve tail and maximum tenor (Illustrative). Setting: the same Cerro Albarda mine, banking case of December 2024. Proven and probable reserves 403.7 Mt (replaces the version-1.0 figure); throughput 30.5 Mtpa: reserve life 13.24 years. Lenders require at least 30% of reserves to remain at final maturity: maximum tenor from start of operations = 0.70 x 13.24 = 9.27 years. At a 9-year tenor, 32.0% of reserves remain ((403.7 - 9 x 30.5)/403.7). Equation 78.1: maximum tenor = (1 - tail) x reserves / annual throughput. Extension: grade decline (front-loaded high-grade) means a tonnage tail overstates the cash-flow tail; the writer shows the fix (tail measured on NPV of remaining cash flows on the bank deck), as a principle here and with numbers in Exercise 78.6.

Example 78.3. Sizing on a bank price deck and testing a downside (Illustrative). Setting: Cerro Albarda's lenders size the senior debt in December 2024. The Example 78.1 mine, using its volumes and costs (TC/RC at the 2024 benchmark). Method (stated so the auditor can check it): revenue = payable copper x copper price + payable gold x gold price; royalty 5% of that revenue; EBITDA = revenue - royalty - site costs (438.5) - TC/RC and freight (114.4); tax 25% of EBITDA above USD 310 million of tax depreciation; CFADS = EBITDA - tax - sustaining capex (212.4). Consensus deck (copper USD 4.30/lb, gold USD 2,350/oz): EBITDA USD 1,004.0 million, tax 173.5, CFADS USD 618.1 million. Bank deck (USD 3.85/lb, USD 1,950/oz): EBITDA 828.1, tax 129.5, CFADS USD 486.2 million. Downside deck (USD 3.20/lb, USD 1,650/oz): EBITDA 597.4, tax 71.9, CFADS USD 313.2 million. Size at 1.50x on the bank deck: debt service USD 324.1 million a year; 8 years at 8.12%: annuity factor 5.7206; debt USD 1,854.2 million (38.1% of development capital of USD 4,862.0 million). DSCR on the consensus deck 1.91x; on the downside deck 0.97x. The bank-deck DSCR that keeps the downside at 1.00x is 1.55x (486.2/313.2), which cuts debt to USD 1,791.5 million (36.8%). Teaching point: the sizing DSCR on a bank deck is chosen so that a plausible downside still meets debt service, and 1.50x is not quite enough here; level debt service is used for simplicity (sculpting is Chapter 36). Recomputed in revision round 1 (the version-1.0 CFADS figures did not reconcile with Example 78.1).

Example 78.4. The cost of a gold stream as construction capital (Illustrative). Setting: Cerro Albarda's sponsor sells a gold stream to Andes Royalty and Stream Corp. (fictional) in 2024 to fund construction. Stream deposit USD 318.6 million paid 30%, 40%, 30% over three construction years; streamer receives 25% of payable gold (96,300 oz x 92% x 25% = 22,149 oz a year) for 13 years and pays an ongoing 20% of spot per ounce. Net value to the streamer each year at USD 2,350/oz: USD 41.64 million; at USD 1,950/oz: USD 34.55 million; at USD 1,650/oz: USD 29.24 million. Streamer's IRR (timing: deposit years 0 to 2, deliveries years 3 to 15): 7.29%, 4.56%, 2.27%. Compare with senior debt at 8.12% in Example 78.3: the stream is cheaper than debt in the base case and the streamer, not the lenders, takes gold price risk; the writer explains what the miner gives up (gold upside) and how the stream ranks against senior lenders (intercreditor terms, cross-reference Chapter 53). Verified in Python.

Example 78.5. Testing a completion result (Illustrative). Setting: Cerro Albarda's completion test, run from April to July 2027 and certified by the lenders' IE (a fictional firm) in August 2027. Clause 78.2 tests: mechanical completion certified; physical completion: mill throughput at least 90% of nameplate (83,560 t/d) averaged over 90 consecutive days; metallurgical: copper recovery at least 86.0%; operating: C1 at most USD 1.25/lb over the test period; financial: projected DSCR on the bank deck at least 1.40x for each remaining period, with reserve tail still met. Results: throughput 77,410 t/d (92.6%: pass); recovery 86.9% (pass); C1 USD 1.18/lb (pass); projected minimum DSCR 1.37x (fail). Outcome: completion not achieved; options: prepayment to restore 1.40x (computed with numbers in Exercise 78.7), sponsor cure, retest after a deck update; the writer explains why lenders insist on the financial test in mining (the plant can work and the mine still not support the debt).

## 78.7 Walkthrough

Walkthrough: marking up a mining completion test (Section 78.11).
1. Read the sponsor's draft schedule: list each test and its measurement period.
2. Check that physical tests use sustained periods (90 days rather than 30) and average rather than peak rates.
3. Tie the metallurgical test to the recovery used in the banking case.
4. Add an operating cost test and define which costs count (C1 versus AISC).
5. Add the financial test on the bank deck in force at the test date, with the reserve tail re-tested on the updated mine plan.
6. Specify the IE's certificate, the long-stop date and the consequences of failure (guarantee call, cash trap, mandatory prepayment).
7. Apply the marked-up schedule to the Example 78.5 results and write the facility agent's notice.

## 78.8 Frameworks

Framework 78.1, the four-part completion test (label fw:four-part-completion). Established practice in mining finance, organized here as an original checklist. Parts: (1) mechanical completion: built to specification, certified; (2) physical completion: sustained throughput at a stated share of nameplate over a stated period; (3) metallurgical and operating completion: recovery, product quality and unit cost at or better than the banking case; (4) financial completion: projected cover ratios on the current bank deck and reserve tail still met, reserves funded, no default. Use: drafting and testing the release of sponsor completion support; each part is matched to the risk it retires (construction, ramp-up, process, economics). Demonstrated in Example 78.5 and the walkthrough.

Framework 78.2, the deck-tail-test triangle (original; label fw:deck-tail-test). Purpose: check that the three main mining debt parameters are consistent. Steps: (1) size on the bank deck; (2) set tenor inside the reserve tail; (3) make the financial completion test use the same deck and tail; (4) stress all three together on the downside deck. When the three are set by different teams on different decks, the structure leaks. Demonstrated across Examples 78.2, 78.3 and 78.5.

## 78.9 Real cases and fact sheets

- oyu-tolgoi: multi-source mining finance with DFI, ECA and MIGA; geotechnical overrun; obsolescing bargain resolved by renegotiation; carried interest funded by the sponsor. Sec:78.9, 78.3.2, 78.5.1, 78.5.4, 78.6.1, 78.6.2, 78.7.4.
- cobre-panama: stream as construction capital and its total loss; fiscal-regime renegotiation; legal-foundation risk; social license against technical compliance; closure liability and care-and-maintenance cost. Opening, sec:78.10, 78.3.3, 78.4.3, 78.5.4 to 78.5.6.
- t-market-norms: Teck QB2 (limited-recourse USD 2.5 billion, distribution restrictions to completion); DOE Thacker Pass (structure and redaction); Barrick Reko Diq (stated rationale only). 78.1.3, 78.2.4.
- t-critical-minerals-policy (placed in revision round 1): ssec:78.2.1 (EU benchmarks), ssec:78.2.2 (DoD and MP Materials price floor), ssec:78.2.4 (Thacker Pass first-draw terms with warrants, Rhyolite Ridge, EXIM SCRI, DFC, EU Strategic Projects, RESourceEU, JOGMEC). Lesson in one line: state finance in critical minerals now arrives as a package of floors, equity and offtake that replaces merchant risk and makes the state a co-investor. Respect "Do not state" (no SCRI closings, no DFC amounts for Serra Verde or Aclara, no claim that the Thacker Pass first-draw terms were final as announced, no CRMA article numbers).
- t-untied-resource-finance (placed in revision round 1): ssec:78.4.2 (three models: JBIC import and untied loans, the Petrobras and CDB loan-plus-export pair, CDB's lender-controlled accounts). Lesson in one line: control of the collection account, not the loan's label, decides how resource-linked debt ranks in a restructuring. Do not state KEXIM terms, JBIC rates or tenors, or totals of Chinese oil-for-loans.
- t-mining-offtake (placed in revision round 1): ssec:78.4.1 (benchmark TC/RC series 2021 to 2026, payables, penalties, smelter distress), ssec:78.5.2, ssec:78.5.3, ssec:78.8.2 and Example 78.1. Lesson in one line: TC/RC is a market price whose sign can flip, so it belongs in the price deck, and a distressed custom smelter is an offtaker credit risk.
- t-market-norms-2 (mining items; placed in revision round 1): ssec:78.1.3 (Sigma's BNDES loan), ssec:78.5.3 (Skouries hedging), ssec:78.7.3 (QB2 completion and guarantee release) and ssec:78.7.5 (Skouries, QB2, Aya, Cariboo terms). Respect its "Do not state" list (no Skouries, QB2 or Colombian margins; no generic mining norms).
- t-reserves-codes: one sentence in ssec:78.3.1 (code in force on the deal date).
- t-decommissioning-liabilities: one paragraph in ssec:78.3.3 (US reclamation bonding rules).
- t-us-tax-credits: §45X phase-out for applicable critical minerals from 2031 to 2033 (one sentence, dated, in ssec:78.2.4, cited to sec:67.10).

Open requests (the chapter can be taught without them):
- fact sheet requested: ambatovy (optional): Ambatovy nickel project (Madagascar) financing and restructuring, as a second mining failure with a processing plant.
- fact sheet requested: tailings (optional): the Global Industry Standard on Tailings Management (2020) and lender requirements; until it exists ssec:78.3.3 names no tailings standard.

## 78.10 Running-case installment

Case P (small), story date 2026, Kilnworth's Dabakro office (writer chooses the month in 2026 and reports it). Character: Tomasz Wierzbicki (Head of Portfolio Management from 2024). Inputs (Annex P 4.14, book inputs): Hautes-Moraba Bauxite SA, a junior mining company listed abroad (nationality not stated), developing a 12 Mtpa bauxite mine in the upper Moraba basin with a planned 1.2 Mtpa alumina refinery in phase 2; it asks for 150 MW of baseload power for 25 years from a Bélanou expansion at a USD-indexed tariff; it is unrated, sells to a single trader and offers a parent guarantee from an entity with no operating revenue.

Scene outline: Hautes-Moraba's request reaches Tomasz. He converts it into tariff terms (his verbal habit), then hits the regulatory fact: under the Electricity Code (Law No. 2009-112) Kessara runs a single-buyer model, so a direct sale to the mine needs SEKA or the regulator, OREK (Case Bible 1.1). Sylvestre Ahouansou (Minister of Energy and Hydrocarbons from October 16, 2023) may appear in one line as the minister who wants the mine (optional; Annex P name register lists Chapter 78). The narration after the box teaches: a mine as a power offtaker is a commodity-price credit with a reserve life that may be shorter than a 25-year power contract; a take-or-pay from an unrated junior with a single trader offtake and an empty parent guarantee is only as good as the mine's position on its cost curve (Example 78.1); the lenders to Bélanou would test the new offtaker under Framework 78.2 logic and would ask where the extra gas for an expansion comes from (Chapter 77's Azotes scene and Chapter 76's FSRU study). Figures displayed: Case Bible inputs only (the Hautes-Moraba request terms); no ledger figures. State at end: an inquiry.

## 78.11 Practitioner's notebook (outline)

Checklist: reserve statement and competent person's report reconciled to the mine plan; bank deck set, with by-products and FX in the same scenario; reserve tail tested in tonnes and in NPV; four-part completion test; host agreement terms on fiscal stability, carried interest funding and dispute resolution; closure plan, cost estimate and bonding; tailings design standard; power, water and logistics contracts; stream and offtake intercreditor positions.

Red flags: sizing on consensus or spot; physical completion over 30 days at peak rates; a single concentrate offtaker with set-off rights; a state partner whose equity is funded by the sponsor with no repayment path; a mining right granted without the process the law required; closure costs unfunded until late in life.

Rules of thumb with limits: the lender's deck sits below consensus by a margin that widens with volatility (limit: price floors from creditworthy parties); the tail is at least a quarter to a third of reserves (indicative, D-011; limit: long-life deposits with stable grade); completion takes longer than the schedule for underground mines.

Common mistakes: treating by-product credits as independent of the main metal; modeling local costs in USD without FX; ignoring care-and-maintenance cost in a downside; treating political risk insurance as cover for creeping fiscal change.

Questions experts ask: Where is the mine on the cost curve at the bank deck? What happens to cover in year 3 if grade comes in 10% below plan? Who funds the state's share? How was the mining right granted, and has its approval been challenged? What does closure cost now, and who holds the bond?

## 78.12 Judgment drill

The situation: you are the DFI investment officer on Salar de Pocitos Litio SA (fictional), a lithium brine operation and hydroxide refinery in Salta province, Argentina (Illustrative), June 2026. Senior debt requested USD 1,137.8 million on a USD 1,986.4 million cost. Hanbit Energy Materials Co. (fictional), a Korean battery maker, offers a 10-year offtake for 60% of hydroxide output with a floor of USD 11,500/t and a ceiling of USD 24,000/t and a USD 150.0 million prepayment. The sponsor asks you to size on the floor at 1.30x and to treat the 40% balance as merchant at 2.00x. Board paper due in two weeks.

Reasoning outline: the floor is only as good as the battery maker's credit and its right to reject off-spec product, which bites during qualification; refinery ramp-up and yield are manufacturing risks (Chapter 77) not covered by the floor; the prepayment ranks somewhere against the senior debt and must be subordinated or capped; the expert sizes contracted volume at the floor only after qualification, uses a stricter merchant deck for the rest, adds a four-part completion test with a yield and specification test, and requires sponsor completion support through qualification.

What would change the answer: an investment-grade offtaker with a take-or-pay and a narrow rejection right (raises debt); government price-floor support of the MP Materials kind or import-linked lending from the buyer's state (ssec:78.2.2, ssec:78.4.2; raises debt, adds policy risk); a sponsor with an operating refinery of the same flowsheet (shortens completion); a host fiscal regime under review (lowers debt or requires PRI).

## 78.13 Exercise set

Tier 1 (4):
- 78.1: Explain C1 versus AISC and why lenders look at AISC for downside cover.
- 78.2: Explain why mining debt is sized at higher DSCRs than an availability PPP, using the amortization periods of Skouries and QB2 as evidence.
- 78.3: List the four parts of a mining completion test and the risk each retires.
- 78.4: Explain why the Oyu Tolgoi dispute was resolved by renegotiation rather than a MIGA claim.

Tier 2 (5; all inputs and expected answers below, computed in Python):
- 78.5: Sierra Zacatecana Zinc SA de CV (fictional), an underground zinc-lead-silver mine in Zacatecas, Mexico, 2025. Inputs: ore 2.85 Mt a year at 6.4% Zn; recovery 89.5%; zinc concentrate grade 52%; payable 85%; site costs USD 41.80/t ore; TC USD 260/dmt; freight 1.09 wmt per dmt at USD 48/wmt; lead by-product net credit USD 41.2 million; silver by-product net credit USD 52.8 million; sustaining capex USD 38.7 million; royalty 3% of net smelter return at zinc USD 1.31/lb (NSR = payable zinc revenue - TC - freight + by-product credits); 2,204.62 lb per t. Compute C1 and AISC per lb of payable zinc. Answers: payable zinc 305.92 million lb; site costs USD 119.1 million; TC USD 81.6 million; freight USD 16.4 million; C1 USD 0.40/lb; royalty USD 11.9 million; AISC USD 0.57/lb; AISC margin USD 0.74/lb.
- 78.6: Tamarugal Copper SpA (fictional), Chile, banking case 2025: reserves 96.0 Mt, throughput 8.0 Mtpa (12-year life); CFADS USD 486.0 million in year 1 falling 5.5% a year as grade declines (year 12 USD 260.8 million); discount rate 8%; required tail 30%. Compute maximum tenor under a tonnage tail and under an NPV tail (NPV of CFADS after maturity at least 30% of NPV of all CFADS). Answers: tonnage tail allows 8.4 years; NPV of all CFADS USD 2,874.9 million; remaining NPV share after 6 years 31.0% (passes) and after 7 years 24.0% (fails), so the NPV tail allows 6 years.
- 78.7: Part A, sizing: Cerro Pelado Gold Ltd (fictional), Ghana, 2025: bank-deck CFADS USD 507.9 million, downside CFADS USD 341.6 million, sizing DSCR 1.55x, 7-year level annuity at 7.85%. Answers: debt service USD 327.7 million; annuity factor 5.2332; debt USD 1,714.8 million; downside DSCR 1.04x. Part B, the Example 78.5 situation at Cerro Albarda: debt outstanding at the test date USD 1,612.4 million, 7 remaining annual level payments at 8.12%, minimum projected annual CFADS on the bank deck USD 426.0 million. Compute the projected minimum DSCR and the prepayment that restores 1.40x. Answers: annuity factor 5.1851; debt service USD 311.0 million; DSCR 1.37x; target debt service USD 304.3 million; debt that service supports USD 1,577.7 million; prepayment USD 34.7 million.
- 78.8: Ashanti Ridge Gold Ltd (fictional), Ghana, 2025: stream deposit USD 148.7 million paid 40% and 60% over two construction years (years 0 and 1); streamer receives 6.5% of payable gold of 152,400 oz a year (9,906 oz) for 14 years from year 2 and pays 22% of spot per ounce. Compute the annual net value to the streamer and its IRR at gold prices of USD 2,600, 2,150 and 1,800/oz. Answers: USD 20.09, 16.61 and 13.91 million a year; IRR 9.25%, 6.21% and 3.60%.
- 78.9: Draft a fiscal stability clause (lender-friendly variant) and annotate each paragraph.

Tier 3 (4):
- 78.10: Model task (blank workbook, one scenario selector): Pampa Roja Copper SpA (fictional), Chile, a 10-year plan. Inputs: ore 12.4 Mtpa; head grade 0.71% in year 1 falling 0.03 percentage points a year; recovery 87%; payable 96.5%; concentrate grade 25%; TC USD 80/dmt and RC USD 0.08/lb; site cost USD 14.20/t ore; tax 27% of EBITDA (simplified, no depreciation shield); sustaining capex USD 64.8 million a year; decks flat at USD 4.40/lb (base), USD 3.90/lb (bank) and USD 3.25/lb (downside); no by-products. Build tonnes to payable metal to CFADS with one selector. Answers: year-1 CFADS USD 302.7, 243.2 and 165.9 million; year-10 CFADS USD 114.0, 77.2 and 29.3 million; 10-year totals USD 2,083.5, 1,601.8 and 975.6 million (base, bank, downside).
- 78.11: Case analysis: Cobre Panamá. Write a two-page memo, as a prospective lender in 2019, on what diligence on the legal foundation and fiscal terms would have shown, using only fact-sheet facts available by then.
- 78.12: Credit paper: recommend a structure (debt quantum, tenor, completion test, cover) for the Salar de Pocitos project in the judgment drill under a changed fact (the offtaker is unrated).
- 78.13: Case P extension: list the conditions under which Bélanou's lenders could consent to supplying Hautes-Moraba directly, using the Case Bible's description of the single-buyer law and the Annex P 4.14 request terms.

## 78.15 Glossary entries owned

Per the glossary canon: "bank price deck" (ssec:75.3.2, R-103), "reserve tail" (ssec:45.6.3, R-104) and "strip ratio" (ssec:12.3.2, R-026) are homed elsewhere and are cross-referenced, not owned. Terms owned here:

| Term | Abbreviation | Definition | Home |
|---|---|---|---|
| cost curve | – | A ranking of all producers of a commodity by unit cost, plotted against cumulative output, used to judge which mines survive low prices. | ssec:78.1.1 |
| C1 cash cost | C1 | Site operating costs plus treatment, refining and freight charges, less by-product credits, per unit of payable main metal. | ssec:78.1.1 |
| all-in sustaining cost | AISC | C1 cash cost plus sustaining capital, royalties and corporate costs needed to keep the mine producing, per unit of payable metal. | ssec:78.1.1 |
| by-product credit | – | Revenue from secondary metals in a mine's output, deducted from costs when computing unit cost of the main metal. | ssec:78.1.1 |
| critical mineral | – | A mineral that a government designates as essential to its economy or security and exposed to supply concentration or disruption. | ssec:78.2.1 |
| care and maintenance | – | The state of a mine that has stopped production but is kept safe and capable of restart, at a continuing cost. | ssec:78.3.3 |
| profit-based royalty | – | A royalty calculated on a measure of mine profit rather than on gross revenue or volume. | ssec:78.5.4 |
| fiscal stability clause | – | A host-agreement provision that freezes or compensates for changes in the tax and royalty regime applying to a project. | ssec:78.6.1 |
| carried interest (state) | – | A host state's equity share in a mine or field whose capital contributions are funded by the other shareholders, usually repayable from the state's share of distributions (canon wording). | ssec:78.6.2 |
| four-part completion test | – | Framework 78.1: mechanical, physical, metallurgical and operating, and financial tests that a mine must pass before sponsor completion support is released. | ssec:78.7.3 |
| deck-tail-test triangle | – | Framework 78.2: the check that sizing deck, reserve tail and financial completion test use consistent assumptions. | ssec:78.7.3 |
| debt cap | – | The maximum senior debt the finance documents allow, including any supplemental tranches that may be added later on ratio tests. | ssec:78.7.4 |

## 78.16 Neighbors

Before: Chapter 77, Refining, petrochemicals, and manufacturing finance. It treats refineries, crackers and cell factories as spread businesses, ranks margin protection with Framework 77.1, teaches multi-unit completion tests and sponsor support, and follows Sadara, Duqm, Northvolt Ett and the DOE battery plants from financing to distress or exit.

After: Chapter 79, Toll roads, bridges, and tunnels. It teaches value of time, toll elasticity and ramp-up; bridge and tunnel geotechnical risk; ways of sharing demand risk (minimum revenue guarantees, revenue bands, flexible-term concessions, rebalancing); and the record of real toll-road failures, against which it sets the Merrick Link's 2019 to 2025 ramp-up (Case T).
