# Chapter 79. Toll roads, bridges, and tunnels

Rulings applied:
- R-113 and D-028: title "Toll roads, bridges, and tunnels" (the parenthetical "Case T ramp-up" leaves the title; the beat stays in sec:79.14).
- R-052 and D-021: the minimum revenue guarantee is owned by Chapter 57 (ssec:57.5.1, valuation as a contingent liability, eq:57.3); shadow tolls, revenue-sharing bands and collars and least-present-value-of-revenue concessions by Chapter 58 (ssec:58.1.3, eq:58.1). Sec:79.4 applies and calibrates them for toll roads with real cases (Chile, Colombia). Framework 79.1 is renamed "Demand-risk sharing menu for toll roads" and cites ssec:57.5.1 and ssec:58.1.3. The u16 glossary entries for these instruments are deleted. Economic equilibrium rebalancing is owned by ssec:17.1.3; ssec:79.4.3 applies it.
- R-053: the competing-facility principle and the glossary term "competing facility" are owned by ssec:17.2.1; ssec:79.6.2 and cl:79.1 own the road-specific drafting and network-change compensation.
- R-026: value of time (ssec:12.4.1), toll elasticity and ramp-up (ssec:12.4.2) and revenue leakage (ssec:12.4.4) are Chapter 12 terms used here without bold; this chapter owns the sector mechanics built on them.
- R-035: operating leverage is cited to eq:14.2 where the chapter explains why a small traffic shortfall hits equity hard.
- R-101 and D-029: the chapter owns only the items in architecture.md's Owns note for Chapter 79.
- R-105 and Annex TR T.16 and T.17: the Case T beat uses figures T-F04, T-F06, T-F18 and T-F19; Callum's appearance (story date 2021) precedes in story order his last scene in Chapter 64.
- Sequencing review: ssec:79.4.1 retitled "Calibrating guarantees and revenue-sharing bands for toll roads"; standards review defects 3 (sec:79.6, sec:79.7, ssec:79.7.1, ssec:79.7.2 retitled), 13 (ssec:79.5.3 "The record" retitled), 4 (examples located), 6 (Tier 2 and Tier 3 inputs and answers); coverage defect 5 (t-traffic-forecast-accuracy, chile-concessions and covid-toll-roads placed; the request chile-lpvr is superseded by chile-concessions).

## 79.1 Header

- Chapter: 79
- Title: Toll roads, bridges, and tunnels
- File: `chapters/79-roads-bridges-and-tunnels.tex`
- Target length: 10,000 to 11,000 words (longer than other sector chapters because it carries the Case T ramp-up installment); the target is a floor for the core teaching, not a cap
- Dominant lens: a transport project finance banker who has lived through toll-road ramp-up failures, with the traffic advisor's and the PPP official's views.

## 79.2 Purpose and capability statement

Purpose. A toll road sells time to drivers who can usually choose a free road instead. This chapter teaches the economics of that choice (value of time, elasticity, competing routes, ramp-up), what makes bridges and tunnels different (monopoly crossings, geotechnical risk, safety systems), the full menu of ways to share demand risk between the state and the concessionaire, the financing terms and modeling specifics of the sector, and the record of real toll-road failures. It then runs the Merrick Link's actual 2019 to 2025 traffic against that record and classifies its shortfall.

What you will be able to do:
- Estimate willingness to pay and the diversion share for a toll road, and the revenue effect of a toll change at a given elasticity (Capabilities 2, 13).
- Choose among full demand transfer, revenue bands, minimum revenue guarantees, flexible-term concessions and availability payments for a given road, and calibrate the chosen instrument, from the government's and the lenders' side (Capabilities 9, 15).
- Diagnose a ramp-up shortfall as timing or structural and say what each means for debt (Capabilities 10, 11).
- Recognize the patterns of Dulles Greenway, SH 130, Indiana Toll Road, Sydney's tunnels and Eurotunnel in a live deal (Capabilities 13, 15).

## 79.3 Concepts owned and assumed

Owned:
1. Toll road economics built on the Chapter 12 terms (value of time, toll elasticity, ramp-up, revenue leakage; R-026): generalized cost, willingness to pay, route choice and diversion (binary logit at intuitive level), elasticity by vehicle class, the revenue-maximizing toll, competing free routes and network effects, complementary public works.
2. Greenfield against brownfield toll roads: the value of a traffic history; upfront concession fees and asset recycling (Indiana).
3. Bridge and tunnel specifics: monopoly crossings and captive demand, geotechnical risk and risk-sharing bands, tunnel safety systems, ventilation and M&E lifecycle, regulator-imposed design changes after financial close.
4. Road revenue forms at sector level: real tolls, shadow tolls, availability payments for roads, hybrids; managed lanes and dynamic pricing; free-flow tolling, leakage and enforcement. (Contract form of user tolls is owned by Chapter 21; availability and payment mechanisms by Chapter 58.)
5. Application and calibration for toll roads of the demand-risk sharing instruments owned by Chapters 57 and 58 (R-052): where to set a minimum revenue guarantee's floor and a revenue band's thresholds for a road, how Chile ran least-present-value-of-revenue auctions, how Colombia's 4G program topped up toll revenue, and how rebalancing (principle ssec:17.1.3) was applied to roads in the pandemic.
6. Framework 79.1, the demand-risk sharing menu for toll roads (original; a sector selection tool citing ssec:57.5.1 and ssec:58.1.3).
7. Ramp-up at sector level (term home ssec:12.4.2): typical duration as reported by the NSW Auditor-General (18 months to 3 years), the distinction between a timing shortfall and a structural shortfall, catch-up arithmetic, and the reference-class evidence on forecast optimism (S&P traffic risk studies 2002 to 2005 and Bain 2009, from t-traffic-forecast-accuracy).
8. Framework 79.2, the ramp-up diagnosis (original).
9. Road-specific competing-facility and network-change drafting (Clause 79.1 with variants; principle and glossary term at ssec:17.2.1, R-053).
10. Toll road financing at sector level: US managed lanes with PABs and TIFIA, bullet and mini-perm refinancing risk on brownfield leases, accreting swaps, long-tenor public credit.
11. Modeling specifics for roads (lender's review layer): class mix and multipliers, leakage, toll escalation formulas, elasticity link between tariff and traffic, lifecycle for pavement and tunnel systems, handback.

Assumed:
- Roads at financier depth: Chapter 12.
- User tolls and tariffs as contracts: Chapter 21 (toll regime of Case T introduced there).
- Design-build JV for Case T: Chapter 23.
- Traffic and demand studies (reading and challenging): Chapter 48 (Case T traffic study).
- Traffic ramp-up modeling: Chapter 45 (Case T traffic model, Sasha's revised method).
- Winner's curse and Case T's bid: Chapter 47.
- Value for money, PSC, Case T's decision to procure; the minimum revenue guarantee and its valuation as a contingent liability: Chapter 57 (ssec:57.5.1). Procurement, delivery models, payment mechanisms, shadow tolls, revenue-sharing bands and LPVR (ssec:58.1.3), termination compensation, refinancing gain sharing, Case T's preferred bidder: Chapter 58.
- Economic equilibrium and competing-facility undertakings: Chapter 17 (ssec:17.1.3, ssec:17.2.1). Operating leverage: eq:14.2.
- Value of time, toll elasticity, ramp-up and revenue leakage as terms: Chapter 12 (ssec:12.4.1, ssec:12.4.2, ssec:12.4.4).
- Federal credit programs (NILO in Case T; TIFIA-type lenders): Chapter 29. Project bonds and tax-exempt structures: Chapter 30.
- Sizing and sculpting: Chapter 36. Reserves and lock-ups: Chapter 37. Swaps and their mark-to-market: Chapter 6.
- Distress, standstill, restructuring plan and cram-down, Case T's restructuring: Chapter 64.
- Force majeure, relief events: Chapters 10 and 17.

Forward references: none.

## 79.4 Locked table of contents

Opening (no heading). The Dulles Greenway opens on September 29, 1995, six months ahead of schedule, at a USD 1.75 toll; traffic falls well short because the corridor develops more slowly than expected; in March 1996 the owner cuts the toll to USD 1.00 and is in default almost at once. Thirty years later the road earns positive cash flow but its accreting debt is above USD 1.1 billion and distributions are locked up until at least the end of 2028. Source: dulles-greenway. The opening ends by pointing to a 2019 opening in Brannock.

What you will be able to do (unnumbered).

79.1 What a toll road sells (sec:79.1)
- 79.1.1 Value of time and willingness to pay (ssec:79.1.1). Generalized cost; Example 79.1 (logit diversion). Equation 79.1 (binary logit share).
- 79.1.2 Elasticity and the revenue-maximizing toll (ssec:79.1.2). Example 79.2 (the Greenway's toll cut at two elasticities). Elasticities by class (trucks against cars) as a principle.
- 79.1.3 Greenfield and brownfield (ssec:79.1.3). Traffic history; Indiana Toll Road as brownfield (75-year lease, about USD 3.8 billion upfront fee at close on June 29, 2006) against SH 130 as greenfield (opened late 2012, traffic roughly half of forecast per the Bond Buyer).
- 79.1.4 Networks, free alternatives, and promised public works (ssec:79.1.4). Parallel free routes (SH 130 against I-35); surface road changes and reversals around Sydney's Cross City Tunnel; Lane Cove compensation of AUD 25 million for the RTA's own changes. Sets up the competing-facility clause in 79.6.

79.2 Bridges and tunnels (sec:79.2)
- 79.2.1 Monopoly crossings and captive demand (ssec:79.2.1). Why crossings with no alternative carry less demand risk and attract regulation; why a second crossing can destroy the first.
- 79.2.2 Ground risk and how to share it (ssec:79.2.2). Port of Miami Tunnel's geotechnical band (first USD 10 million of changed-ground costs on the concessionaire, next USD 150 million on FDOT, next USD 20 million on the concessionaire, then a mutual termination right); TBM selection; Exhibit 79.1 (the band as a stacked bar, Real case).
- 79.2.3 Safety systems, lifecycle, and regulation after close (ssec:79.2.3). Ventilation, fire suppression, control systems; M&E replacement cycles; Eurotunnel as the case where regulator-imposed design changes after financial close added cost (fact sheet angle; state as the fact sheet frames it).

79.3 How roads earn revenue (sec:79.3)
- 79.3.1 Real tolls, shadow tolls, availability, and hybrids (ssec:79.3.1). One paragraph each; cross-reference Chapters 21 and 58 (shadow toll defined at ssec:58.1.3); Port of Miami Tunnel as the availability-paid tunnel (no tolls; milestone and availability payments; about USD 1.1 billion cost; USD 341.5 million senior bank debt; USD 341 million TIFIA; about USD 80 million equity).
- 79.3.2 Managed lanes and dynamic pricing (ssec:79.3.2). How dynamic tolls trade volume for price; US managed-lane financings from t-market-norms (NTE, I-66, I-77, LBJ).
- 79.3.3 Free-flow tolling, class mix, and leakage (ssec:79.3.3). Example 79.5 (revenue build and the cost of losing trucks).

79.4 Sharing demand risk (sec:79.4)
- 79.4.1 Calibrating guarantees and revenue-sharing bands for toll roads (ssec:79.4.1). Application only (R-052): the instruments are defined in ssec:57.5.1 and ssec:58.1.3. Example 79.3 calibrates a floor and a band for one road; Colombia 4G's minimum toll revenue top-ups at set intervals (colombia-4g); Chile's early fixed-term concessions with "generous minimum toll revenue guarantees" in Engel, Fischer and Galetovic's words (chile-concessions); the fiscal cost and contingent liability (cross-reference Chapter 57).
- 79.4.2 Flexible-term concessions in Chile (ssec:79.4.2). Application only (LPVR defined at ssec:58.1.3, eq:58.1). Credit the concept to Engel, Fischer and Galetovic (World Bank Viewpoint No. 112 and the AER paper of May 1997; Journal of Political Economy, October 2001). Real use from chile-concessions: Chile ran the first outright PVR auction for the Santiago–Valparaíso–Viña del Mar highway in February 1998 and used it again for Route 160 (February 2007), the road to Santiago's main airport (December 2007), Melipilla–Camino de la Fruta and Vallenar–Caldera (January 2008); the winning bid fixes early-termination compensation as the bid minus the present value of revenue collected; the cost is weaker incentives to raise demand, so quality standards must be regulated. Context: Chile's 50 concessions of 1993 to 2006 saw 148 renegotiations by 2007, raising investment from about USD 8.4 to 8.5 billion to USD 11.3 billion, 73% of the renegotiated amount during construction (one sentence; the full lesson belongs to Chapter 57). Example 79.4. Do not state the 1998 contract's discount rate, winning bid or consortium, or that all Chilean concessions since 1998 use LPVR.
- 79.4.3 Rebalancing and term extension (ssec:79.4.3). Economic-equilibrium principle (owned by ssec:17.1.3) applied to civil-law road concessions; Spain's Royal Decree-law 8/2020, Article 34.4 (term extension up to 15% or modification of economic clauses, only where the authority accepts impossibility of performance); Ontario's acceptance that COVID-19 was force majeure under the 407 ETR concession, which suspended "Schedule 22" traffic-threshold payments until traffic regained its 2017 to 2019 average or tolls rose (they rose on February 1, 2024), and Quebec's compensation for the A25 toll suspension of March 25 to May 24, 2020 (covid-toll-roads); Case T's six-year extension in 2023 as the common-law negotiated equivalent (cross-reference Chapter 64).
- 79.4.4 Choosing a demand-risk structure for a road (ssec:79.4.4). Framework 79.1 applied to three roads (a brownfield interstate, a greenfield urban tunnel, a rural bypass).

79.5 Ramp-up and how it fails (sec:79.5)
- 79.5.1 Timing shortfalls and structural shortfalls (ssec:79.5.1). Framework 79.2.
- 79.5.2 Catch-up arithmetic (ssec:79.5.2). Example 79.6; Equation 79.2 (years to converge).
- 79.5.3 The forecasting record and five toll-road failures (ssec:79.5.3). First the reference class from t-traffic-forecast-accuracy: S&P's traffic risk studies (Bain and co-authors, 2002 to 2005, rating-agency research, not peer-reviewed) found actual year-1 traffic averaging 0.73 to 0.77 of the forecast used at financial close (104 projects by 2005, standard deviation about 0.26, range 0.14 to 1.51), with means of 0.78, 0.79, 0.80 and 0.79 in years 2 to 5, so the shortfall did not close after ramp-up; bank-commissioned forecasts averaged 0.82 against 0.66 for sponsors' and bidders' (2002); countries new to tolling 0.58 against 0.81 with a tolling history (Bain 2009); truck forecasts were more volatile (standard deviation 0.33). Bain's synthesis: toll forecasts carry the random error of untolled ones plus an optimism bias of about 20%, attributed to bidding; Flyvbjerg and co-authors (2005) found untolled road forecasts unbiased on average but half off by more than 20% (do not use those results as toll-road evidence); GHD's December 8, 2011 report to the Australian Department of Infrastructure and Transport found that bidding selects "the most optimistic of optimistic forecasts". Lender lesson as S&P stated it: adjust the base case for bias before running sensitivities, and size liquidity for persistent shortfalls. Then Exhibit 79.2 (table of real cases, each with forecast basis, outcome and failure mode, sourced only from fact sheets): Dulles Greenway (corridor growth slower; toll cut); SH 130 (roughly half of forecast; parallel I-35; rural corridor dependent on development); Cross City Tunnel (under 25,000 vehicles a day at full toll against a bid forecast of about 70,000 at opening and 87,088 by June 2006; winning bid's projections higher than every other projection); Lane Cove Tunnel (receivership January 19, 2010); Eurotunnel (market share forecast correctly, total market overestimated, fare competition). No traffic forecast numbers for Greenway, Lane Cove or Eurotunnel (fact-sheet "Do not state").

79.6 Concession deed, D&C contract, and tolling-system contract for a toll road (sec:79.6)
- 79.6.1 The road contract map (ssec:79.6.1). Exhibit 79.3 (TikZ contract map of a user-pay road concession, Illustrative: contracting authority, project company, D&C JV, O&M and tolling operator, independent certifier, senior lenders, bondholders, public subordinated lender, sponsors).
- 79.6.2 Competing facilities and network change (ssec:79.6.2). Principle and glossary term at ssec:17.2.1 (R-053); here the road-specific drafting. Clause 79.1 with variants a (sponsor), b (lender), c (government), and a landing paragraph ("In user-pay road PPPs in common-law OECD markets, 2005 to 2025, the compromise..." stated as practitioner observation; reviewer sign-off under D-011).
- 79.6.3 Toll regime, tolling back office, and enforcement (ssec:79.6.3). Cross-reference Chapter 21; sector points: video enforcement, interoperability, back-office fee structures (Case T's back office at 6.2% of toll revenue plus a fixed amount, Case Bible 2.5).
- 79.6.4 Relief events and pandemic outcomes (ssec:79.6.4). Case T's relief event without compensation (Case Bible 2.7, 2.8); COVID-19 responses for demand-risk transport (covid-transport): UK PFI guidance (not force majeure; keep paying, suspend deductions), Spain RDL 8/2020. What toll roads actually lived through (covid-toll-roads): Transurban's group average daily traffic fell 34.6% in the June 2020 quarter (Melbourne 44.1%; North American express lanes 51.5% in traffic and 76.7% in toll revenue) and recovered from mid-April as restrictions eased, with large vehicles more resilient than cars; Transurban kept its BBB+/Baa1/A- ratings, raised AUD 8.6 billion of debt including AUD 1.5 billion of bilateral facilities and cut its distribution; Atlantia reported 2020 traffic falls of 27.3% in Italy, 30.8% in Spain and 24.6% in France against 76.8% at Rome's airports; 407 ETR's 2020 revenue fell 39.6% and EBITDA 43.5% (computed from Ferrovial's factbook), with dividends cut. Lessons: liquidity and distribution lock-ups protected senior debt through a sharp but short shock; contractual traffic thresholds needed force majeure relief; relief came by contract, compensation or statute, and its wording decided who got it. Do not state rating actions on any toll road in 2020 other than Transurban's unchanged ratings, Abertis's compensation outcomes, or 407 ETR traffic in vehicle-kilometers.

79.7 How toll roads have been financed (sec:79.7)
- 79.7.1 Financing terms from SH 130 to the US managed lanes (ssec:79.7.1). Exhibit 79.4 (table): SH 130 (2008: about USD 686 million senior bank loans, USD 430 million TIFIA (35 years, principal deferred to 2018, maturing 2047), about USD 210 million equity; debt about 84% of USD 1.33 billion cost); Indiana Toll Road (2006: about USD 3.4 billion initial debt, nine-year interest-only bullet, 20-year accreting swap with pre-set rates from 3% to 11.3%); Port of Miami Tunnel (2009: 80% debt of USD 903 million private uses); US managed lanes (NTE PABs 5.50% maturing 2052 to 2058, issued August 3, 2023; I-66 PABs 5.00% to 2047 to 2056 and TIFIA about USD 1,339 million at 2.8% to 2057; I-77 6.57% notes to 2051 refinancing TIFIA on April 25 to 26, 2024; LBJ TIFIA about USD 836 million at 4.22%); Eurotunnel's 2007 term loan of GBP 1.5 billion plus EUR 1.965 billion with tenors to 2050. Latin American instruments without public pricing (t-market-norms-2): IFC's COP-linked A loans to Colombian 4G and 5G concessions (for example up to USD 200 million, approved at USD 213.2 million, for Buga–Loboguerrero–Buenaventura, signed August 8, 2025) and IFC's bridge of up to BRL 700 million (signed March 28, 2023) toward a BRL 1.29 billion upfront concession fee in Brazil; margins and tenors are not disclosed, which is itself the lesson.
- 79.7.2 Cover and gearing ranges for user-pay roads, 2010 to 2025 (ssec:79.7.2). D-011 indicative only: greenfield user-pay toll roads in OECD markets, 2010 to 2025, sized at minimum DSCRs of roughly 1.40x to 1.75x on the lenders' traffic case with gearing of roughly 55% to 75%; brownfield roads with five or more years of history at roughly 1.30x to 1.50x (indicative, D-011). Case T's own sizing (1.50x on the banking case, LLCR 1.55x, 55% cap; Case Bible 2.5) is the worked instance and is stated as Case T, not as market data. Drivers ranked: traffic history, existence of a free alternative, state support instruments, refinancing structure.
- 79.7.3 Structures that turn traffic risk into refinancing risk (ssec:79.7.3). Bullet loans and accreting swaps (Indiana), mini-perms (Case T's bank tranche), zero-coupon accreting debt (Greenway 1999 and 2005), long public credit (TIFIA, NILO); what each does when traffic disappoints.

79.8 Modeling a road for lenders (sec:79.8)
- 79.8.1 From trips to toll revenue (ssec:79.8.1). Revenue build with class mix, trip length, multipliers, leakage (Example 79.5 is the instance); cross-reference Chapter 45 for the cell-level traffic model.
- 79.8.2 Linking tolls to traffic (ssec:79.8.2). Why a toll change must move traffic in the model through elasticity, not leave it fixed; scenario switch for toll policy.
- 79.8.3 Lifecycle, handback, and tunnel systems (ssec:79.8.3). Lifecycle profiles (pavement, M&E) and the lifecycle reserve (cross-reference Chapter 37 and Chapter 65).

79.9 SH 130 and the Indiana Toll Road, two roads to Chapter 11 (sec:79.9)
Real case section. SH 130: greenfield, parallel free road, debt sized on forecasts that came in at roughly half, downgrades in 2013, Chapter 11 in March 2016, plan effective June 28, 2017 (sponsors' equity cancelled, more than USD 1 billion of debt removed, new USD 260 million term loan). Indiana: brownfield, high bid, short bullet debt and an accreting swap whose liability reached about USD 2.15 billion, prepackaged Chapter 11 announced September 22, 2014, sale to IFM for USD 5.725 billion closing May 27, 2015; state kept its USD 3.8 billion. Lesson: asset risk against structure risk; both ended in the same court. Cross-reference Chapter 64 for process.

79.10 Sydney's tunnels and the outlier forecast (sec:79.10)
Real case section from sydney-tunnels: Auditor-General's findings; upfront payment linked to optimistic patronage; receiverships and going-concern sales; the second Cross City failure (do not state its cause as established). Lesson: when the winning bid's forecast is above every other forecast, the gap is the winner's curse in traffic form (cross-reference Chapter 47).

79.11 Eurotunnel and the Greenway, when the market is smaller than the forecast (sec:79.11)
Real case section from eurotunnel and dulles-greenway. Eurotunnel: share right, market wrong, fare competition, interest suspended September 1995, restructurings of 1998 and 2006 to 2007 (debt cut from EUR 9.073 billion to EUR 4.164 billion). Greenway: restructurings of 1999 and 2005 into accreting zero-coupon debt, concession extended to 2056, the SCC hearing examiner's 2020 finding. Lesson: extending time preserves the asset and transfers value from equity to time; regulated toll ceilings can make a demand problem permanent.

79.12 Colombia's 4G program and how a template made demand risk bankable (sec:79.12)
Real case section from colombia-4g: functional units, ANI payments and minimum toll revenue top-ups, FDN liquidity, currency-matched tranches, Law 1882 of 2018 on lender repayment upon annulment; 18 of 29 projects at financial close by April 2020. Lesson: the state can make user-pay roads bankable without taking all demand risk, if the instruments are designed for lenders. Do not give a single project count or investment figure without attribution.

79.13 Walkthrough: diagnosing a ramp-up shortfall from the first year of transaction data (sec:79.13)
Task with an Illustrative 12-month dataset (Exhibit 79.5). Steps under Walkthroughs.

79.14 Case T: the Merrick Link ramp-up, 2019 to 2025 (sec:79.14)
Running-case installment.

79.15 Practitioner's notebook (sec:79.15). 79.16 Judgment drill (sec:79.16).

79.17 Drivers choose; lenders can only price the choice (sec:79.17)
Close. Crystallizes value of time, elasticity, ramp-up diagnosis and the sharing menu. Final paragraph poses the next problem: rail, airports and ports also carry users, but their users are airlines, shipping lines and transit authorities with their own strategies; how does a lender analyze demand that belongs to another company's network (Chapter 80).

79.18 Exercises (sec:79.18). 79.19 Solutions to exercises (sec:79.19). Sources.

## 79.5 Opening

Dulles Greenway's opening day and its toll cut within six months, from the fact sheet (no traffic forecast numbers: the fact sheet forbids the commonly repeated ones). Then one paragraph moving to May 6, 2019, the Merrick Link's opening 36 days late (Case Bible 2.2), and its first half-year at 28.1 thousand trips a day (Case Bible 2.6), without explaining causes until Section 79.14.

## 79.6 Worked examples

Example 79.1. Willingness to pay and diversion (Illustrative). Setting: Brazos Bypass Partners LLC (fictional) bids in 2025 for an 18.7 km tolled bypass near College Station, Texas, procured by a fictional regional mobility authority; the bypass saves 14 minutes against the free route for a car; value of time USD 19.40/hour (2025 prices): willingness to pay = 19.40 x 14/60 = USD 4.53. Binary logit share of the tolled route P = 1/(1 + exp(theta x (toll - WTP))) with theta = 0.92 per USD (a calibration parameter, stated as illustrative). Corridor demand 48,200 car trips a day. At toll USD 2.40: share 87.6%, 42,231 trips, toll revenue USD 101,354 a day. At USD 3.20: 77.2%, 37,218 trips, USD 119,098 a day. At USD 4.10: 59.7%, 28,770 trips, USD 117,958 a day. Revenue-maximizing toll in this model: about USD 3.61 (revenue about USD 121,656 a day). Teaching point: the bidder's revenue is most sensitive to the value of time it assumes, which is exactly what Case T's traffic advisor overstated (Case Bible 2.6; 22% above the later survey); the writer makes that link in one sentence without computing anything for Case T.

Example 79.2. A toll cut at two elasticities (Illustrative, set at the Dulles Greenway in Loudoun County, Virginia, in March 1996, using its public toll figures but not presented as its traffic or revenue data). Toll falls from USD 1.75 to USD 1.00 (a 42.9% cut). Constant-elasticity demand Q1 = Q0 x (P1/P0)^e. At e = -0.30: traffic rises 18.3%, revenue falls to 67.6% of before. At e = -0.60: traffic rises 39.9%, revenue falls to 79.9% of before. Teaching point: with inelastic demand a toll cut buys traffic and loses revenue; the writer labels this as illustrative arithmetic and does not claim what happened to Greenway revenue. Sasha's habit ("minus point six, not minus point three") may be referenced in the Case T installment, not here.

Example 79.3. Calibrating a minimum revenue guarantee with a revenue-sharing band (Illustrative). Setting: Concesionaria Vial Río Cauca SAS (fictional), an interurban toll road in Valle del Cauca, Colombia, offered for tender in 2024 by a fictional regional concession agency (the instruments are defined in ssec:57.5.1 and ssec:58.1.3; this example calibrates them). Base-case revenue USD 164.8 million a year. State guarantees 80% of base (pays the shortfall below USD 131.84 million) and takes 50% of revenue above 120% of base (USD 197.76 million). Outcomes: revenue at 62% of base (USD 102.18 million): state pays USD 29.66 million; net USD 131.84 million. At 94% (USD 154.91 million): no payments. At 131% (USD 215.89 million): state receives USD 9.06 million; net USD 206.82 million. Debt sizing with operating costs of USD 38.6 million: on the floor, CFADS USD 93.24 million, debt service at 1.30x = USD 71.72 million; on the base case without guarantee at 1.60x, CFADS USD 126.20 million, debt service USD 78.88 million. Teaching point: the guarantee lets lenders size on the floor at a lower ratio and gets close to base-case debt with far less traffic risk; the state carries a contingent liability that Chapter 57 teaches it to count.

Example 79.4. A least-present-value-of-revenue concession (Illustrative). Setting: Autopista Biobío Costa SA (fictional) wins a 2025 LPVR auction for a coastal highway in Chile's Biobío region run by a fictional grantor on the Chilean model (chile-concessions); amounts in USD for comparability. Winning bid PVR USD 1,284.6 million at a 6.5% contractual discount rate; maximum term 45 years. Base: first-year revenue USD 71.4 million growing 3.5%: concession ends after 27.2 years. High: USD 88.9 million growing 4.0%: 18.9 years. Low: USD 58.6 million growing 2.8%: would need 47.1 years; at the 45-year cap the concessionaire has collected PV USD 1,261.2 million (98.2% of the bid), a shortfall of USD 23.4 million in present value. Teaching point: LPVR converts traffic risk into term risk, which suits debt only if lenders can lend to a variable maturity (cross-reference Chapter 36 on tenor and tail); the cap brings some traffic risk back; at early termination the concessionaire is owed the bid less the present value already collected. Verified in Python (revision round 1).

Example 79.5. Revenue build and the cost of losing trucks (Illustrative). Setting: Peachtree Ridge Expressway LLC (fictional), a 31.6 km free-flow tolled expressway in suburban Atlanta, Georgia, 2025 budget; 38,450 trips a day; average trip 17.4 km; car toll USD 0.168/km; mix 82.0% cars (1.0), 7.5% light commercial (1.5), 10.5% heavy (3.0); weighted multiplier 1.2475; leakage 2.7%; 365 days. Gross toll revenue USD 51.18 million; net revenue USD 49.80 million. If heavy vehicles fall to 6.0% of the original trips (4.5 points lost; trips fall to 36,720 a day; weighted multiplier 1.1649), net revenue falls to USD 44.41 million, a 10.8% loss for a 4.5% loss of trips. Teaching point: trucks are few and carry a large share of revenue; set the heavy-vehicle multiplier against the free route's cost for trucks (the Case T problem, Case Bible 2.6).

Example 79.6. Catch-up arithmetic (Illustrative). Actual traffic in year 1 is 65% of forecast; forecast grows 3.4% a year. If actual grows 6.0% a year, it meets the forecast path after ln(1/0.65)/ln(1.06/1.034) = 17.3 years; at 4.5% growth, 40.7 years. Equation 79.2. Setting: placeless arithmetic, used to read the evidence in ssec:79.5.3 (the S&P studies' year-1 mean of about 0.77 that did not improve by year 5). Teaching point: a large first-year gap does not close through faster growth within a debt tenor unless the cause is timing (development arriving later); otherwise it is a level shift and the debt must be resized.

## 79.7 Walkthrough

Walkthrough: diagnosing a ramp-up shortfall from the first year of transaction data (Section 79.13). Exhibit 79.5 (Illustrative, writer builds the dataset and recomputes all figures; 12 months of transactions by class, average trip length, revenue per transaction, peak-hour share, origin-destination share from the toll system, compared with the banking case by month).
1. Reconcile transactions to revenue (class mix, trip length, discounts, leakage) to find whether the gap is volume or yield.
2. Split by class: if trucks are far below forecast while cars are close, test the truck toll against the free-route cost.
3. Split by time: peak-hour share and weekday-weekend pattern tell whether commuters (value of time) or growth-area trips (development timing) are missing.
4. Compare origin-destination patterns with the land-use assumptions (housing completions).
5. Check network events (works on the free road promised and not delivered).
6. Classify each component of the gap under Framework 79.2 as timing or structural, with evidence.
7. Write the lenders' traffic advisor's instruction for the re-forecast and the lenders' note on sizing consequences (lock-up, sweep, DSRA).

## 79.8 Frameworks

Framework 79.1, the demand-risk sharing menu for toll roads (original; label fw:demand-risk-menu; a sector selection tool that cites ssec:57.5.1 and ssec:58.1.3 for the instruments, R-052). Purpose: choose how much demand risk a road concessionaire should carry. Steps: (1) score traffic predictability (history, free alternative, captive demand); (2) score the government's fiscal capacity and accounting constraints (cross-reference Chapter 57); (3) pick from the menu: full transfer, revenue band or collar, minimum revenue guarantee, flexible-term (LPVR), availability payment with real tolls retained by the state, and calibrate the chosen instrument; (4) check bankability with the lenders' sizing logic (Example 79.3); (5) check incentives (who still cares about traffic). When to use: at the procurement design stage and when a bidder proposes changes.

Framework 79.2, the ramp-up diagnosis (original; label fw:ramp-up-diagnosis). Purpose: classify a traffic shortfall. Steps: (1) separate volume from yield; (2) separate by class, by time of day and by origin-destination; (3) test each component against the forecast's assumptions: development timing, value of time, toll by class against the free route, network changes, macro shocks; (4) classify each as timing (closes as the cause arrives), structural (permanent level shift) or policy-fixable (closes only if a tariff or network change is made); (5) translate into debt consequences: wait (timing), resize or restructure (structural), negotiate (policy-fixable). Demonstrated in the walkthrough and in the Case T installment.

## 79.9 Real cases and fact sheets

- dulles-greenway: ramp-up risk, toll cut, extend-and-pretend restructurings; opening, Example 79.2 setting and sec:79.11.
- sh130 and indiana-toll-road: two routes to Chapter 11 (greenfield traffic, brownfield structure); sec:79.9 and ssec:79.7.1.
- sydney-tunnels: outlier forecast, upfront fee linked to optimism, going-concern enforcement; sec:79.10 and ssec:79.1.4.
- eurotunnel: market size against market share, regulator-imposed changes; sec:79.11 and ssec:79.2.3.
- port-of-miami-tunnel: availability-paid tunnel and geotechnical band; ssec:79.2.2 and ssec:79.3.1.
- colombia-4g: state instruments that made demand risk bankable; sec:79.12 and ssec:79.4.1.
- covid-transport: relief and rebalancing outcomes; ssec:79.4.3 and ssec:79.6.4.
- t-market-norms: US managed lanes (PABs, TIFIA, refinancing notes); ssec:79.7.1.
- t-traffic-forecast-accuracy (placed in revision round 1): ssec:79.5.3 (the reference class), ssec:79.1.1 (one sentence: value-of-time and willingness-to-pay errors head the S&P list of causes), Example 79.6 and the Case T narration. Lesson in one line: toll forecasts at financial close have averaged about three-quarters of outturn in year 1 and did not catch up by year 5, so lenders correct the base case for bias before running sensitivities. Do not present the S&P studies as peer-reviewed, name roads in the anonymized sample, or claim the mean returns to 1.0 after ramp-up.
- chile-concessions (placed in revision round 1; supersedes the request chile-lpvr): ssec:79.4.1 (Chile's early minimum revenue guarantees), ssec:79.4.2 (the LPVR auctions of 1998 and 2007 to 2008, termination compensation, incentive weakness, renegotiation context) and Example 79.4. Lesson in one line: LPVR turns traffic risk into term risk and gives a formula for termination compensation, but it does not stop renegotiation.
- covid-toll-roads (placed in revision round 1): ssec:79.4.3 (407 ETR force majeure and the A25 compensation) and ssec:79.6.4 (Transurban, Atlantia and 407 ETR traffic, liquidity and distributions). Lesson in one line: a sharp, short demand shock is survivable with liquidity and lock-ups, and relief depends on wording and the grantor.
- t-market-norms-2: Colombian 4G and 5G IFC loans and the Brazilian upfront-fee bridge (ssec:79.7.1), pricing not public.

Open requests (the chapter can be taught without them): none. The version-1.0 requests t-traffic-forecast-accuracy, chile-lpvr (superseded by chile-concessions) and covid-toll-roads were delivered.

## 79.10 Running-case installment

Case T, story date 2019 to 2025; the scene is set at Pellow Transport Economics, Port Ellery, September 2021 (writer may choose the month in 2021 and reports it). Characters: Oleksandr (Sasha) Hrytsenko (Director, Pellow), Callum Petrie (Head of Investments, Holbrook Infrastructure). Callum's scene here is earlier in story order than his last scene, the 2023 restructuring meeting in Chapter 64 (Annex TR T.16). The installment opens by stating the time jump ("Two years and four months after the Merrick Link opened..."). If the Case Bible adds the lenders' technical and traffic monitoring adviser that the coverage review asks for (coverage defect 10), that character may take one line in the narration as the lenders' seat; until then no lenders' adviser is named.

Scene outline: Sasha walks Callum through his 2021 revisit of the bid forecast. Callum wants the gap to be COVID-19 and housing (timing). Sasha separates the components: housing completions about four years late (timing); a car value of time 22% above the later revealed-preference survey (structural); trucks avoiding the road because the 2.85 heavy-vehicle multiplier made the tolled route dearer than SR 14 for trips under 30 km (policy-fixable); deferred SR 14 traffic-calming works (policy-fixable, a state action); and the pandemic (timing). Sasha's elasticity habit is used once, about trucks. Callum's habit ("Close enough to win") appears once, ironically, about the BAFO case. Nobody concedes cleanly; Callum leaves insisting the 2022 numbers will show catch-up.

Narration after the box (teaching, outside the box): applies Framework 79.2 to Case T using the ledger decomposition; sets Case T against the reference class of ssec:79.5.3 (actual over Pellow 0.65 in 2019 and 0.62 in 2022 from T-F18, below the S&P year-1 mean of about 0.77; the ratio stays near 0.65 through 2026, which is the S&P "no catch-up" pattern); displays the traffic path and the revenue ramp; notes how the 2024 multiplier cut to 2.40 and the change in vehicle mix (Case Bible 2.2 and 2.6) moved traffic afterwards, citing 2024 to 2026 actuals; one sentence pointing to Chapter 64 for how the restructuring priced the structural component, and one to Chapter 45 for Sasha's revised ramp-up method. The narration does not compute any running-case figure; every ratio and decomposition comes from the ledger.

Ledger figures displayed:
- T-F04 (traffic: Pellow, Ridgeway, downside and actual, 2019 to 2026), as Exhibit 79.6 (table) and a pgfplots chart (Exhibit 79.7). Exhibit 79.6 also carries T-F18 (actual as a ratio of Pellow, Ridgeway and downside, 2019 to 2026, and of Ridgeway's 2023 case from 2024).
- T-F06 (revenue ramp-up: forecast against actual, 2019 to 2025), as Exhibit 79.8.
- T-F19 (shortfall against Pellow by cause, 2019 to 2022: housing, value of time, heavy vehicles, SR 14 works, COVID-19, in thousand trips a day and the indicative revenue split), as Exhibit 79.9 (new, appended), with each cause labeled timing, structural or policy-fixable under Framework 79.2.
- Case Bible inputs cited directly: 2019 average 30.4 against a base forecast of 46.7 (34.9% below); 2022 average 40.2 against base 64.6 (37.7% below); April 2020 traffic 62% below February 2020; heavy-vehicle multiplier 2.85 then 2.40 from January 1, 2024; vehicle mix 11.6% heavy, then 13.8% from 2024; average trip 23.8 km.
State at start: open road, lock-up at first test (Chapter 64 has told the distress). State at end: no change to the case; analysis only.

## 79.11 Practitioner's notebook (outline)

Checklist: free-alternative cost by class and trip length; value of time source and date (stated preference against revealed preference); development assumptions with independent evidence; ramp-up profile against the reference class; toll escalation formula and political tolerance; competing-facility clause; relief and compensation events for demand shocks; back-office leakage and enforcement; lifecycle for tunnel systems; refinancing structure.

Red flags: winning forecast above every other forecast; upfront concession payment tied to the bidder's traffic case; trucks priced above the free route's cost; ramp-up shorter than 18 months in a new corridor; bullet debt or accreting swaps on a demand-risk asset; promised public works not contracted.

Rules of thumb with limits: ramp-up of 18 months to 3 years is normal for a new urban road (NSW Auditor-General; limit: a corridor waiting for development); a first-year gap above about a quarter rarely closes within a debt tenor (Example 79.6 logic; limit: identified timing causes); truck share drives revenue disproportionately.

Common mistakes: holding traffic constant when tolls change; treating ramp-up as a percentage applied to a correct mature level; using peak-hour design models for daily revenue (the RTA's admission); adding growth on top of an over-forecast base.

Questions experts ask: What does a truck pay on the free road? Who decides the toll escalation in practice? Which forecast is the outlier? What happens to the debt if year three looks like year one? What did the government promise to build, and is it in the contract?

## 79.12 Judgment drill

The situation: you are credit officer for the senior lenders to Tagus Narrows Crossing SA (fictional), a greenfield toll bridge near Setúbal, Portugal (Illustrative), in March 2026, 14 months after opening; traffic is 71% of the banking case; the sponsor proposes a 30% toll discount for 12 months to "build habit", funded from the DSRA, and asks for the lock-up to be waived during the discount period. Lenders' meeting in five days.

Reasoning outline: test the proposal with elasticity (Example 79.2): at the plausible elasticity, revenue falls during the discount; habit formation is a weak empirical claim; the DSRA is the lenders' liquidity, not marketing money; diagnose the shortfall first (Framework 79.2): if the cause is a missing free-route restriction or development timing, a discount does not help. The expert allows a targeted discount (for example trucks, if the truck toll exceeds the free-route cost) funded by sponsor equity, keeps the lock-up, commissions a ramp-up diagnosis, and agrees in advance the trigger for a re-forecast and resizing.

What would change the answer: evidence from transaction data that price is the main barrier for a class (supports a targeted discount); a contracting authority willing to fund the discount or deliver the promised network change (changes who pays); a sponsor equity injection to fund the trial (removes the DSRA objection).

## 79.13 Exercise set

Tier 1 (4):
- 79.1: Explain value of time and why it drives toll revenue forecasts, citing the causes of error in the S&P 2002 study.
- 79.2: Distinguish timing and structural ramp-up shortfalls with one example of each from the real cases.
- 79.3: Explain why a monopoly bridge carries less demand risk than a parallel toll road and what can destroy that advantage.
- 79.4: Compare an MRG with an LPVR concession from the lenders' side, using Chile's record.

Tier 2 (5; all inputs and expected answers below, computed in Python):
- 79.5: Ruta Lagos Verdes SA (fictional), a 2025 bypass bid near Puerto Montt, Chile, priced in USD for the exercise. Inputs: value of time USD 22.60/hour; time saved 11 minutes; logit parameter 0.85 per USD; corridor demand 36,900 car trips a day; tolls USD 2.10, 3.00 and 3.90. Compute willingness to pay, shares, trips and daily revenue, and find the revenue-maximizing toll by trial to the nearest USD 0.10. Answers: willingness to pay USD 4.14; shares 85.0%, 72.5%, 55.2%; trips 31,375, 26,770, 20,351; revenue USD 65,889, 80,311 and 79,370 a day; revenue-maximizing toll USD 3.40 (about USD 81,913 a day).
- 79.6: Rhine Valley Link GmbH (fictional), Germany, 2025: 2,840 heavy-vehicle trips a day, trip length 21.5 km, car toll EUR 0.168/km, heavy multiplier cut from 3.0 to 2.5, truck elasticity -0.6 (constant elasticity). Compute daily heavy-vehicle revenue before and after and the annual change. Answers: before EUR 30,774 a day; trucks rise to 3,168 a day; after EUR 28,610 a day (minus 7.0%); annual change minus EUR 0.79 million. Then explain when the cut raises total revenue (when diverted trucks return in large enough numbers, an elasticity beyond minus 1.0).
- 79.7: Autopista del Sol Naciente SA (fictional), Peru, 2025: base-case revenue USD 118.3 million a year; floor 75% of base; upper threshold 125% of base with 50% of revenue above it shared to the state; five-year path at 61%, 72%, 88%, 109% and 133% of base; operating costs USD 29.4 million; size debt on the floor at 1.30x over 18 years at 6.2% (level annuity). Answers: state payments USD 16.56 and 3.55 million in years 1 and 2, nothing after; state receives USD 4.73 million in year 5; net revenue 88.72, 88.72, 104.10, 128.95, 152.61; floor CFADS USD 59.33 million; debt service USD 45.63 million; debt USD 486.8 million.
- 79.8: Ruta Costera Norte SA (fictional), Chile, LPVR bid USD 942.7 million at a 6.0% contract discount rate, maximum term 40 years; three revenue paths: USD 55.8 million growing 3.2%, USD 68.4 million growing 4.1%, USD 44.1 million growing 2.4% (revenue at year-end). Compute the concession term in each case and the shortfall under the cap. Answers: 23.9 years; 16.8 years; the low path needs 42.5 years, so at the 40-year cap the concessionaire collects USD 917.5 million in present value (97.3% of the bid), a shortfall of USD 25.2 million.
- 79.9: Draft the government-friendly variant of a competing-facility clause and annotate it (principle at ssec:17.2.1).

Tier 3 (4):
- 79.10: Model task (blank workbook): Lakeshore Connector LLC (fictional), Ohio, 2025. Inputs: 41,300 trips a day; average trip 19.6 km; car toll USD 0.154/km; class shares 84.0% cars (multiplier 1.0, elasticity -0.35), 6.5% light commercial (1.6, -0.45), 9.5% heavy (2.85, -0.60); leakage 2.2%; 365 days; scenario: all tolls rise 12% with traffic responding by constant elasticity per class. Answers: base net revenue USD 54.06 million; after the rise trips fall 4.2% to 39,559 a day and net revenue rises 6.9% to USD 57.77 million.
- 79.11: Case T: using only T-F04, T-F18, T-F19 and Case Bible 2.6, classify the 2019 to 2022 shortfall components under Framework 79.2 and say which the 2023 restructuring addressed (cross-reference Chapter 64). Key ledger facts the answer must use: shortfall against Pellow 16.3, 28.6, 26.0 and 24.4 thousand trips a day in 2019 to 2022; 2022 split 8.8 housing, 6.1 value of time, 4.4 heavy vehicles, 2.2 SR 14 works and 2.9 COVID-19 (T-F19).
- 79.12: Case problem: as a PPP unit designing a rural bypass with a free alternative, choose a demand-risk structure with Framework 79.1 and defend it to the treasury and to bidders' lenders.
- 79.13: Credit memo: compare SH 130 and Indiana Toll Road as a lender would, identifying the earliest warning sign in each from fact-sheet facts.

## 79.15 Glossary entries owned

Per the glossary canon: value of time (ssec:12.4.1), toll elasticity and ramp-up (ssec:12.4.2), revenue leakage (ssec:12.4.4), shadow toll, revenue-sharing band and least present value of revenue (ssec:58.1.3), minimum revenue guarantee (ssec:57.5.1), economic equilibrium (ssec:17.1.3) and competing facility (ssec:17.2.1) are homed elsewhere and are cross-referenced, not owned (R-026, R-052, R-053). Terms owned here:

| Term | Abbreviation | Definition | Home |
|---|---|---|---|
| generalized cost | – | The total cost of a trip as a traveler perceives it: money cost plus travel time valued at the value of time. | ssec:79.1.1 |
| diversion rate | – | The share of corridor trips that choose the tolled route over the free alternative. | ssec:79.1.1 |
| revenue-maximizing toll | – | The toll at which further increases lose more revenue from lost traffic than they gain from higher price. | ssec:79.1.2 |
| managed lanes | – | Tolled lanes alongside free lanes on the same road, priced (often dynamically) to keep traffic flowing. | ssec:79.3.2 |
| demand-risk sharing menu for toll roads | – | Framework 79.1: the structured choice among ways of allocating traffic risk between government and concessionaire. | ssec:79.4.4 |
| structural shortfall | – | A traffic shortfall caused by a permanently wrong assumption, which does not close with time. | ssec:79.5.1 |
| timing shortfall | – | A traffic shortfall caused by an event arriving later than forecast, which closes when the event arrives. | ssec:79.5.1 |
| ramp-up diagnosis | – | Framework 79.2: a procedure for classifying a traffic shortfall as timing, structural or policy-fixable and drawing the debt consequences. | ssec:79.5.1 |
