# Chapter 80. Rail, urban transit, airports, and ports

Rulings applied:
- R-113 and D-028: title "Rail, urban transit, airports, and ports" (serial comma).
- R-055 and R-117: single till and dual till are owned by Chapter 21 (ssec:21.8.1, glossary homes); this chapter owns hybrid till, price-cap regulation and the sector application, in ssec:80.2.2 retitled "Choosing a till and a price cap". The u16 glossary entries for single and dual till are deleted; "hybrid till" is added (central change: the canon has no entry).
- R-056: landlord, tool and service port models are owned by Chapter 12 (sec:12.7); the minimum annual guarantee by Chapter 21 (ssec:21.8.2). Sec:80.3 applies them; glossary duplicates deleted. MAG is distinct from MAGA (Chapter 17).
- R-026: farebox recovery ratio and rolling stock (sec:12.5), aeronautical revenue (sec:12.6), landlord port, TEU and transshipment (sec:12.7) are Chapter 12 terms used here without bold.
- R-053: competing-facility principle at ssec:17.2.1 and road drafting at ssec:79.6.2 are cited for port and airport exclusivity.
- R-051: availability payment and deduction regimes are cited to ssec:21.6.1 and sec:58.3.
- R-101 and D-029: the chapter owns only the items in architecture.md's Owns note for Chapter 80. R-105 and Annex TR T.13: the Case T beat uses the Ellery Crosstown Light Rail inputs and may bring in Nerida Faulkes.
- Standards review defects 3 (sec:80.5, sec:80.6, sec:80.7, ssec:80.7.2, sec:80.8 retitled; sec:80.2 and sec:80.3 retitled because they duplicated sec:12.6 and sec:12.7), 4 (examples located), 5 (the "412.6" fleet cost in Example 80.4 replaced), 6 (Tier 2 and Tier 3 inputs and answers), 7 (target raised); coverage defect 4 and 5 (airport-concession, port-concession and t-airport-port-revenue placed in named subsections; t-airport-regulation superseded by t-airport-port-revenue).

## 80.1 Header

- Chapter: 80
- Title: Rail, urban transit, airports, and ports
- File: `chapters/80-rail-airports-and-ports.tex`
- Target length: 13,000 to 15,000 words (four subsectors; raised in revision round 1 per standards review defect 7). The target is a floor for the core teaching of each sector, not a cap.
- Dominant lens: a transport infrastructure investor and lender who has worked on transit PPPs, airport concessions and port terminals, with the government PPP official's view on transit and the regulator's view on airports.

## 80.2 Purpose and capability statement

Purpose. Rail, airports and ports all move people or goods, but their revenue comes through other organizations: transit authorities that set fares, airlines that choose hubs, shipping lines that choose ports. This chapter teaches the economics of each subsector, the revenue and regulatory models that make them financeable (availability-paid transit, regulated airport tills and concessions, landlord-port terminal concessions with minimum annual guarantees), the risks that are particular to them (interfaces, supply-chain governance, footloose demand, pandemics), and the record of Metronet, the Purple Line, the pandemic rail contracts, Heathrow's price control, LaGuardia's Terminal B and Lekki Deep Sea Port.

What you will be able to do:
- Compute a light-rail line's farebox recovery and the public cost of an availability-paid line, and a fleet lease rental with residual value (Capabilities 9, 13).
- Set an airport's aeronautical charge under single-till and dual-till regulation and test an airport's cover through a passenger shock (Capabilities 4, 13).
- Analyze a container terminal concession, including the minimum annual guarantee, from the port authority's and the lenders' side (Capabilities 3, 9).
- Use the footloose-demand test to judge whether a transport asset's users can leave, and price that in the structure (Capabilities 2, 15).

## 80.3 Concepts owned and assumed

Owned:
1. Rail and transit economics: farebox recovery (term home sec:12.5, R-026), why urban transit is rarely self-funding, ridership drivers, vertical separation of infrastructure and operations.
2. Transit delivery models at sector level: availability-paid DBFOM light rail (Purple Line), infrastructure maintenance and renewal PPPs with periodic review (London Underground PPP), operating franchises and gross-cost contracts, and their transition under a demand shock (GB rail emergency agreements).
3. Rolling stock finance: fleet leases, residual value risk, lessor models, availability-based train service agreements.
4. Rail-specific risks: utility relocation and third-party interfaces, systems integration (signalling, rolling stock, power), operator interface, supply-chain governance with tied shareholder-contractors.
5. Airport economics: aeronautical and commercial revenue, passenger drivers, hub against origin-destination traffic, airline concentration, slot constraints.
6. Airport economic regulation beyond the till basics of ssec:21.8.1 (R-055): hybrid till, price caps on a regulated asset base with building blocks and traffic risk sharing (RAB mechanics cross-referenced to Chapter 19), concession forms (full privatization, long-term concession, terminal PPP).
7. Port economics applying the Chapter 12 port models (sec:12.7, R-056): terminal concessions; container, bulk and dedicated terminals; shipping-line alliances; transshipment as footloose demand; berth productivity at sector level.
8. Port concession terms applying the minimum annual guarantee owned by ssec:21.8.2 (R-056): fixed and variable concession fees, the MAG of throughput or fees, tariff freedom or caps, exclusivity, expansion obligations, concession horizons and handback, and the landlord-port layering of grantor, infrastructure company and terminal sub-concession.
9. Framework 80.1, the footloose-demand test (original).
10. Demand shocks in transport and how governments respond (state as stabilizer: management-fee contracts, payment continuity, rebalancing) at sector level for rail, airports and ports. (Distress process is Chapter 64.)
11. Modeling specifics: passenger and TEU forecasts at sector level, aero yield and commercial revenue per passenger, MAG and fee mechanics, rail performance regimes (deduction design is Chapter 58), fleet lifecycle.

Assumed:
- Roads, rail, airports and ports at financier depth: Chapter 12.
- Airport and port revenue models as contracts, single and dual till, and the minimum annual guarantee: Chapter 21 (ssec:21.8.1, ssec:21.8.2).
- Farebox recovery, rolling stock, aeronautical revenue, port models, TEU and transshipment as terms: Chapter 12 (sec:12.5 to sec:12.7).
- Competing-facility principle: ssec:17.2.1.
- RAB mechanics: Chapter 19.
- PPP rationale, VfM, fiscal treatment, contingent liabilities: Chapter 57. Procurement, availability payments and deduction regimes, handback, termination compensation: Chapter 58.
- Demand-risk sharing instruments and ramp-up diagnosis: Chapter 79 (Frameworks 79.1 and 79.2).
- Construction structures and supply-chain risk; Carillion as contractor insolvency: Chapter 23 and Chapter 61.
- Traffic and demand diligence: Chapter 48. Ramp-up modeling: Chapter 45.
- Sizing, reserves, covenants: Chapters 36 and 37. Pricing: Chapter 38.
- Distress and restructuring: Chapter 64.

Forward references: none.

## 80.4 Locked table of contents

Opening (no heading). July 2007: the PPP Arbiter allows Metronet GBP 121 million of the GBP 551 million it sought in an extraordinary review; lenders stop funding; shareholders who were also its main contractors decline to inject more; administration on July 18, 2007; the Department for Transport later pays GBP 1.7 billion so that TfL can meet the debt guarantee on at least 95% of Metronet's borrowing. Source: metronet. The puzzle: the public sector guaranteed the debt, so what risk had it transferred?

What you will be able to do (unnumbered).

80.1 Who pays for a train (sec:80.1)
- 80.1.1 Farebox recovery and the public budget (ssec:80.1.1). Example 80.3.
- 80.1.2 Four ways to contract a railway (ssec:80.1.2). Availability-paid DBFOM line; infrastructure maintenance and renewal PPP with periodic review; operating franchise with revenue risk; gross-cost management contract. Exhibit 80.1 (table: who carries construction, ridership, cost and fare risk in each).
- 80.1.3 Interfaces that break rail projects (ssec:80.1.3). Utilities, third parties, systems integration, operator interface; Purple Line's 2023 modification driven by utility relocation (fact sheet).
- 80.1.4 Rolling stock finance (ssec:80.1.4). Example 80.4 (rolling stock is a sec:12.5 term; "rolling stock lease" is owned here); lessor's residual-value risk; availability-based train service agreements in outline.

80.2 Airport economics and regulation (sec:80.2)
- 80.2.1 Two businesses under one roof (ssec:80.2.1). Aeronautical and commercial revenue (aeronautical revenue is a sec:12.6 term); passenger drivers; hub and origin-destination traffic; airline concentration as counterparty risk.
- 80.2.2 Choosing a till and a price cap (ssec:80.2.2). Single and dual till are defined in ssec:21.8.1 (R-055); this subsection owns hybrid till and price-cap regulation. Example 80.1 (single, dual and hybrid till). Equation 80.1 (single-till charge per passenger) and Equation 80.2 (dual-till aeronautical charge), each captioned as a specialized application of the ssec:21.8.1 definitions. Real case from t-airport-port-revenue: Heathrow under the UK CAA's single-till building-block control. The H7 control (January 2022 to December 2026, Final Decision March 9, 2023): a GBP 300 million RAB adjustment for COVID-19 (April 2021), a vanilla WACC point estimate of 3.18% on an RPI-indexed RAB, notional gearing 60% tested against BBB+ metrics, a 2022 to 2026 net revenue requirement of GBP 8,658 million (2020 CPI prices) after deducting GBP 5,159 million of commercial revenues and GBP 92 million of cargo, over 375.5 million forecast passengers (unprofiled average yield GBP 23.06), a flat GBP 21.03 cap in 2020 CPI prices for 2024 to 2026, and a traffic risk-sharing mechanism. The CMA's October 17, 2023 determinations upheld most of the decision and remitted three points. For H8 (2027 to 2031) the CAA's March 2026 Initial Proposals raised the CPIH-real vanilla WACC to 4.87% from an H7 equivalent of 4.06%, mainly through the cost of debt, with a midpoint average charge of about GBP 28.80 per passenger; a 2027 holding cap of GBP 28.658 (2027 prices) was set in September 2026 because the final decision is due in April 2027. Lessons: the till decides whether shops subsidize airlines; financing conditions pass straight into user charges through the allowed WACC; traffic risk sharing is how a regulator keeps a five-year control alive through shocks. Do not state Heathrow's RAB in pounds, its actual gearing, ratings or bond terms, or final H8 parameters.
- 80.2.3 Privatizations, concessions, and terminal PPPs (ssec:80.2.3). Forms and what lenders hold in each. Real case from airport-concession: LaGuardia Airport Central Terminal B, New York. The Port Authority of New York and New Jersey selected LaGuardia Gateway Partners in 2015; financial close and a lease to 2050 (about 35 years) in June 2016; a 35-gate, 1.3 million square foot terminal built while the 1964 terminal (designed for 8 million passengers a year and handling more than 16 million) stayed open; completed in 2022, on time and on budget according to the sponsor. Financing per the sponsor (Vantage, medium confidence): predominantly tax-exempt special facilities revenue bonds rated BBB (Fitch) and Baa3 (Moody's), USD 200 million of equity contributed equally by the partners, and future passenger facility charges; airline consultation on the rate structure credited for the ratings. Lesson: a US terminal PPP is a lease from an airport authority that stays landlord, funded in the municipal bond market, with airline rate agreement as the credit's backbone. Contrast with whole-airport concessions (Rome's ADR, whose concession was extended by two years to 2046 by law after 2020; covid-toll-roads). Do not state the bonds' par, series, coupons, yields or maturities (reported as about USD 2.4 billion), any PANYNJ contribution, the original consortium's composition, COVID-19 effects on Terminal B, or the USD 5.1 billion "project value" as an audited cost.

80.3 Port economics and terminal concessions (sec:80.3)
- 80.3.1 Landlord, tool, and service ports (ssec:80.3.1). The models are defined in sec:12.7 (R-056); here why the landlord model with private terminal concessions became the financeable form. From the World Bank Port Reform Toolkit, 3rd edition, July 2025 (t-airport-port-revenue): the landlord port is "the most common port management model"; the authority provides land and common-user infrastructure and recovers its costs from port dues and concession fees, which are often the bidding parameter; concession horizons of 10 to 30 years (up to 95) for DBFOM or concessions, 10 to 15 (up to 25) for leases, 2 to 5 (up to 10) for management contracts; the private party bears market risk under leases and concessions; listed pitfalls are overcapacity, fragmentation and mistimed capacity additions. Do not cite concession fee levels or minimum guaranteed throughput figures from the Toolkit (it gives none).
- 80.3.2 Terminals and the shipping lines that choose them (ssec:80.3.2). Container against bulk and dedicated terminals; alliances; transshipment hubs as footloose demand (transshipment and TEU are sec:12.7 terms); anchor-line commitments.
- 80.3.3 Concession fees and the minimum annual guarantee (ssec:80.3.3). The MAG is defined in ssec:21.8.2 (R-056); Example 80.2 applies it. Real case from port-concession: Lekki Deep Sea Port, about 60 km east of central Lagos in the Lagos Free Zone, Nigeria. Three layers: the Nigerian Ports Authority as grantor; Lekki Port LFTZ Enterprise Limited as the infrastructure company, owned by China Harbour Engineering Company and Tolaram (through Lekki Port Investment Holdings), Lagos State and the federal government through the NPA, with a 45-year concession per Tolaram; and a container terminal sub-concession first signed in 2012 with an ICTSI-led company (CMA Terminals agreed to take 25% in January 2014) and, under an April 16, 2018 agreement, operated by CMA CGM's terminal company as Lekki Freeport Terminal (1,200 m quay, design throughput 2.7 million TEU a year, 16.5 m depth). China Development Bank signed facility agreements in Lagos in October 2019; commercial operations began in April 2023, about six years after the 2014 expectation of 2017 (computed from the operator's own statements; causes not verified). Lessons: a carrier-linked operator brings volume and transshipment; an EPC contractor that is also a sponsor pairs with policy-bank debt from its home country; public shareholders give the state a stake in the infrastructure company. Do not state the CDB amount (reported as about USD 629 million), tenor or pricing, the total cost, LPLEL shareholdings, the current terminal ownership, or the delay's causes.

80.4 Footloose demand (sec:80.4)
- 80.4.1 The footloose-demand test (ssec:80.4.1). Framework 80.1 applied to a commuter rail line, a hub airport, an origin-destination airport, a gateway container terminal and a transshipment terminal (Exhibit 80.2, Illustrative scoring table).
- 80.4.2 What lenders do with a footloose score (ssec:80.4.2). Sizing discipline, contracted anchor volumes, shorter tenor, liquidity.

80.5 Demand shocks, interfaces, supply chains, and regulatory resets (sec:80.5)
- 80.5.1 Demand shocks (ssec:80.5.1). Example 80.5 (airport through a passenger shock). GB rail's emergency agreements (EMAs from March 23, 2020, fee capped at 2% of the pre-pandemic cost base; ERMAs from September 20, 2020, 1.5% cap) as the state-as-stabilizer response; UK PFI guidance on payment continuity (covid-transport). Airports fell much further than roads: Atlantia reported 2020 passenger falls of 76.8% at Rome's airports and 68.4% at Nice against 24.6% to 30.8% traffic falls on its European motorways; Heathrow Finance obtained a 2021 covenant waiver that bars dividends until its regulatory asset ratio falls below 87.5%, and AGS amended its facility with a dividend block (covid-toll-roads).
- 80.5.2 Construction interfaces and cost growth (ssec:80.5.2). Purple Line contract value from about USD 5.6 billion to about USD 9.8 billion over the term (contract values including O&M and financing; construction-phase cost about USD 2.0 billion originally and about USD 3.4 billion after the 2022 restructuring); utility relocation; court-vacated environmental approval in August 2016.
- 80.5.3 Supply-chain governance (ssec:80.5.3). Metronet's shareholders as its main contractors; NAO finding of poor corporate governance and leadership as the main cause.
- 80.5.4 Regulatory resets and counterparty concentration (ssec:80.5.4). Periodic review (Metronet's seven-and-a-half-year reset and Arbiter); airport price reviews; shipping-line and airline concentration.

80.6 Transit, airport, and port concession contracts (sec:80.6)
- 80.6.1 Contracts for a transit availability PPP (ssec:80.6.1). Payment mechanism and deduction design are cited to sec:58.3 (R-051). Project agreement with performance regime, design-build contract back-to-back, O&M or operator interface, utility agreements; Purple Line's 365-day extended-delay termination right in both the P3 agreement and the design-build contract.
- 80.6.2 Contracts for an airport concession or terminal lease (ssec:80.6.2). Concession agreement or terminal lease (Terminal B's lease to 2050), regulatory framework or charges formula (Heathrow's licence-based maximum yield), airline use and lease agreements and rate consultation, commercial concessions (retail, parking).
- 80.6.3 Contracts for a port terminal concession (ssec:80.6.3). Concession agreement with MAG, tariff provisions, exclusivity (competing-facility principle ssec:17.2.1), expansion triggers; the landlord-port layering of concession and sub-concession (Lekki); terminal services agreements with shipping lines; Clause 80.1 (minimum annual guarantee, port terminal concession agreement) with variants a (sponsor), b (lender), c (port authority) and a landing paragraph stated as practitioner observation (D-011 style, reviewer sign-off).

80.7 How rail, airport, and port assets have been financed (sec:80.7)
- 80.7.1 Financing terms at Metronet, the Purple Line, Terminal B, and Lekki (ssec:80.7.1). Metronet's 95% public debt guarantee and the GBP 1.7 billion payment; Purple Line's 2016 USD 313 million of PABs (do not name the issuer) and the planned TIFIA and PAB amounts only as planned; LaGuardia Terminal B's tax-exempt special facilities revenue bonds (BBB and Baa3) with USD 200 million of sponsor equity (sponsor source, medium confidence); Lekki's CDB facility agreements of October 2019 (no amount); Heathrow's notional 60% gearing in the regulator's financeability test (actual gearing higher and not stated). Exhibit 80.4 (new, appended): the verified terms table. t-market-norms states there are no verified 2023 to 2026 norms for rail, airports or ports, and that is printed as the finding.
- 80.7.2 Gearing and cover ranges for transit, airports, and terminals, 2015 to 2025 (ssec:80.7.2). D-011 indicative only, each labeled with market and period and requiring reviewer sign-off: availability-paid transit PPPs in North America and Europe, 2015 to 2025, gearing roughly 80% to 90% with minimum DSCRs of roughly 1.15x to 1.25x; regulated or concession airports with traffic history, 2015 to 2025, senior DSCR covenants and leverage tests tied to EBITDA rather than sculpted cover (no number given unless the reviewer supplies and signs one); container terminals, 2015 to 2025, sized at higher cover than availability assets, with anchor-line contracts driving the bankable volume. Drivers ranked: demand risk retained by the state or not; footloose score; regulatory regime; sponsor and anchor-user credit.
- 80.7.3 Public credit, airport bonds, and ring-fenced terminal debt (ssec:80.7.3). Public subordinated credit for transit; conduit revenue bonds for US terminal leases (Terminal B) and bond refinancing of regulated airports; corporate-style debt at port groups with ring-fenced terminal financing; policy-bank debt where the EPC contractor is a sponsor (Lekki).

80.8 Modeling passengers, throughput, and rail performance (sec:80.8)
- 80.8.1 Passengers, yield, and commercial revenue (ssec:80.8.1). Aero yield per passenger, commercial revenue per passenger, elasticity of passenger demand to charges at sector level; cross-reference Chapter 45 for ramp-up mechanics.
- 80.8.2 Throughput, MAG, and fees (ssec:80.8.2). TEU forecast, berth capacity cap, MAG billing as max(actual, MAG); escalation.
- 80.8.3 Rail performance regimes and fleet lifecycle (ssec:80.8.3). Performance deductions by service unit (cross-reference Chapter 58), fleet overhaul cycles, mid-life refurbishment reserve.

80.9 Metronet and Tube Lines, same contract and different supply chains (sec:80.9)
Real case from metronet. Lesson: an availability-style PPP with periodic review and an independent arbiter moves disputes into "economic and efficient" cost; a supply chain owned by the shareholders defeats the cost discipline the structure assumes; a 95% public guarantee means the public sector paid for transfer it did not receive (NAO's direct loss estimate of GBP 170 million to GBP 410 million). Do not state monthly payment, debt quantum or contract values beyond NAO's present value of GBP 15.7 billion for the three contracts.

80.10 The Purple Line and the 365-day exit (sec:80.10)
Real case from purple-line. Sequence: 2016 award; August 2016 court vacatur; delays and claims; contractor notice May 2020; PLTP termination notice June 2020; settlement December 2020 (USD 250 million from MDOT MTA to PLTP; Fluor exits); new contractor approved January 2022 (Maryland Transit Solutions, Dragados USA and OHLA USA); restructured agreement worth USD 9.3 billion, later about USD 9.8 billion; revenue service expected winter 2027. Lesson: an extended-delay termination right gives a solvent contractor an exit; restructuring rather than terminating kept the concession; availability payments kept ridership risk with the state while interfaces drove cost. Do not attribute fault.

80.11 Rail contracts in the pandemic and the end of revenue risk (sec:80.11)
Real case from covid-transport: EMAs, ERMAs and the move to National Rail Contracts; what a lender to a demand-risk rail, airport or port concession should take from it (grantor's track record and fiscal capacity, not just the force majeure clause). Do not state a total support figure beyond the NAO's GBP 4.1 billion for March to September 2020 and its GBP 4 billion to GBP 5 billion estimate for the rest of 2020 to 2021.

80.12 Walkthrough: reading a container terminal concession agreement (sec:80.12)
Artifact: an Illustrative term sheet of a 30-year container terminal concession (Exhibit 80.3). Steps under Walkthroughs.

80.13 Case T: Brannock procures the Ellery Crosstown Light Rail (sec:80.13)
Small installment.

80.14 Practitioner's notebook (sec:80.14). 80.15 Judgment drill (sec:80.15).

80.16 Demand that belongs to someone else's network (sec:80.16)
Close. Crystallizes footloose demand, regulation and the state's role in shocks. Final paragraph poses the next problem: some infrastructure has no user demand at all, because the state pays for a hospital being available or a utility pays for water delivered; where does the risk go when the payment is certain, and what can still go wrong (Chapter 81).

80.17 Exercises (sec:80.17). 80.18 Solutions to exercises (sec:80.18). Sources.

## 80.5 Opening

The Metronet administration of July 18, 2007, told from the lenders' seat (they were protected by the guarantee) and the shareholder-contractors' seat (they would not fund more), with the Arbiter's GBP 121 million against GBP 551 million as the moment. Ends on the question of what risk transfer means when debt is guaranteed.

## 80.6 Worked examples

Example 80.1. Single, dual, and hybrid till (Illustrative). Setting: Aeropuerto del Pacífico Sur SA (fictional), a concessioned airport in Peru whose charges are regulated in US dollars by a fictional transport regulator, at its 2025 price review. Regulated asset base USD 2,384.7 million, real pre-tax WACC 5.85%, regulatory depreciation USD 118.4 million, operating costs USD 241.6 million, commercial revenue USD 286.3 million, 31.2 million passengers. Single till (definition ssec:21.8.1): allowed revenue = 2,384.7 x 5.85% + 118.4 + 241.6 = USD 499.5 million (return USD 139.5 million); aeronautical charge = (499.5 - 286.3)/31.2 = USD 6.83 per passenger. Dual till: aeronautical RAB 82% of total; aeronautical depreciation USD 97.1 million; aeronautical opex USD 179.2 million; aeronautical revenue requirement = 0.82 x 139.5 + 97.1 + 179.2 = USD 390.7 million; charge USD 12.52 per passenger. The airport keeps commercial profit of USD 177.5 million under dual till (286.3 less commercial share of return 25.1, commercial depreciation 21.3 and commercial opex 62.4). Hybrid till (owned here, R-055): if 50% of that commercial profit offsets aeronautical charges, the charge is (390.7 - 0.5 x 177.5)/31.2 = USD 9.68 per passenger. Teaching point: the till decides whether shops subsidize airlines; airlines lobby for single till, investors for dual till, and a hybrid splits the difference; lenders care about the stability of the regime more than its choice (Heathrow's single till is the real case in ssec:80.2.2). Verified in Python (revision round 1).

Example 80.2. A container terminal's minimum annual guarantee (Illustrative). Setting: Mekong Gateway Terminal JSC (fictional) holds a 2024 concession for a container terminal at a fictional landlord port in the Cai Mep area of southern Vietnam; its lenders test the 2026 budget. Concession fee: fixed USD 18.6 million a year plus USD 14.20 per TEU, with a MAG of 850,000 TEU billed if throughput falls short (MAG defined in ssec:21.8.2). Terminal tariff USD 182.00 per TEU; variable opex USD 74.50 per TEU; fixed opex USD 46.8 million. At 1,210,000 TEU: fees USD 35.78 million; revenue USD 220.22 million; EBITDA USD 47.49 million (21.6% margin). At 905,000 TEU: fees USD 31.45 million; revenue USD 164.71 million; EBITDA USD 19.04 million. At 620,000 TEU (an alliance moves its transshipment calls): MAG binds, fees USD 30.67 million against USD 27.40 million without the MAG; revenue USD 112.84 million; EBITDA (10.82) million. Teaching point: the MAG transfers volume risk to the operator and makes the downside steeper; lenders ask who brings the volume (anchor-line contracts) before they look at the MAG.

Example 80.3. Farebox recovery of a light-rail line (Illustrative). Setting: the Riverside Line (fictional), an availability-paid DBFOM light-rail line in Sacramento, California, operated for a fictional regional transit authority, 2025. 61,400 boardings a day, 350 effective days, average fare USD 1.85: fare revenue USD 39.76 million. Operating cost USD 64.3 million: farebox recovery 61.8%. Availability payment to the concessionaire USD 112.7 million a year (capital, maintenance and operations). Net cost to the authority after fares: USD 72.94 million, or USD 3.39 per boarding. Teaching point: a transit line's credit is the authority's budget, not the farebox; lenders analyze appropriation risk and the payment mechanism, not ridership.

Example 80.4. Leasing a fleet with a residual value (Illustrative). Setting: Pennine Rail Leasing Ltd (fictional), a UK rolling stock lessor, leases a fleet of electric multiple units to a train operator under a fictional 2025 rolling stock lease in England; amounts in GBP. Fleet cost GBP 438.9 million (replaces the version-1.0 figure); 25-year lease; lessor's rate 6.1%; expected residual value 15% of cost at year 25 (useful life 35 years): GBP 65.84 million. PV of residual = 65.84/(1.061^25) = GBP 14.98 million. Annual rental (in arrears) = (438.9 - 14.98)/annuity factor 12.663 = GBP 33.48 million; without a residual assumption GBP 34.66 million. Teaching point: the lessor's residual assumption lowers the rent by GBP 1.18 million a year and puts technology and re-lease risk on the lessor; a lender to the lessor tests whether trains of that type can be re-leased. Recomputed in Python (revision round 1).

Example 80.5. An airport through a passenger shock (Illustrative). Setting: Aeropuerto del Este SA (fictional), a leisure-traffic concession airport in the Dominican Republic with dollar charges, testing its 2019 budget against the 2020 pandemic in April 2020. 18.4 million passengers; aeronautical revenue USD 14.60 and commercial USD 11.85 per passenger; variable cost USD 4.10 per passenger; fixed costs USD 168.3 million; debt service USD 112.6 million. Base: revenue USD 486.7 million; CFADS USD 242.9 million; DSCR 2.16x. At 38% of base traffic: CFADS (12.0) million; debt service unpaid without liquidity. At 71% of base: CFADS USD 123.7 million; DSCR 1.10x. Breakeven traffic for 1.00x: 12.57 million passengers, 68.3% of base. Teaching point: airports with high fixed costs need liquidity (DSRA, revolver, covenant holidays) more than high base cover; tie to the pandemic responses in ssec:80.5.1 (Rome's airports lost 76.8% of passengers in 2020).

## 80.7 Walkthrough

Walkthrough: reading a container terminal concession agreement (Section 80.12). Exhibit 80.3 is an Illustrative term sheet for a 30-year concession.
1. Identify the port model and what the concessionaire builds, owns and hands back.
2. Read the fee structure (fixed, variable, MAG) and run Example 80.2's three volumes.
3. Read the tariff clause: freedom, cap, regulator; and exclusivity and competing-terminal provisions (link to Chapter 79's competing-facility logic).
4. Read the expansion triggers (berth occupancy, throughput) and who pays.
5. Read the anchor-line terminal services agreement: volumes, take-or-pay, duration against debt tenor, termination rights.
6. Read the termination and handback provisions and lenders' step-in (cross-reference Chapters 28 and 58).
7. Score the terminal on Framework 80.1 and write the lenders' summary of bankable volume.

## 80.8 Frameworks

Framework 80.1, the footloose-demand test (original; label fw:footloose-demand). Purpose: judge how easily a transport asset's users can take their demand elsewhere. Steps: (1) identify the decision-maker for demand (passenger, airline, shipping line, authority); (2) identify the alternatives and the switching cost and time; (3) identify contractual locks (anchor-line agreements, airline use agreements, concession exclusivity); (4) score as captive, contestable or footloose; (5) link the score to structure (availability or regulation for captive; contracted anchor volumes and higher cover for contestable; short tenor, high cover or no project debt for footloose). Demonstrated in Exhibit 80.2 and the walkthrough.

## 80.9 Real cases and fact sheets

- metronet: governance and tied supply chains in a rail PPP with a public debt guarantee (sec:80.9, opening, ssec:80.5.3).
- purple-line: availability transit PPP, interfaces, extended-delay termination, restructuring (sec:80.10, ssec:80.5.2, ssec:80.6.1).
- covid-transport: GB rail EMAs and ERMAs; UK PFI guidance (sec:80.11, ssec:80.5.1).
- eurotunnel: one paragraph in ssec:80.1.2 as the fully private, revenue-risk rail link, cross-referencing Chapter 79.
- t-market-norms: absence of verified norms for these subsectors (ssec:80.7.1).
- t-airport-port-revenue (placed in revision round 1; supersedes the request t-airport-regulation): Heathrow H7 and H8 in ssec:80.2.2; the World Bank Port Reform Toolkit in ssec:80.3.1. Lesson in one line: an airport's charges are a regulated building block that passes financing costs to users, while a port terminal concessionaire bears market risk and pays the landlord.
- airport-concession (LaGuardia Central Terminal B; placed in revision round 1): ssec:80.2.3, ssec:80.6.2, ssec:80.7.1 and ssec:80.7.3. Lesson in one line: a terminal PPP lease funded with municipal conduit bonds relies on airline rate agreement and construction phasing around a live terminal.
- port-concession (Lekki Deep Sea Port; placed in revision round 1): ssec:80.3.3, ssec:80.6.3, ssec:80.7.1 and ssec:80.7.3. Lesson in one line: the landlord port stacks grantor, infrastructure company and terminal operator, and each layer brings different volume and credit.
- covid-toll-roads: airport traffic in 2020 and the Heathrow and AGS dividend blocks (ssec:80.5.1); ADR's two-year extension (ssec:80.2.3).

Open requests (the chapter can be taught without them):
- fact sheet requested: rolling-stock (optional): the UK Intercity Express Programme or a ROSCO, for availability-based train financing; until it exists ssec:80.1.4 uses Example 80.4 only.
- The version-1.0 requests airport-concession and port-concession were delivered; t-airport-regulation is superseded by t-airport-port-revenue.

## 80.10 Running-case installment

Case T (small), story date 2024, Port Ellery; place and month "Brannock Infrastructure Advisory Board, Port Ellery, March 2024" (expressions of interest open that month; writer may choose a month in 2024 up to August and reports it). Characters: Margaret (Maggie) Dunleavy, Commissioner of the independent Brannock Infrastructure Advisory Board since 2020 (Case Bible 4.2); she is no longer Director of Partnerships Brannock, and the scene must not show her running the procurement. Nerida Faulkes, Director of Partnerships Brannock from July 2020 (Annex TR T.13), presents the line (optional but recommended); her verbal habit is "Show me the payment mechanism." Inputs (Annex TR T.13, book inputs): the Ellery Crosstown Light Rail, a 16.2 km line with 21 stops across Port Ellery's inner suburbs; availability-based DBFOM including rolling stock; the state keeps fare revenue and demand risk; estimated capital cost ARD 3.1 billion in 2024 prices; expressions of interest March 2024; three consortia shortlisted September 2024; RFP planned for 2025.

Scene outline: Maggie, months after giving evidence to the parliamentary inquiry (2023), reviews the business case for the Ellery Crosstown Light Rail that Nerida's Partnerships Brannock proposes to procure as an availability PPP. She presses on what the Merrick Link taught (demand risk sat with lenders and equity, as designed; the PSC had shared the bidders' optimism) and asks whether ridership forecasts still matter if the state keeps fare risk. Her verbal habit ("What does the standard form say?") appears once; Nerida's answer turns on the payment mechanism. The narration after the box teaches: under availability payment the ridership forecast moves from the lenders' credit to the state's affordability analysis (Chapter 57) and the farebox recovery (Example 80.3); the interface risks the Purple Line exposed remain with whoever holds construction risk; including rolling stock brings fleet lifecycle (Example 80.4) into the payment mechanism. Figures displayed: Case Bible inputs only (length, stops, ARD 3.1 billion estimate, timetable); no ledger figures. State at end: a recommendation to Cabinet, outcome not told.

## 80.11 Practitioner's notebook (outline)

Checklist: who sets fares and charges; demand decision-maker and alternatives (Framework 80.1); utilities and third-party interface register; systems integration responsibility; operator interface agreement; anchor-user contracts and their tenor against the debt; regulatory regime and review cycle; liquidity for demand shocks; supply-chain governance where shareholders are contractors.

Red flags: shareholders who are also the main contractors with no independent cost control; an extended-delay termination right without owner-delay carve-outs; a transshipment terminal financed on throughput without line commitments; airport debt sized on peak-year passengers; periodic reviews without clear "efficient cost" tests.

Rules of thumb with limits: transit is a budget credit (limit: where fare revenue is pledged); captive gateway ports are more bankable than transshipment hubs (limit: alliance contracts with take-or-pay); high fixed-cost airports need liquidity sized for a severe shock.

Common mistakes: treating ridership as the transit lender's risk under availability payment; modeling MAG as revenue rather than cost; ignoring commercial revenue's dependence on passenger mix; equating a public guarantee with risk transfer.

Questions experts ask: Who chooses whether this demand comes here? How long does it take them to leave? What does the state do when a shock hits, and what did it do last time? Which interface is not in anyone's contract? Who owns the supply chain?

## 80.12 Judgment drill

The situation: you are a lender asked in May 2026 to refinance Bahía Azul Container Terminal SA (fictional), a transshipment-heavy terminal at a landlord port on Panama's Pacific coast (Illustrative), with 12 years of throughput history; 46% of volume comes from one alliance whose terminal services agreement expires in three years; the sponsor wants debt sized on the 12-year average with a 15-year tenor. Credit committee in two weeks.

Reasoning outline: score the terminal on Framework 80.1 (transshipment share and alliance concentration make it contestable or footloose); the history proves the alliance chose the terminal, not that it will stay; the expert sizes bankable volume on gateway cargo plus contracted alliance volume, adds a cash sweep and a tenor step-down if the alliance agreement is not renewed by a set date, or requires renewal before close; considers MAG exposure in the downside (Example 80.2).

What would change the answer: an alliance shareholding in the terminal or a renewal with take-or-pay to beyond the debt tenor (raises bankable volume); no competing terminal within the region (moves toward captive); port authority willingness to adjust the MAG in a downturn (reduces downside).

## 80.13 Exercise set

Tier 1 (4):
- 80.1: Explain farebox recovery and why it is not the lender's main risk under availability payment.
- 80.2: Compare single till, dual till and hybrid till for an airport investor, an airline and a lender, using Heathrow's H7 control as the single-till instance.
- 80.3: Score three assets with the footloose-demand test and justify each score.
- 80.4: Explain what the Metronet guarantee meant for risk transfer.

Tier 2 (5; all inputs and expected answers below, computed in Python):
- 80.5: Aeroporto do Planalto SA (fictional), a concession airport in Brazil regulated in USD-equivalent terms for the exercise, 2025 review. Inputs: RAB USD 1,147.3 million; real pre-tax WACC 6.10%; regulatory depreciation USD 63.8 million; operating costs USD 118.9 million; commercial revenue USD 131.6 million; 14.7 million passengers; aeronautical shares: RAB 78%, depreciation USD 51.2 million, opex USD 87.4 million; hybrid till offsets 50% of commercial profit. Compute the single-till, dual-till and hybrid-till charges. Answers: return USD 69.99 million; single-till allowed revenue USD 252.69 million and charge USD 8.24 per passenger; dual-till aeronautical requirement USD 193.19 million and charge USD 13.14; commercial profit under dual till USD 72.10 million; hybrid charge USD 10.69.
- 80.6: Puerto Caldera Sur Terminal SA (fictional), Costa Rica, 2025. Inputs: concession fee fixed USD 9.4 million plus USD 11.60 per TEU with a MAG of 480,000 TEU; tariff USD 168 per TEU; variable opex USD 69.30 per TEU; fixed opex USD 27.5 million. Compute fees and EBITDA at 690,000, 530,000, 455,000 and 340,000 TEU. Answers: fees USD 17.40, 15.55, 14.97 and 14.97 million; revenue USD 115.92, 89.04, 76.44 and 57.12 million; EBITDA USD 23.20, 9.26, 2.44 and (8.91) million.
- 80.7: Clyde Valley Rolling Stock Ltd (fictional), Scotland, 2025: fleet cost GBP 286.4 million; 20-year lease; lessor's rate 5.85%; residual value 18% of cost at year 20. Compute the annual rental in arrears with the residual, with no residual, and if the residual falls to 5%. Answers: GBP 23.24 million; GBP 24.67 million; GBP 24.27 million.
- 80.8: Aeropuerto Costa Brava Norte SA (fictional), a leisure airport concession in Mexico with dollar charges, 2020: base 9.6 million passengers; aeronautical USD 12.30 and commercial USD 9.45 per passenger; variable cost USD 3.70 per passenger; fixed costs USD 96.4 million; debt service USD 61.8 million. Compute breakeven traffic at 1.00x and the liquidity needed in a two-year shock with traffic at 42% then 74% of base, in months of debt service. Answers: breakeven 8.76 million passengers (91.3% of base); CFADS (23.6) and 31.8 USD million; shortfalls against debt service USD 85.4 and 30.0 million; total USD 115.4 million, 22.4 months of debt service.
- 80.9: Draft the port-authority-friendly variant of a MAG clause and annotate each paragraph.

Tier 3 (4):
- 80.10: Model task (blank workbook, 10 annual periods): Selat Harbour Container Terminal Sdn Bhd (fictional), Malaysia, 2026 to 2035. Inputs: 410,000 TEU in year 1 growing 6.5% a year, capacity cap 620,000 TEU; tariff USD 171 per TEU, variable opex USD 70.20 per TEU and fixed opex USD 26.3 million, all escalating 2.5% a year from year 2; concession fee fixed USD 8.9 million plus USD 12.40 per TEU (not escalated) with a MAG of 450,000 TEU; alliance-loss scenario: volume falls 38% from year 6. Answers (EBITDA, USD million): base year 1 0.5, year 5 14.2, year 6 18.4, year 8 26.4 (cap reached), year 10 28.6; scenario year 6 (4.5), year 7 (1.6), year 8 0.3, year 10 1.1, with the MAG binding from year 6.
- 80.11: Case analysis: Purple Line. Write a memo for a state PPP unit on how to draft an extended-delay termination right after the 2020 events.
- 80.12: Case analysis: compare the EMA approach with the UK PFI payment-continuity approach and the Heathrow traffic risk-sharing mechanism, from a lender's view.
- 80.13: Case T extension: list the questions Maggie and Nerida should ask about the Ellery Crosstown business case, linking each to Chapters 57, 58 and 80.

## 80.15 Glossary entries owned

Per the glossary canon: farebox recovery ratio (sec:12.5), aeronautical revenue (sec:12.6), landlord port, twenty-foot equivalent unit and transshipment (sec:12.7) (R-026); single till and dual till (ssec:21.8.1, R-055); minimum annual guarantee (ssec:21.8.2, R-056) are homed elsewhere and are cross-referenced, not owned. Terms owned here:

| Term | Abbreviation | Definition | Home |
|---|---|---|---|
| vertical separation | – | The division of a railway into separate infrastructure and train-operating businesses. | ssec:80.1.2 |
| gross-cost contract | – | A transit operating contract in which the authority keeps fare revenue and pays the operator its costs plus a fee. | ssec:80.1.2 |
| rolling stock lease | – | A lease of trains from a lessor that owns them, with rentals set over a term shorter than the trains' life. | ssec:80.1.4 |
| commercial revenue (airport) | – | An airport's revenue from retail, food, parking, property and other non-aeronautical sources. | ssec:80.2.1 |
| hybrid till | – | Airport regulation that counts a stated share of commercial profit toward the allowed revenue, between single till and dual till. | ssec:80.2.2 (new; central change: add to the canon, R-055) |
| footloose-demand test | – | Framework 80.1: a scoring of how easily a transport asset's demand decision-makers can move elsewhere. | ssec:80.4.1 |
| anchor-line agreement | – | A terminal services agreement under which a shipping line commits volumes to a terminal, often in return for tariffs or berthing priority. | ssec:80.6.3 |
