# Chapter 81. Social infrastructure, water, desalination, and waste-to-energy

Rulings applied:
- R-113 and D-028: title "Social infrastructure, water, desalination, and waste-to-energy" (serial comma).
- R-051: availability payment, unitary charge and availability deduction are owned by Chapter 21 (ssec:21.6.1); payment mechanism and deduction design by Chapter 58 (sec:58.3, fw:deduction-calibration, eq:58.2). This chapter applies them to social infrastructure; "unitary charge" is never abbreviated.
- R-026: hard and soft facilities management and lifecycle cost (sec:12.8), non-revenue water and reverse osmosis (sec:12.9) are Chapter 12 terms used here without bold; "hard FM" and "soft FM" appear only as compounds (R-114).
- R-067: IWPP is owned by Chapter 69 (ssec:69.3.2) and cited; "independent water project" (IWP) is owned here.
- R-001: levelized water cost cites ssec:5.3.3 (eq:5.11); Example 81.2 builds a tariff, not a new levelization method.
- R-101 and D-029: the chapter owns only the items in architecture.md's Owns note for Chapter 81. R-105 and Annex TR T.14: the Case T beat uses the Port Ellery Northern Hospital inputs.
- Standards review defects 3 (sec:81.6, sec:81.7, ssec:81.7.1, ssec:81.7.2, sec:81.8 retitled; ssec:81.1.2 "Why" heading made declarative), 4 (examples located), 5 (the "412.6" production figure in Example 81.4 and the 412.0 contract price in Example 81.5 replaced), 6 (Tier 2 and Tier 3 inputs and answers), 7 (target raised); coverage defect 4 (wte, municipal-water-concession placed; WtE financing terms recorded as D-011 indicative with Willows as the landmark failure) and 5 (t-market-norms-2 PPP evidence replaces the request t-ppp-norms).

## 81.1 Header

- Chapter: 81
- Title: Social infrastructure, water, desalination, and waste-to-energy
- File: `chapters/81-social-infrastructure-water-and-waste.tex`
- Target length: 13,000 to 15,000 words (four subsectors; raised in revision round 1 per standards review defect 7). The target is a floor for the core teaching of each sector, not a cap.
- Dominant lens: a PPP investor and lender in availability-based social infrastructure and utilities, with the government PPP official's and the water regulator's views.

## 81.2 Purpose and capability statement

Purpose. In these sectors the payer is usually a public body or a utility, not a market: a health authority pays for a hospital being available, a single buyer pays for desalinated water, a municipality pays a gate fee for waste. That makes the revenue look safe and the debt look cheap, which is why gearing is high and cover thin. This chapter teaches where the risk goes when payment is near-certain: into construction and contractor credit (Carillion), into lifecycle and service performance, into the payer's own collection chain (Tideway and Thames Water), into feedstock quality, quantity and planning consent (waste, Norfolk's Willows plant), and into tariffs and currencies that a public must be able to afford (water, Manila). It covers four subsectors and the record of Carillion's hospitals, Tideway, Manila's two concessions, England's waste PFIs and the Gulf's desalination tenders.

What you will be able to do:
- Explain why availability-based social infrastructure supports high gearing and thin cover, and test a hospital's cover against a deduction (Capabilities 5, 9).
- Build a desalination tariff from capacity and output charges and compare it with real Gulf tariffs (Capabilities 4, 13).
- Analyze a waste-to-energy plant's revenue stack and its sensitivity to waste quantity and calorific value (Capabilities 2, 13).
- Trace who pays when a social-infrastructure contractor fails mid-construction (Capabilities 10, 11).

## 81.3 Concepts owned and assumed

Owned:
1. Social infrastructure economics: what the state buys (accommodation and services, not outcomes); hard FM, soft FM, lifecycle and equipment (terms at sec:12.8, R-026); retained clinical or core services; why availability credit attracts high gearing and thin cover; thin equity and its consequence in contractor failure.
2. Contractor dependence in availability PPPs: the single fixed-price contractor, latent defects on part-built works, cost-to-complete premiums, public refusal to rescue.
3. Water and wastewater sector models: municipal concessions and leases (affermage), BOT treatment plants with water purchase agreements, regulated utilities with RAB (mechanics in Chapter 19), carve-outs such as Tideway.
4. Water tariffs, affordability and collection; non-revenue water (term at sec:12.9) and its value; currency mismatch in water concessions and tariff pass-through formulas (Manila).
5. Desalination at financier depth: reverse osmosis (term at sec:12.9) against thermal (MSF, MED), energy intensity, membranes and their replacement, intake and outfall, capacity charge and output charge structure in water purchase agreements, decoupling of water from power.
6. Waste-to-energy at financier depth: moving-grate combustion, gate fees, put-or-pay waste supply, net calorific value and thermal capacity, electricity and heat sales, residues (bottom ash, air-pollution-control residues), recyclate revenue, and pre-construction planning risk with its termination cost (Norfolk's Willows plant).
7. Framework 81.1, the payment-chain trace (original): from the end-payer to the project company, each link's credit and collection risk.
8. Sector financing terms, verified and indicative.
9. Modeling specifics: lifecycle profiles and reserves, deduction passdown to subcontractors, availability and output volumes for water, NCV-throughput relationship for waste, membrane and energy cost pass-through.

Assumed:
- Hospitals and water plants at financier depth: Chapter 12.
- Availability payments, unitary charge and availability deduction as contract forms: Chapter 21 (ssec:21.6.1). PPP payment mechanisms and deduction regimes (sec:58.3), handback, benchmarking and market testing, termination compensation, refinancing gain sharing: Chapter 58 (R-051).
- Hard and soft FM, lifecycle cost, non-revenue water and reverse osmosis as terms: Chapter 12 (sec:12.8, sec:12.9). Levelized water cost: ssec:5.3.3 (R-001). IWPP: ssec:69.3.2 (R-067).
- PPP rationale, VfM, UK PFI history and end: Chapter 57.
- RAB model and Tideway's construction-phase RAB: Chapter 19.
- PPA capacity and energy payments: Chapter 18 (the water purchase agreement mirrors it). Gulf IWPPs as thermal plants: Chapter 69.
- O&M and FM contracts, incentive design: Chapter 24.
- Construction structures and joint-and-several JV obligations; Carillion and AWPR: Chapter 23. Contractor distress in construction: Chapter 61.
- Reserves (lifecycle reserve as a form of MMRA): Chapter 37.
- Electricity markets for WtE power sales: Chapter 11.
- E&S standards for waste and water: Chapter 50.
- Toll-road demand-risk menu (contrast with availability): Chapter 79.

Forward references: Chapter 84 for green and blue bond labels used by Tideway (one sentence in 81.10).

## 81.4 Locked table of contents

Opening (no heading). January 15, 2018: Carillion plc enters compulsory liquidation with liabilities of nearly GBP 7 billion and GBP 29 million of cash. Two hospital project companies, Royal Liverpool and Midland Metropolitan, lose their fixed-price builder mid-construction; neither can fund completion; the government refuses a bailout and terminates both PFI contracts in 2018, pays GBP 42 million to the Liverpool lenders and nothing to the Midland lenders, and finishes both hospitals with public money years late. Source: carillion. The puzzle: the payment was as safe as a government's promise; how did lenders still lose?

What you will be able to do (unnumbered).

81.1 What the state buys when it buys a building (sec:81.1)
- 81.1.1 Accommodation and services, not outcomes (ssec:81.1.1). Hospitals, schools, prisons, courts, government offices; what is in the project company's scope and what the authority keeps (clinical services).
- 81.1.2 High gearing and thin cover on availability credit (ssec:81.1.2). Example 81.1. Evidence: UK PFI deals were financed about 90% senior debt and 10% equity over 25 to 30 years, and PF2, launched at 75:25, reverted to 90:10 in practice (NAO, January 18, 2018); Morningstar DBRS cites projected minimum DSCRs of 1.20x to 1.44x for rated Canadian availability PPPs in 2026 releases and calls 1.20x standard for the "A" range (t-market-norms-2; one agency's description, not a rating rule).
- 81.1.3 Hard FM, soft FM, lifecycle, and equipment (ssec:81.1.3). Who bears which costs; market testing and benchmarking of soft FM (cross-reference Chapter 58); medical equipment refresh as a technology risk.
- 81.1.4 The thin-equity problem (ssec:81.1.4). When the builder fails, the project company has no second balance sheet; joint-and-several JVs (AWPR) against single contractors (the hospitals).

81.2 Water and wastewater concessions and carve-outs (sec:81.2)
- 81.2.1 Concessions, treatment-plant BOTs, and regulated utilities (ssec:81.2.1). Exhibit 81.1 (table: who carries tariff, volume, collection and construction risk in each model).
- 81.2.2 Tariffs, affordability, collection, and non-revenue water (ssec:81.2.2). Example 81.4. Real case from municipal-water-concession: Metro Manila's two 1997 concessions, the currency-mismatch water concession. With IFC as adviser, MWSS's service area was split into two 25-year concessions awarded on the lowest average tariff bid (no bidder could win both); tariffs initially fell 74% in the East Zone (Manila Water: Ayala with International Water) and 43% in the West Zone (Maynilad: Benpres with Lyonnaise des Eaux); operations began in August 1997 as the Asian financial crisis started. Manila Water cut non-revenue water from 63% (1997) to 29.9% (2006), was profitable from 1999 and listed in 2005. Maynilad had about 90% of its concession fees in foreign currency plus large FX borrowings; after the peso's devaluation it suspended fees and loan payments in March 2001, served a termination notice in February 2003 and sought rehabilitation in November 2003; non-revenue water rose from 61% (1998) to 69% (2004); MWSS drew the full USD 120 million performance bond after a Supreme Court decision; a court-approved debt and capital restructuring agreement of 2005 converted unpaid fees into an 84% MWSS stake. Tariffs carried FX pass-through charges (CERA and FCDA). In December 2021 Republic Acts 11600 and 11601 gave both companies 25-year franchises conditional on listing at least 30% of their shares within five years; Maynilad listed on November 7, 2025. Lessons: same city, contract and regulator, different FX exposure and outcome; tariff pass-through is only as good as the regulator's willingness to approve it in a crisis. Do not state the price or buyers in the sale of MWSS's Maynilad stake, the 2019 arbitration or contract-review details, post-2007 NRW or tariffs, Manila Water's float compliance, or Aguas Argentinas facts.
- 81.2.3 Carving a project out of a utility (ssec:81.2.3). Tideway: separately licensed infrastructure provider (Bazalgette Tunnel Limited), revenue billed and collected by Thames Water from its wastewater customers, bid WACC 2.497% (real, RPI-linked), Government Support Package for tail risks, NAO's reasonable-worst-case exposure of GBP 6.6 billion; cost estimate at completion GBP 4.7 billion against a GBP 3.5 billion regulatory baseline. Counterparty and collection risk from the incumbent's later distress (no restructuring outcomes stated).

81.3 Desalination plants and water purchase agreements (sec:81.3)
- 81.3.1 Reverse osmosis, thermal plants, and energy (ssec:81.3.1). Energy intensity as the main variable cost; membrane life; intake and outfall; why RO displaced thermal co-production.
- 81.3.2 The water purchase agreement (ssec:81.3.2). Capacity charge for availability, output charge for volume, pass-through of energy; mirrors the Chapter 18 PPA; Example 81.2.
- 81.3.3 Decoupling water from power (ssec:81.3.3). Gulf procurers' shift from IWPPs (term at ssec:69.3.2, R-067) to stand-alone RO IWPs and solar IPPs; Exhibit 81.2 (Sharakat tariff series from gulf-iwpp: Shuaibah Expansion 2009 SAR 3.71/m3 down to Jubail 3A SAR 1.55/m3 and Rabigh 4 SAR 1.7162/m3, with capacity, term and cost; USD conversions at the 3.75 peg labeled computed).

81.4 Waste-to-energy economics (sec:81.4)
- 81.4.1 Three revenue lines and two cost lines (ssec:81.4.1). Gate fees, electricity (and heat), recyclates; residue disposal and emissions control. Example 81.3.
- 81.4.2 Waste supply and calorific value (ssec:81.4.2). Put-or-pay contracts with municipalities; composition change as recycling rises; thermal capacity limits tonnage when waste gets "better".
- 81.4.3 Technology, emissions, and planning consent (ssec:81.4.3). Moving-grate as the bankable reference technology; emissions limits; bottom ash and air-pollution-control residues; why gasification and pyrolysis plants struggled to raise project debt (principle only; the wte sheet covers no gasification failure). Real case from wte, the sector's landmark failure: England's waste PFI program and Norfolk's Willows plant. Defra's Waste Infrastructure Delivery Programme (July 2006) part-funded 25-year local-authority contracts with "Waste Infrastructure Credits" paid as grant over the contract life (GBP 3.2 billion available after the 2007 Spending Review; credits withdrawn from seven projects on October 20, 2010 as no longer needed for the 2020 landfill target). Norfolk signed a 25-year, roughly GBP 582 million contract with Cory Wheelabrator in February 2012 for the Willows plant near King's Lynn, with bank financing arranged and hedges executed while planning consent was outstanding; the application was called in (August 2012), Defra's planning long-stop of June 10, 2013 was missed, the GBP 91 million credit allocation was revoked in October 2013, and Norfolk announced termination on April 7, 2014 at an estimated cost of GBP 33.7 million: GBP 20.3 million (a contractual cap) for the contractor's costs, mainly bank financing fees; GBP 11.8 million to unwind FX and interest-rate hedging; GBP 1.6 million of planning-inquiry costs; terminating before the May 1, 2014 breakpoint avoided about GBP 4.7 million more. Contrast Herefordshire and Worcestershire's Hartlebury plant, where delay outlasted lender appetite and the councils became sole lenders through PWLB borrowing in May 2014, losing GBP 30 million of central support (from GBP 143 million to GBP 113 million). Lessons: in waste PPPs the credit risk sits before financial close; hedges executed before consents become termination costs; when private finance walks away the authority becomes the lender, with the conflicts the NAO described. Do not state construction cost, debt, lenders or gearing for Willows or EnviRecover, later litigation, or Riverside, Warsan, Manchester or Merseyside terms.

81.5 Risks that remain when payment is certain (sec:81.5)
- 81.5.1 The payment-chain trace (ssec:81.5.1). Framework 81.1 applied to a hospital, a desal plant, Tideway and a WtE plant (Exhibit 81.3, Illustrative), with the Norfolk case as the WtE column's real check: the end-payer was the council tax and landfill-saving budget, the central grant was a second link that could be (and was) withdrawn, and the planning consent was a link outside any contract.
- 81.5.2 Construction and contractor credit (ssec:81.5.2). Example 81.5 (cost to complete after contractor insolvency).
- 81.5.3 Performance, deductions, and passdown (ssec:81.5.3). Deductions flow to the FM subcontractor up to caps; the project company keeps the residual (link to Example 81.1).
- 81.5.4 Pandemic and payment continuity (ssec:81.5.4). HM Treasury guidance of April 2, 2020: COVID-19 not regarded as force majeure; authorities to keep paying unitary charges and to suspend performance deductions where performance suffered despite best efforts (covid-transport).
- 81.5.5 Political, affordability, and currency risk in water (ssec:81.5.5). Tariff increases as political events; currency mismatch between local-currency tariffs and hard-currency fees or debt (Maynilad); cross-reference Chapter 59 on currency for water concessions with hard-currency debt.

81.6 Project agreements, water purchase agreements, and waste contracts (sec:81.6)
- 81.6.1 Social infrastructure project agreement and subcontracts (ssec:81.6.1). Exhibit 81.4 (TikZ contract map of an availability hospital PPP, Illustrative: authority, project company, D&C contractor, hard FM, soft FM, lenders, sponsors, independent tester).
- 81.6.2 Water purchase and energy supply agreements (ssec:81.6.2). Clause 81.1 (capacity charge and availability, water purchase agreement, Illustrative) with annotations.
- 81.6.3 Waste supply, power sale, and residue contracts (ssec:81.6.3). Put-or-pay waste supply agreement; Clause 81.2 (put-or-pay waste supply, with variants a sponsor-friendly, b lender-friendly, c municipality-friendly) and a landing paragraph as practitioner observation (D-011 style, reviewer sign-off).

81.7 How social infrastructure, water, and waste plants have been financed (sec:81.7)
- 81.7.1 Financing terms from Taweelah, UK PFI, Tideway, and Canadian P3s (ssec:81.7.1). Taweelah RO IWP (financial close October 19, 2019; cost AED 3.19 billion; senior loans AED 2.71 billion, about 85% of cost (computed); conventional and Islamic tranches; soft mini-perm with expected refinancing within at most three years after commercial operation; 909,200 m3/day); Jubail 3A (financial close December 3, 2020; ACWA Power's USD 650 million project description); Hassyan IWP tariff USD 0.365/m3 (2023) and financial close in March 2024 (t-market-norms-2). Long Gulf water debt from ACWA Power's 2025 accounts (t-market-norms-2): Shuaibah II refinanced in 2021 by USD 166.2 million of 3.7% notes amortizing to May 27, 2044; Rabigh 3 IWP refinanced SAR 1,766.25 million of floating-rate debt in 2025 with SAR 1,237.5 million of amortizing bonds plus a SAR 375 million facility, maturing September 2045 to December 2046 (margins and DSCRs not disclosed). UK PFI: program statistics (694 projects with GBP 54.7 billion capital value and GBP 152.2 billion of unitary charges still to be paid at March 31, 2021) and the NAO's 2018 finding of about 90% senior debt and 10% equity over 25 to 30 years, with PF2 reverting from 75:25 to 90:10. Canadian availability P3s rated by DBRS in 2026: projected minimum DSCRs of 1.21x (Plenary Justice Okanagan), 1.22x (Plenary Health Hamilton), 1.27x (Capital City Link), 1.37x (Accès Recherche Montréal) and 1.44x (Women's College Partnership); operating projects run close to their model DSCRs, and DBRS downgraded Plenary Health Care Partnerships Humber to BBB on October 2, 2026 because of hospital lawsuits against the design-build contractor, a non-ratio event (t-market-norms-2). Tideway's financing as a regulated single-asset company (gearing and interest cover covenants, RPI-linked debt, green and blue bonds; no specific covenant levels unless in the fact sheet). Waste-to-energy: no verified financing of an operating project-financed EfW plant exists in the fact sheets (wte bars Riverside, Warsan and the Manchester and Merseyside PFIs), so WtE terms are taught only as the D-011 ranges in ssec:81.7.2, and Norfolk's Willows termination (ssec:81.4.3) is the sector's landmark failure. Exhibit 81.6 (new, appended): verified financing terms table.
- 81.7.2 Gearing and cover ranges for availability, water, and waste projects (ssec:81.7.2). Availability PPPs: the verified points of ssec:81.7.1 now carry the teaching (UK PFI about 90:10, NAO 2018; Canadian A-range P3s at projected minimum DSCRs of about 1.20x to 1.27x, with BBB(high) and research-centre deals higher, DBRS 2026); any wider range across the UK, Europe, Canada and Australia, 2010 to 2025, is D-011 indicative only and printed as practitioner consensus with reviewer sign-off (t-market-norms lists "PPP gearing 85 to 90 percent" under "Do not state" as a generic norm; t-market-norms-2 says no Australian figure could be verified and 1.20x is not a rating rule). Gulf IWPs, 2019 to 2025, around 85% debt (Taweelah verified; tenors run to within a few years of 25-year contracts on the ACWA evidence; the gearing range otherwise indicative). Contracted waste-to-energy in Europe, 2015 to 2025, lower gearing than availability PPPs, with higher cover when gate fees are merchant (D-011, no numbers unless the reviewer supplies and signs them). Drivers ranked: payer credit and payment mechanism; construction complexity, planning status and contractor credit; volume or feedstock exposure; refinancing assumptions.
- 81.7.3 Refinancing and gain sharing (ssec:81.7.3). Soft mini-perms in Gulf water; UK PFI refinancing gain sharing (0% in early deals, a voluntary 30% code, at least 50% in later standard terms, 70% above GBP 3 million for deals agreed since 2009, and the PF2 33% cap linked to Eurostat rules); cross-reference Chapter 58.

81.8 Modeling lifecycle, water volumes, and waste tonnage (sec:81.8)
- 81.8.1 Lifecycle and deductions (ssec:81.8.1). Lifecycle profiles, reserve funding (cross-reference Chapter 37), deduction passdown with caps and the residual to the project company.
- 81.8.2 Water volumes, availability, and energy (ssec:81.8.2). Contracted capacity, availability, dispatch by the single buyer; energy price pass-through; membrane replacement schedule.
- 81.8.3 Waste tonnage, calorific value, and thermal capacity (ssec:81.8.3). The model must cap tonnage by thermal capacity (Example 81.3); gate-fee indexation; residues.

81.9 Carillion's hospitals and the limits of risk transfer (sec:81.9)
Real case from carillion: sequence from the July 10, 2017 provision of GBP 845 million (do not state the as-at date) through GBP 1,045 million in September 2017 to liquidation; the two hospitals; terminations; NAO's estimate that investors, their insurers and Carillion lost at least GBP 603 million on the two hospitals, the public sector paid about 1% more in real terms, patients waited roughly four to six years longer, and the trusts took on uncapped latent-defect risk; AWPR partners finished the work. Lesson: availability PPP debt is a construction credit until completion; a replacement contractor will not take latent-defect risk; governments may refuse rescue for precedent and national-accounts reasons. Do not give a single total taxpayer cost; do not give Midland's out-turn as GBP 988 million.

81.10 Thames Tideway and the carve-out from a utility (sec:81.10)
Real case from tideway. Lesson: revenue during construction on a growing regulatory capital value plus a capped state backstop for tail risks compressed the cost of capital; the cost was collection dependence on an incumbent and a contingent public exposure. Do not state handover, a GBP 5 billion cost, licence date of November 3, 2015 (it was August 2015), or that the GSP was drawn. One-sentence forward reference to Chapter 84 for green and blue labels.

81.11 Gulf desalination and the price of competition (sec:81.11)
Real case from gulf-iwpp: Exhibit 81.2 tariff series; Taweelah RO financing; Shuaibah 3 conversion as contract restructuring; Hassyan record. Lesson: standardized contracts, creditworthy single buyers, repeat bidders and high leverage drive tariffs down; present the winner's curse only as a hypothesis (the fact sheet says no verified analysis supports claims of subsidized fuel or loss-making bids).

81.12 Walkthrough: a desalination water purchase agreement tariff schedule (sec:81.12)
Artifact: an Illustrative tariff schedule (Exhibit 81.5) for a 600,000 m3/day RO plant. Steps under Walkthroughs.

81.13 Case T: the Port Ellery Northern Hospital (sec:81.13)
Small installment.

81.14 Practitioner's notebook (sec:81.14). 81.15 Judgment drill (sec:81.15).

81.16 Certain payment moves risk and does not remove it (sec:81.16)
Close. Crystallizes the payment-chain trace, contractor dependence and feedstock risk. Final paragraph poses the next problem: in digital infrastructure the payers are private (mobile operators, hyperscalers) and the assets age in years, not decades; how does a lender lend for 20 years against a 4-year lease (Chapter 82).

81.17 Exercises (sec:81.17). 81.18 Solutions to exercises (sec:81.18). Sources.

## 81.5 Opening

Carillion's liquidation day and the two hospital sites, told from the hospital project company's position: a fixed-price contract, a government payer, and no money to finish. Facts from carillion only.

## 81.6 Worked examples

Example 81.1. Gearing and cover in a hospital availability PPP (Illustrative). Setting: Niagara West Health Partners LP (fictional), the project company for a fictional regional hospital P3 in Ontario, Canada, at financial close in 2024; amounts in CAD. Capital cost CAD 486.2 million; debt 90% (CAD 437.6 million) at 5.4% over 27 years, level annuity debt service CAD 31.16 million; capital element of the unitary charge set at 1.20x debt service (CAD 37.39 million), consistent with the 1.20x that DBRS calls standard for A-range Canadian availability PPPs (t-market-norms-2); hard FM CAD 21.4 million, lifecycle CAD 6.8 million, project company costs CAD 2.3 million; unitary charge CAD 67.89 million. A bad year brings deductions of 3.1% of the unitary charge (CAD 2.10 million). If deductions are not passed down, DSCR falls from 1.20x to 1.13x. If the hard FM contract passes deductions down with a cap at 10% of the FM fee (CAD 2.14 million), the full deduction is passed down and DSCR stays at 1.20x. Teaching point: thin cover is safe only if deductions and cost overruns are passed down to creditworthy subcontractors; the passdown cap is the real risk buffer (deduction design cited to sec:58.3).

Example 81.2. Building a desalination tariff (Illustrative). Setting: Aquasud Dakhla SA (fictional), a 600,000 m3/day reverse-osmosis IWP in Dakhla, Morocco, bidding in 2025 to sell water to Office Régional des Eaux du Sud (fictional) under a 25-year water purchase agreement; amounts in USD. Capital cost USD 640.3 million; 85% debt (USD 544.3 million) at 5.85% over 25 years: debt service USD 41.97 million; equity USD 96.0 million recovered at 10% over 25 years: USD 10.58 million; fixed O&M USD 14.6 million. Capacity charge USD 67.15 million a year; at 95% availability (208.05 million m3 a year) USD 0.3228/m3. Implied DSCR on the capacity charge net of fixed O&M: 1.25x. Output charge: energy 3.4 kWh/m3 x USD 0.052/kWh = USD 0.1768, chemicals USD 0.031, membranes USD 0.018, variable O&M USD 0.012: USD 0.2378/m3. Total tariff USD 0.5606/m3 (levelization arithmetic cited to ssec:5.3.3). Teaching point: compare with Jubail 3A (SAR 1.55/m3, about USD 0.41 at the peg, computed) and Hassyan (USD 0.365/m3): Exercise 81.6 shows which inputs a winning bidder must have changed (tenor, cost of debt, energy price, capex per m3).

Example 81.3. A waste-to-energy plant's revenue stack (Illustrative). Setting: Ouse Valley Energy Recovery Ltd (fictional), a moving-grate energy-from-waste plant in the East Riding of Yorkshire, England, under a 25-year contract with a fictional waste disposal authority; 2024 budget; amounts in GBP. Design throughput 389,700 t a year of residual waste at a design net calorific value of 9.8 MJ/kg (thermal capacity 3,819,060 GJ a year); mechanical limit 418,000 t. Gate fee GBP 96.40/t: GBP 37.57 million. Net electrical efficiency 26.5%: electricity 281,125 MWh; at GBP 71.20/MWh: GBP 20.02 million. Residue costs: bottom ash 21% of input at GBP 18.50/t (GBP 1.51 million); air-pollution-control residues 3.5% at GBP 142.00/t (GBP 1.94 million). If NCV falls to 8.6 MJ/kg, the thermal cap would allow 444,077 t, so the mechanical limit binds at 418,000 t: gate fees GBP 40.30 million, electricity 264,617 MWh (GBP 18.84 million). If NCV rises to 11.2 MJ/kg (more plastics), thermal capacity caps tonnage at 340,988 t: gate fees fall to GBP 32.87 million (a GBP 4.70 million loss) while electricity stays at GBP 20.02 million. Teaching point: in WtE "better" waste can cost money; the model must cap throughput by thermal capacity (Equation 81.3). Recomputed in Python (revision round 1; the version-1.0 tonnage was replaced).

Example 81.4. Non-revenue water and cash revenue (Illustrative). Setting: PT Tirta Kencana Lestari (fictional), a 25-year municipal water concession for a fictional city of about 4.1 million people in East Java, Indonesia, 2025; tariffs set in rupiah and shown here at their USD equivalent for comparability. Production 398.3 million m3 a year (replaces the version-1.0 figure); collection rate 87%; average tariff USD 0.94/m3. At 42% non-revenue water, billed volume 231.0 million m3 and cash revenue USD 188.9 million. At 30%, billed volume 278.8 million m3 and cash revenue USD 228.0 million: USD 39.1 million more without new production. Teaching point: in water concessions, operational performance on losses and collection is worth more than tariff increases that politics may refuse; Manila Water's fall from 63% to 29.9% non-revenue water (1997 to 2006) is the real-world scale (ssec:81.2.2). Recomputed in Python.

Example 81.5. Cost to complete after a contractor fails (Illustrative). Setting: Wrekin Health Partners Ltd (fictional), the project company for a fictional PFI hospital in Shropshire, England, in February 2018, weeks after its builder (fictional, modeled on the Carillion pattern) enters liquidation; amounts in GBP. GBP 191.3 million of work is left under a GBP 426.7 million fixed-price contract. A replacement contractor prices the remaining work 62% higher, at GBP 309.9 million, and excludes latent defects in completed work. Resources: undrawn debt equal to the original remaining cost (GBP 191.3 million); contingency GBP 18.9 million; performance bond 10% of contract price (GBP 42.7 million); retention and LDs held GBP 12.8 million; total extra resources GBP 74.4 million. Shortfall GBP 44.2 million (309.9 - 191.3 - 74.4). Teaching point: the bond and contingency rarely cover the replacement premium; the shortfall falls on equity, then on lenders through termination compensation (cross-reference Chapter 58 for compensation on project company default and Chapter 61 for the process). Recomputed in Python (revision round 1; the version-1.0 contract price was replaced).

## 81.7 Walkthrough

Walkthrough: a desalination water purchase agreement tariff schedule (Section 81.12). Exhibit 81.5 (Illustrative schedule).
1. Identify the capacity charge components (capital recovery, fixed O&M, insurance) and their indexation (USD, local CPI, none).
2. Identify the output charge components and which are pass-through (energy at actual cost or at a deemed consumption).
3. Read the availability definition and the capacity charge reduction for unavailability (link to Clause 81.1).
4. Read the water quality specification and penalties.
5. Read the energy supply arrangement (grid tariff, dedicated solar PPA) and who bears the energy price.
6. Recompute the levelized tariff (Example 81.2) and compare with the bid evaluation formula.
7. Write the lenders' summary: which risks the schedule leaves with the project company.

## 81.8 Frameworks

Framework 81.1, the payment-chain trace (original; label fw:payment-chain). Purpose: find the weak link between the end-payer and the project company when revenue looks certain. Steps: (1) name the end-payer (taxpayer through budget, water customer through bill, municipality through gate fee, power market); (2) list each intermediary that collects or appropriates the money (ministry, utility, single buyer, municipality); (3) for each link, state the credit, the legal obligation and the collection or appropriation risk; (4) identify the protections (guarantees, escrow, licence obligations, reconciliation mechanisms); (5) identify the link most likely to break in a downturn and size liquidity for it. Demonstrated on Exhibit 81.3 and in Section 81.10 (Tideway's collection by Thames Water). Distinct from Chapter 28's "Who pays if...?" trace, which follows a loss through contracts; this traces the revenue to its source.

## 81.9 Real cases and fact sheets

- carillion: contractor failure in availability PPPs; limits of risk transfer (opening, sec:81.9, ssec:81.1.4, ssec:81.5.2).
- tideway: carve-out from a utility; collection dependence; state backstop (ssec:81.2.3, sec:81.10).
- gulf-iwpp: desalination procurement, tariffs, financing, decoupling (sec:81.3, sec:81.11, ssec:81.7.1).
- uk-pfi: program statistics, refinancing gain sharing, end of PFI (ssec:81.7.1, ssec:81.7.3, sec:81.1).
- covid-transport: UK PFI payment-continuity guidance (ssec:81.5.4).
- municipal-water-concession (Metro Manila, 1997; placed in revision round 1): ssec:81.2.2 (the currency-mismatch water concession), ssec:81.5.5 and Example 81.4's comparison. Lesson in one line: the same contract in the same city succeeded or failed according to the operator's currency exposure and performance on losses.
- wte (England's waste PFIs, Norfolk's Willows and Herefordshire and Worcestershire's EnviRecover; placed in revision round 1): ssec:81.4.3 (the landmark failure and its termination cost), ssec:81.5.1 (the payment-chain trace's WtE column) and ssec:81.7.1 (no verified WtE financing; terms taught as D-011). Lesson in one line: a waste PPP fails before construction, through planning and grant links that no contract controls.
- t-market-norms-2 (PPP and Gulf items; placed in revision round 1; supersedes the request t-ppp-norms): ssec:81.1.2 and ssec:81.7.1 (NAO's 90:10 PFI structure; DBRS minimum DSCRs for Canadian availability P3s; the Humber downgrade), ssec:81.7.1 (ACWA's Shuaibah II and Rabigh 3 refinancings; Hassyan's 2024 close) and ssec:81.7.2. Respect its "Do not state" list (1.20x is not a rating rule; no Australian figures; no Gulf margins or DSCRs).

Open requests (the chapter can be taught without them):
- fact sheet requested: an operating project-financed energy-from-waste plant with verified financial close and terms (coverage review defect 4; the wte sheet bars Riverside, Warsan and the Manchester and Merseyside PFIs). Until it exists, ssec:81.7.1 states that WtE financing terms are taught as D-011 indicative ranges and that Willows is the sector's landmark failure.
- The version-1.0 requests wte and municipal-water-concession were delivered; t-ppp-norms is superseded by t-market-norms-2.

## 81.10 Running-case installment

Case T (small), story date 2016; place and month "Partnerships Brannock, Port Ellery, June 2016" (between the preferred-bidder decision of March 2016 and financial close on August 30, 2016; writer may choose a month in that window). Characters: Maggie Dunleavy, Director of Partnerships Brannock (2011 to 2020); Nerida Faulkes, Partnerships Brannock's commercial lead on the hospital (Annex TR T.13 and T.14; optional, verbal habit "Show me the payment mechanism"). Inputs (Annex TR T.14, book inputs): the Port Ellery Northern Hospital, 412 beds; availability-based DBFM with hard facilities management only (clinical and soft services stay with the state health service); capital cost ARD 735 million; preferred bidder March 2016; financial close August 30, 2016; construction to April 2019; 25-year service period; maximum annual service payment ARD 68.2 million in 2016 prices, 75% indexed to Ardmore CPI, with an availability-and-performance deduction regime by functional unit; concessionaire Ellery Health Infrastructure Partners (consortium; members never named); senior debt 88% of funding from a bank club led by Penhallow Bank with Kaito Pacific Bank.

Scene outline: a year after the Merrick Link's financial close, Maggie chairs a pre-close review of the hospital. A Treasury analyst asks why the hospital deal shows 88% senior debt when the Merrick Link's senior debt was sized at 1.50x on a traffic banking case (Case Bible 2.5). Maggie's habit ("What does the standard form say?") appears once, about deduction passdown and contractor replacement; Nerida answers through the payment mechanism. The narration after the box teaches: the state keeps demand risk (patients) and pays for availability, so lenders size on construction and performance risk (Example 81.1); contrast with the Merrick Link's demand risk (Chapter 79); in 2016 the Merrick Link has not yet opened, so the scene must not show knowledge of its ramp-up. Figures displayed: Case Bible inputs only (the T.14 hospital terms; Merrick Link senior sizing at 1.50x on the banking case, Case Bible 2.5); no ledger figures. State at end: procurement continues toward the August close.

## 81.11 Practitioner's notebook (outline)

Checklist: payer and appropriation process; deduction regime and passdown caps; contractor credit, bonds, parent guarantees, replacement-contractor plan; latent-defect allocation; lifecycle plan and reserve; for water, tariff-setting process, collection rates and non-revenue water plan; for desal, energy supply and price, membrane plan, intake risk; for WtE, waste supply contracts, composition forecasts, thermal capacity, residue outlets, emissions permits.

Red flags: a single contractor for D&C and FM who is also the main shareholder; passdown caps far below plausible deductions; WtE sized on tonnage without a thermal-capacity cap; a water concession dependent on tariff increases not yet approved; a carve-out whose revenue is collected by a stressed incumbent.

Rules of thumb with limits: availability PPP debt is a construction credit first (limit: brownfield refinancings); WtE gate fees from long municipal contracts support more debt than merchant gate fees; desal tariffs are mostly capital and energy (limit: tenor and cost of debt drive the capital part).

Common mistakes: treating unitary charge as risk-free revenue during construction; modeling deductions without passdown; ignoring that recycling changes waste composition; assuming a utility collector's credit equals the regulator's protection.

Questions experts ask: Who actually writes the check, and from which budget line? What happens if the builder disappears tomorrow? What deductions does the FM contractor bear, and can it pay them? What does a tonne of waste look like in ten years? Who pays for energy above the deemed price?

## 81.12 Judgment drill

The situation: it is September 2025 and you are the contracting authority's project director for Gwent Valleys Acute Hospital (fictional), an availability-based hospital PPP in Wales (Illustrative), eight months from completion. The D&C contractor, which also holds 40% of the project company's equity and the hard FM contract, announces a large provision against its construction contracts and its share price falls 39% in a day. The project company's lenders ask whether you will consent to an assignment of the D&C contract to a joint-venture partner and a deferral of the completion date by six months without deductions. Board meeting in ten days.

Reasoning outline: the authority's protections are the contract's termination regime and its public interest in a finished hospital; compare scenarios using Example 81.5 logic (cost to complete, bond, contingency, latent defects); an early, orderly novation to a solvent contractor with a parent guarantee is usually cheaper than termination after insolvency (the Carillion record); the authority should not waive deductions without consideration; it should require independent cost-to-complete review, step-in readiness, and a firm long-stop date.

What would change the answer: the JV partner's credit and willingness to accept latent-defect risk; the national-accounts consequence of the authority taking more risk (cross-reference Chapter 57); evidence that the contractor will survive (then monitor rather than act).

## 81.13 Exercise set

Tier 1 (4):
- 81.1: Explain why availability PPPs carry higher gearing than toll roads, using the NAO's 90:10 finding and Example 81.1.
- 81.2: Apply the payment-chain trace to a municipal WtE plant, using Norfolk's Willows contract as the real check.
- 81.3: Explain why non-revenue water reduction can be worth more than a tariff increase, with the Manila comparison.
- 81.4: Explain what the Tideway Government Support Package covered and why it lowered the cost of capital.

Tier 2 (5; all inputs and expected answers below, computed in Python):
- 81.5: Using Example 81.1's Niagara West structure (debt service CAD 31.16 million; capital element CAD 37.39 million; hard FM fee CAD 21.4 million; unitary charge CAD 67.89 million), a year brings deductions of 4.6% of the unitary charge. Compute DSCR with passdown caps of 5%, 10% and 20% of the FM fee. Answers: deduction CAD 3.12 million; passdown CAD 1.07, 2.14 and 3.12 million; DSCR 1.134x, 1.168x and 1.200x (prints 1.13x, 1.17x, 1.20x).
- 81.6: Using Example 81.2's Aquasud Dakhla inputs, recompute the tariff with 30-year debt at 4.9% (equity terms unchanged), and find the energy price at which the original USD 0.5606/m3 tariff would fall to USD 0.41/m3 with all else unchanged. Answers: debt service USD 35.00 million; capacity charge USD 60.18 million, USD 0.2893/m3; total tariff USD 0.5271/m3; the energy price would have to fall to USD 0.0077/kWh, which is not credible, so a winning bidder at USD 0.41 must also have cheaper capital or capex.
- 81.7: Rheinkreis Energie aus Abfall GmbH (fictional), North Rhine-Westphalia, Germany, 2025, amounts in EUR: design 255,000 t at 10.2 MJ/kg (thermal capacity 2,601,000 GJ); mechanical limit 270,000 t; gate fee EUR 104.80/t; net electrical efficiency 24.8%; power EUR 63.40/MWh. Compute tonnage, gate fees, electricity and revenue at NCV 9.1, 10.2 and 11.6 MJ/kg. Answers: 270,000 t (mechanical cap), 255,000 t, 224,224 t (thermal cap); gate fees EUR 28.30, 26.72 and 23.50 million; electricity 169,260, 179,180 and 179,180 MWh (EUR 10.73, 11.36 and 11.36 million); revenue EUR 39.03, 38.08 and 34.86 million.
- 81.8: Lothian Schools Partnership Ltd (fictional), Scotland, 2019, amounts in GBP: a school PPP's contractor fails with GBP 138.6 million of work left under a GBP 297.5 million fixed-price contract; the replacement prices the remaining work 71% higher; contingency GBP 11.2 million; performance bond 10% of contract price; retention and LDs held GBP 7.9 million; shareholders' equity at risk GBP 31.4 million, which absorbs losses first, with the rest falling on lenders through termination compensation. Answers: replacement cost GBP 237.0 million; extra resources GBP 48.85 million (bond 29.75); shortfall GBP 49.56 million; equity loses GBP 31.4 million and lenders bear GBP 18.16 million.
- 81.9: Draft the municipality-friendly variant of a put-or-pay waste supply clause and annotate it.

Tier 3 (4):
- 81.10: Model task (blank workbook, five operating years): Fraser Valley Courthouse Partners (fictional), British Columbia, amounts in CAD million. Inputs: unitary charge CAD 52.36 million in year 1, 75% indexed at 2.0% a year (25% fixed); deductions as a share of the unitary charge 0.8%, 3.9%, 1.2%, 6.4% and 0.5% in years 1 to 5; FM fee CAD 16.80 million escalating 2.5%; passdown cap 10% of the FM fee; project company costs CAD 1.9 million; lifecycle reserve funding CAD 4.6 million a year; debt service CAD 24.03 million a year. Build CFADS and DSCR. Answers: DSCR 1.209x, 1.210x, 1.240x, 1.185x and 1.271x; the minimum is 1.185x in year 4, where the deduction (CAD 3.50 million) exceeds the passdown cap (CAD 1.81 million).
- 81.11: Case analysis: Carillion's hospitals. Write a memo for a PPP unit on contract changes that would have reduced public cost, using only fact-sheet facts.
- 81.12: Case analysis: Gulf desalination. Explain from Exhibit 81.2 the main drivers of falling tariffs and argue whether the winner's curse is present, stating what evidence would prove it.
- 81.13: Case T extension: contrast the Port Ellery Northern Hospital's and the Merrick Link's risk allocation using the T.14 inputs and Case Bible 2.5.

## 81.15 Glossary entries owned

Per the glossary canon: hard facilities management, soft facilities management and lifecycle cost (sec:12.8), non-revenue water and reverse osmosis (sec:12.9) are homed in Chapter 12 (R-026), and IWPP at ssec:69.3.2 (R-067); they are cross-referenced, not owned. Terms owned here:

| Term | Abbreviation | Definition | Home |
|---|---|---|---|
| social infrastructure | – | Publicly used buildings and facilities such as hospitals, schools, prisons and courts, typically paid for by government rather than users. | ssec:81.1.1 |
| affermage | – | A water-sector lease in which a private operator runs public assets and earns a share of tariffs, while the public owner funds major investment. | ssec:81.2.1 |
| multi-stage flash | MSF | Thermal desalination that evaporates seawater in successive low-pressure stages using heat, often from a power plant. | ssec:81.3.1 |
| water purchase agreement | WPA | A long-term contract under which a single buyer pays a water project company capacity and output charges for desalinated or treated water. | ssec:81.3.2 |
| independent water project | IWP | A privately financed stand-alone water plant selling to a single buyer under a water purchase agreement. | ssec:81.3.3 |
| gate fee | – | The price per tonne a waste supplier pays a treatment plant to accept waste. | ssec:81.4.1 |
| put-or-pay | – | A waste supplier's obligation to deliver a minimum tonnage or pay as if it had. | ssec:81.4.2 |
| net calorific value | NCV | The usable heat released by burning a unit of fuel or waste, net of the heat used to evaporate its water. | ssec:81.4.2 |
| bottom ash | – | The solid residue left on the grate after waste combustion. | ssec:81.4.3 |
| air-pollution-control residues | APCr | Hazardous fly ash and reagent residues captured from a WtE plant's flue gas. | ssec:81.4.3 |
| payment-chain trace | – | Framework 81.1: tracing a project's revenue from the end-payer through each collecting or appropriating intermediary to find the weakest link. | ssec:81.5.1 |
| deduction passdown | – | The contractual transfer of availability or performance deductions from the project company to the subcontractor responsible, up to a cap. | ssec:81.5.3 |

Rulings applied:
