- R-026: tenancy ratio, homes passed, ARPU and IRU (sec:12.10), build-to-suit, colocation (data centers) and PUE (sec:12.11) are Chapter 12 terms used here without bold; this chapter owns the sector mechanics built on them.
- R-083: "colocation (towers)" is owned here (ssec:82.2.1); "colocation (data centers)" is homed at sec:12.11.
- R-037: cyber risk is cited to ssec:14.19.3, cyber cover to ssec:27.3.4 and operational-technology cyber controls to ssec:62.5.4; one sentence in ssec:82.5.3 (t-cyber-infrastructure).
- Residual value guarantee (RVG) has its glossary home at ssec:21.9.2; ssec:82.6.3 owns the data-center RVG drafting, and the duplicate glossary entry is deleted.
- Sequencing review: "overbuild" is split into "overbuild (networks)" at ssec:82.3.2 (owned here) and "overbuild (storage)" at ssec:73.2.2 (central change).
- R-101 and D-029: the chapter owns only the items in architecture.md's Owns note for Chapter 82. R-105 and Annex TR R.2: the Case R beat uses the Ostrander profile and vPPA credit-support terms.
- Standards review defects 3 (sec:82.5 retitled as the review proposed; sec:82.6, sec:82.7, ssec:82.7.1, sec:82.8 retitled; sec:82.4 retitled because it duplicated sec:12.11), 4 (examples located), 5 (the 412,600 homes in Example 82.2 replaced), 6 (Tier 2 and Tier 3 inputs and answers), 7 (target raised); coverage defects 5 (t-digital-norms, tower-carveout and ftth-altnet placed) and 9 (ssec:82.3.4 "Subsea cables and capacity pre-sales" appended with an Illustrative example, since the subsea-cable sheet was not delivered).

## 82.1 Header

- Chapter: 82
- Title: Telecoms, fiber, towers, and data centers
- File: `chapters/82-digital-infrastructure.tex`
- Target length: 13,000 to 15,000 words (raised in revision round 1 per standards review defect 7; four asset types plus subsea cables). The target is a floor for the core teaching of each sector, not a cap.
- Dominant lens: a digital infrastructure investor and lender (towers, fiber, data centers) with the power-market specialist's view on data-center energy supply.

## 82.2 Purpose and capability statement

Purpose. Digital infrastructure has the shape of infrastructure (long-lived assets, contracted revenue, essential services) and the economics of technology (fast obsolescence, competition, customers who consolidate or move). This chapter teaches tower, fiber, subsea-cable and data-center economics; the contracts that make them financeable (master lease agreements, wholesale access, capacity pre-sales, hyperscale leases, build-to-suit joint ventures with residual value guarantees); how towers, fiber and data centers are securitized; why power now governs data-center timing; how lenders handle the gap between short leases and long debt; and how AI-driven financing of 2024 to 2026 priced tenant credit.

What you will be able to do:
- Compute tower margins and returns as tenancy rises, and fiber EBITDA through a penetration ramp (Capabilities 4, 13).
- Size data-center debt against a long lease and against a short lease backed by a residual value guarantee, and explain what the lender is lending against (Capabilities 5, 15).
- Read a hyperscale data-center lease for the terms that decide credit (Capabilities 3, 7).
- Use the lease-life gap test to compare lease, debt, technology and power-contract lives (Capabilities 2, 14).

## 82.3 Concepts owned and assumed

Owned:
1. Digital infrastructure economics: why contracted and essential, why exposed to technology and consolidation.
2. Tower economics built on the Chapter 12 terms (tenancy ratio, sec:12.10): colocation (towers), rent escalators, ground leases, amendment revenue, margin expansion with tenancy; master lease agreements with mobile network operators (never abbreviated); sale-and-leaseback carve-outs of operator towers, with FX-linked escalators in emerging markets (IHS and MTN).
3. Fiber economics built on the Chapter 12 terms (homes passed, ARPU, IRU, sec:12.10): backbone, metro, fiber-to-the-home (FTTH); penetration (take-up), churn; cost per home passed and per connection; overbuild (networks); open-access wholesale against vertically integrated models; the altnet financing life cycle from equity-funded build to consolidation (CityFibre).
3a. Subsea cables (appended in revision round 1, coverage defect 9): capacity pre-sales and IRUs as revenue contracts and construction funding, consortium against private-cable models, DFI participation in principle.
4. Data-center economics built on the Chapter 12 terms (colocation (data centers), PUE, build-to-suit, sec:12.11): hyperscale and edge; MW of IT load as the unit of sale; powered shell and triple-net leases; rent per kW-month; capital cost per MW at sector level.
5. Power as the binding constraint for data centers: grid connection queues, transformer and turbine lead times, onsite generation and overbuild, data-center PPAs (instrument in Chapter 20).
6. Lease structures and credit: tenant credit, lease tenor against debt tenor, renewal options, residual value guarantees (RVGs), hyperscaler joint ventures off the tenant's balance sheet.
7. GPU and compute-backed lending: special-purpose subsidiaries lending against contracts with AI tenants; obsolescence of IT equipment against building life.
8. Framework 82.1, the lease-life gap test (original).
9. Digital-specific risks: tenant concentration, operator consolidation and network sharing, technology change (small cells, satellite), overbuild, power availability and price, cooling and water, residual value.
10. Sector financing terms: verified 2024 to 2026 data-center pricing and cover; verified US tower, fiber and data-center securitization terms (anticipated repayment dates, DSCR cash traps, step-up interest; t-digital-norms); tower carve-out acquisition debt in local currency (tower-carveout); altnet bank packages (ftth-altnet).
11. Modeling specifics: lease-up curves, tenancy additions, penetration ramps and churn, escalators, power pass-through, refresh capex, residual value.

Assumed:
- Fiber networks and data centers at financier depth, and the terms tenancy ratio, homes passed, ARPU, IRU, colocation (data centers), PUE and build-to-suit: Chapter 12 (sec:12.10, sec:12.11; R-026).
- Residual value guarantee as an instrument: ssec:21.9.2. Cyber risk, cyber cover and OT cyber controls: ssec:14.19.3, ssec:27.3.4, ssec:62.5.4 (R-037).
- Data-center leases as contracts: Chapter 21.
- Corporate PPAs physical and virtual, counterparty credit in vPPAs: Chapter 20.
- Electricity markets, nodal pricing: Chapter 11.
- Securitization compared with project finance: Chapter 2. Holdco debt: Chapter 31. Project bonds and 144A: Chapter 30.
- Consolidation, equity method, variable interest entities: Chapter 66.
- DSCR, sizing, balloons and mini-perms: Chapters 35 and 36. Pricing: Chapter 38.
- Private credit as a lender type: Chapter 29.

Forward references: Chapter 88 for AI-demand and private-credit frameworks (one sentence in 82.9); Chapter 84 for green labels on data-center financing if raised (none required).

## 82.4 Locked table of contents

Opening (no heading). October 2025: Meta and funds managed by Blue Owl form a joint venture to develop the Hyperion data-center campus in Richland Parish, Louisiana, at an estimated development cost of about USD 27 billion; debt is raised privately through Beignet Investor LLC in senior secured notes maturing in 2049; Meta leases the buildings from 2029 under four-year initial terms renewable up to 20 years and gives a residual value guarantee that declines over 16 years; Meta's disclosed maximum exposure to loss is USD 46.03 billion at June 30, 2026. Source: hyperion-meta. The puzzle: why would bondholders lend to 2049 against a four-year lease?

What you will be able to do (unnumbered).

82.1 Infrastructure that ages like technology (sec:82.1)
- 82.1.1 What makes digital assets financeable (ssec:82.1.1). Contracted, essential, long-lived physical layer; what is not long-lived (electronics, IT).
- 82.1.2 Four asset types and their payers (ssec:82.1.2). Exhibit 82.1 (table: towers, fiber, data centers, compute; payer, contract form, typical tenor, main risk; Illustrative, with no market numbers).

82.2 Tower economics (sec:82.2)
- 82.2.1 Tenancy and the economics of sharing (ssec:82.2.1). Example 82.1. Glossary home of "colocation (towers)" (R-083); tenancy ratio is a sec:12.10 term.
- 82.2.2 Master lease agreements and carve-outs (ssec:82.2.2). Master lease agreement terms (escalators, amendments, cancellation rights, renewal); operator sale-and-leaseback of tower portfolios. Real case from tower-carveout: IHS Towers and MTN. IHS bought MTN's towers in Côte d'Ivoire and Cameroon (2013), Zambia and Rwanda (2014) and Nigeria (2014 to 2015), and on May 31, 2022 5,691 towers from MTN South Africa (with managed services for about 7,100 more MTN sites and a 30% B-BBEE ownership condition), for gross cash consideration of USD 421.2 million, partly funded by a ZAR 3,470.0 million South African-law facility at 3-month JIBAR plus 2.75% maturing May 2029, with net debt to EBITDA and interest cover covenants. MTN group master lease agreements now expire between December 2032 (Nigeria) and April 2034 (South Africa), about two years later than the 2022 filing showed; in 2025, 32% of IHS revenue was linked to the US dollar, 15% to the euro and 23% to power costs through indexation and pass-through clauses. On February 17, 2026 MTN agreed to take IHS private at USD 8.50 a share (enterprise value about USD 6.2 billion), against the 2021 IPO price of USD 21 (a share-price comparison, not a return); shareholders approved on August 4, 2026; closing not verified. Lessons: the carve-out converts an operator's passive network into a contracted rent stream sized to the anchor master lease agreement; local-currency acquisition debt matches local-currency rents; FX resets pass currency risk to the tenant without removing it; an anchor tenant may buy its infrastructure back. Do not state the 2013 to 2016 deal prices, any master lease agreement's pricing or escalator formula, the B-BBEE consortium's identity, that the merger has closed, or Cellnex, Vantage Towers or Helios terms.
- 82.2.3 Consolidation, network sharing, and technology (ssec:82.2.3). Mergers of operators remove tenants; network sharing; small cells and satellite as substitutes at the margin.

82.3 Fiber and subsea networks (sec:82.3)
- 82.3.1 Backbone, metro, and FTTH (ssec:82.3.1). IRUs for dark fiber as prepaid long leases (IRU is a sec:12.10 term); anchor tenants.
- 82.3.2 Penetration, ARPU, and overbuild (ssec:82.3.2). Example 82.2. Glossary home of "overbuild (networks)". Real case from ftth-altnet: CityFibre, the UK's largest wholesale-only altnet. Equity first (GBP 1.425 billion raised in 2021 to 2022 from Mubadala, Interogo, Antin and Goldman Sachs's infrastructure business); then a GBP 4.9 billion bank package (April 2022; underwritten by NatWest, Société Générale, Crédit Agricole CIB, BBVA, Intesa Sanpaolo, ING and SEB, with ABN AMRO, Lloyds, M&G and the UK Infrastructure Bank as core lenders) sized to a build toward 8 million premises; then penetration as the test (FY2025: revenue GBP 170 million, adjusted EBITDA GBP 29 million, 848,000 customers on 4.7 million premises passed, penetration above 20% overall and above 40% in mature cohorts, after Sky's July 2025 launch); then consolidation paid in shares (Lit Fibre, May 2024; Connexin's network, March 2025) and a July 14, 2025 GBP 2.3 billion round (GBP 500 million equity, a GBP 960 million debt expansion with the National Wealth Fund among lenders, an GBP 800 million acquisition accordion). Distress at a rival: the CMA's interim report of October 2, 2026 on nexfibre's purchase of Substantial (Netomnia, Brsk, YouFibre) found Substantial's "financial outlook has deteriorated", that CityFibre was the likely buyer otherwise, and provisionally a substantial lessening of wholesale competition. Lessons: an overbuilder is an equity risk until penetration is proven; when altnets run short of capital the exit is a sale, and competition law decides who may buy. Do not state CityFibre's facility pricing, tenor, covenants or leverage, any altnet insolvency, total altnet investment, deal prices, that UK altnets have issued fiber ABS, or the CMA's final decision.
- 82.3.3 Wholesale open access and subsidy programs (ssec:82.3.3). Wholesale models; subsidies for unserved areas as capital grants; one real instance: CityFibre and BDUK re-scoped CityFibre's Project Gigabit contracts on May 26, 2026 because commercial build had reached many subsidized premises (450,000 rural premises expected by 2030, 226,000 subsidized; ftth-altnet).
- 82.3.4 Subsea cables and capacity pre-sales (ssec:82.3.4) (new, appended; coverage defect 9). Capacity pre-sales and IRUs as revenue contracts and as construction funding; consortium cables owned by the carriers that use them against private cables that sell capacity; hyperscalers as anchor buyers; DFI participation where a cable serves an underserved region (principle only). Example 82.3 (Illustrative; no real cable's terms are stated, because the subsea-cable fact sheet was not delivered).

82.4 Data-center economics and leases (sec:82.4)
- 82.4.1 Hyperscale, colocation, and edge (ssec:82.4.1). MW of IT load as unit; PUE (a sec:12.11 term) introduced here and costed in Example 82.5 (ssec:82.5.3).
- 82.4.2 Power as the binding constraint (ssec:82.4.2). IEA figures from t-frontier-data: data centers used about 485 TWh in 2025, up 17%, with AI-focused consumption up 50%; projection of about 950 TWh by 2030 (about 3% of global demand); grid connection waits of five to ten years in many jurisdictions; transformer lead times of two to three years; gas turbine deliveries around five years; onsite gas needing 30% to 70% overbuild; ERCOT's 160 GW of data-center interconnection requests (not all will be built). Cutting-edge AI data centers at USD 40,000 to 50,000 per kW including IT equipment.
- 82.4.3 Leases, tenants, and tenor (ssec:82.4.3). Triple-net and powered-shell leases; build-to-suit (a sec:12.11 term); tenant credit tiers; Example 82.4.
- 82.4.4 Lending against compute (ssec:82.4.4). CoreWeave's USD 2.6 billion delayed-draw term loan of July 31, 2025 at SOFR plus 4%, maturing August 21, 2030, secured on a special-purpose subsidiary's assets and supporting a contract with OpenAI; Applied Digital's USD 2.35 billion 9.250% senior secured notes due 2030 priced at 97 on November 13, 2025 for data centers at Ellendale, North Dakota (t-market-norms). Obsolescence of GPUs against building life.

82.5 Tenant concentration, power supply, and obsolescence risk in digital assets (sec:82.5)
- 82.5.1 The lease-life gap test (ssec:82.5.1). Framework 82.1 applied to a tower portfolio, an FTTH network, a hyperscale campus and a GPU cluster (Exhibit 82.2, Illustrative).
- 82.5.2 Tenant concentration and credit (ssec:82.5.2). Pricing differences by tenant (t-market-norms: Oracle-tenant debt around SOFR plus 250 bps against CoreWeave-tenant deals at SOFR plus 325 to 425 bps, early 2026). Anchor-tenant concentration in towers (MTN under IHS's master lease agreements, ssec:82.2.2).
- 82.5.3 Power, cooling, and water (ssec:82.5.3). Who bears power price and availability under each lease form; Example 82.5 (moved here in revision round 1 so examples appear in label order); cooling water constraints (principle). One sentence on cyber: cyber risk, cover and operational-technology controls are taught in ssec:14.19.3, ssec:27.3.4 and ssec:62.5.4 (R-037).
- 82.5.4 Overbuild and residual value (ssec:82.5.4). FTTH overbuild by a second network (CityFibre against Openreach and VMO2/nexfibre, ssec:82.3.2); data-center residual value against technology change; the FSB's named vulnerabilities (valuation corrections, for example from power shortfalls delaying projects, and overcapacity).

82.6 Leases, access agreements, and guarantees for digital assets (sec:82.6)
- 82.6.1 Tower and fiber contracts (ssec:82.6.1). Master lease agreements, ground leases (booked as right-of-use assets in IHS's South Africa purchase accounting), IRUs, wholesale access agreements, managed-services agreements, construction contracts.
- 82.6.2 Data-center contracts (ssec:82.6.2). Lease (Chapter 21 form), power supply and PPAs (Chapter 20), utility interconnection, design-build and long-lead equipment, construction management by the tenant (Meta as construction and property manager at Hyperion), RVG. Exhibit 82.3 (TikZ contract map of a hyperscale build-to-suit joint venture, Illustrative, modeled on but not stating Hyperion's terms beyond the fact sheet).
- 82.6.3 The residual value guarantee clause (ssec:82.6.3). The RVG instrument is defined at ssec:21.9.2; Clause 82.1 (residual value guarantee, data-center lease, Illustrative) with annotations; the drafting points (threshold, decline schedule, trigger on non-renewal, cap, remarketing process, guarantor credit).

82.7 How digital infrastructure is financed (sec:82.7)
- 82.7.1 Data-center loans, notes, and lease-backed bonds, 2024 to 2026 (ssec:82.7.1). Exhibit 82.4: NRF panel (early 2025): hyperscale data centers SOFR plus 225 to 275 bps, co-location 300 to 350 bps, holdco debt 200 to 250 bps above senior; DSCR about 1.15x, portfolios as tight as 1.05x; early 2026 tenant-based spreads; data-center financings above USD 26 billion in 2024 (Cho); CoreWeave DDTL 3.0; Applied Digital notes (yield-to-maturity roughly 10.0% to 10.1% on the fact sheet's stated assumptions, labeled computed); data-center ABS (Csquare, majority-owned by Brookfield-managed funds: USD 885 million in October 2024 and USD 940 million, 815 million and 1,100 million in 2025, senior A-2 coupons of 6.40% to 7.31% with five-to-seven-year anticipated repayment dates, fee-simple multi-tenant collateral; t-digital-norms); Hyperion structure (no coupon, size or rating stated as fact; hyperion-meta forbids them; t-frontier-data and t-digital-norms give them only by cross-reference at medium confidence, so the writer either omits them or prints them as "reported" with the medium-confidence attribution, and prefers omission).
- 82.7.2 Tower and fiber securitizations and bank debt (ssec:82.7.2). Verified terms replace the version-1.0 D-011 placeholder (t-digital-norms; US market): SBA Communications' Tower Securities (USD 7.2 billion on 9,498 sites at end-2025; excess cash trapped at DSCR of 1.30x or below and amortization below 1.15x; coupons from 1.631% to 2.593% for 2020 to 2021 issues, 6.599% in November 2022, 4.831% and 4.654% after a treasury lock in October 2024; final maturities 2050 to 2056); fiber ABS from 2023 (Frontier 2023-1: A-2 USD 1,119 million at 6.60%, B at 8.30%, C at 11.50%, anticipated repayment date July 2028, legal final 2053, with a penetration-rate sweep trigger; Tucows and Ting May 2023; Uniti February 2025, the first enterprise fiber securitization, about 6.5% weighted yield; Shentel December 2025, A-2 at 5.64%). Emerging-market tower carve-out debt: IHS South Africa's ZAR facility at JIBAR plus 2.75% to May 2029 (tower-carveout). UK altnet bank packages: CityFibre's GBP 4.9 billion and GBP 960 million (terms not disclosed; ftth-altnet). Indicative ranges stated with the sheet's medium-confidence label: US senior A-2 coupons about 1.6% to 2.6% (2020 to 2021), about 6.6% (late 2022) and about 4.7% to 4.8% (late 2024) for towers; 5.6% to 6.6% for fiber (2023 to 2025); 6.4% to 7.3% for data centers (late 2024 to 2025). Do not state class ratings, issuance LTVs or DSCRs, European tower or fiber ABS terms, or private data-center issuers' terms; do not present 2020 to 2021 coupons as normal.
- 82.7.3 How a digital securitization traps cash and steps up interest (ssec:82.7.3). The template once, from SBA and Frontier: a bankruptcy-remote issuer secured on the assets and contracts issues fixed-rate classes with an anticipated repayment date of about 3 to 10 years and a legal final of about 30 years; DSCR-based cash traps and amortization; if not refinanced by the anticipated repayment date, cash is swept and step-up interest of at least 5% applies; risk-retention notes; variable funding notes for working capital. Construction is financed first by warehouse, bank or private-credit facilities and termed out once customers are connected. Cross-reference Chapter 2 for securitization against project finance.

82.8 Modeling lease-up, penetration, and residual value (sec:82.8)
- 82.8.1 Lease-up and tenancy curves (ssec:82.8.1). Building MW lease-up; tower tenancy additions; cross-reference Chapter 45 for ramp-up mechanics.
- 82.8.2 Penetration, churn, and ARPU (ssec:82.8.2). Gross additions less churn; ARPU escalation; connection capex timed with subscribers (Example 82.2).
- 82.8.3 Power pass-through, refresh capex, and residual value (ssec:82.8.3). PUE and load factor in the cost line (Example 82.5); refresh cycles; residual value modeling against an RVG threshold (Example 82.4).

82.9 Hyperion and the short lease with a long guarantee (sec:82.9)
Real case from hyperion-meta and t-frontier-data. Lesson: debt investors underwrote a short lease plus an investment-grade residual value guarantee, not a long contract; off-balance-sheet treatment shifts accounting leverage without removing economic exposure (USD 46.03 billion maximum loss); power procurement gates the timetable. Comparators: Oracle's 15- to 19-year not-yet-commenced leases and Meta's July 2026 El Paso venture with a USD 13 billion RVG (partner not disclosed). Do not say Meta guarantees the bonds. One-sentence forward reference to Chapter 88.

82.10 Walkthrough: reading a hyperscale data-center lease for lenders (sec:82.10)
Artifact: an Illustrative lease summary (Exhibit 82.5). Steps under Walkthroughs.

82.11 Case R: Ostrander Data Systems as a power buyer (sec:82.11)
Running-case installment.

82.12 Practitioner's notebook (sec:82.12). 82.13 Judgment drill (sec:82.13).

82.14 The lease is short, the building is long, the power is scarce (sec:82.14)
Close. Crystallizes tenancy, penetration, the lease-life gap and power. Final paragraph poses the next problem: the projects in Chapter 83 sell molecules (hydrogen, ammonia, captured carbon, fuels) for which there is no deep market at all; who will sign the contract a lender needs (Chapter 83).

82.15 Exercises (sec:82.15). 82.16 Solutions to exercises (sec:82.16). Sources.

## 82.5 Opening

The Hyperion announcement and its filings, from the bondholder's chair: a 2049 maturity, a four-year initial lease starting in 2029, an RVG whose threshold starts at about USD 28 billion and declines; then Meta's maximum-loss disclosure. Facts from hyperion-meta only.

## 82.6 Worked examples

Example 82.1. Tower margins as tenancy rises (Illustrative). Setting: Savanna Towers Ltd (fictional) owns a rooftop-free greenfield tower in Lagos State, Nigeria, built in 2025 for an anchor operator under a fictional master lease agreement with US-dollar-linked rents and FX resets (the pattern IHS's filings describe, ssec:82.2.2). The tower costs USD 186,000; ground lease USD 14,400 a year plus USD 2,100 for each tenant beyond the first; maintenance USD 3,900; rent USD 22,800 per tenant a year. At tenancy 1.0: revenue USD 22,800; costs USD 18,300; EBITDA USD 4,500 (19.7% margin, 2.4% yield on cost). At 1.6: revenue USD 36,480; costs USD 19,560; EBITDA USD 16,920 (46.4%, 9.1%). At 2.2: revenue USD 50,160; costs USD 20,820; EBITDA USD 29,340 (58.5%, 15.8%). Teaching point: towers are a fixed-cost business whose credit improves with each tenant; an operator merger that removes a tenant reverses the curve. Verified in Python.

Example 82.2. An FTTH network through its penetration ramp (Illustrative). Setting: Pennine Fibre Ltd (fictional), a wholesale-only altnet building in West Yorkshire, England, from 2024, with a fictional bank club; amounts in GBP. 386,400 homes passed (replaces the version-1.0 figure) at GBP 742 per home passed (build capex GBP 286.7 million); connection capex GBP 618 per new subscriber; wholesale ARPU GBP 21.40 a month; opex GBP 31.00 per home passed a year plus GBP 4.10 per subscriber a month. Penetration years 1 to 5: 8%, 17%, 26%, 33%, 38% (year-end subscribers used for the year, a stated simplification). Subscribers 30,912; 65,688; 100,464; 127,512; 146,832. Revenue GBP 7.94, 16.87, 25.80, 32.75, 37.71 million; opex GBP 13.50, 15.21, 16.92, 18.25, 19.20 million; EBITDA (5.56), 1.66, 8.88, 14.49, 18.50 million; connection capex GBP 19.10, 21.49, 21.49, 16.72, 11.94 million. EBITDA breakeven penetration 14.9%. If debt is 60% of build plus connections at 38% penetration (GBP 226.5 million) at 6.9%, interest is GBP 15.63 million: EBITDA does not cover interest until year 5 (1.18x interest-only). Teaching point: fiber debt during ramp-up needs equity, capitalized interest or a delayed-draw structure; penetration is the credit (CityFibre's equity-first sequence, ssec:82.3.2). Recomputed in Python (revision round 1).

Example 82.3. Capacity pre-sales as construction funding for a subsea cable (Illustrative; new in revision round 1, coverage defect 9). Setting: Sunda Arafura Cable Pte Ltd (fictional) builds a 4,900 km cable linking Singapore, Jakarta and Darwin, with ready for service in 2027; three anchor buyers (a fictional hyperscaler and two fictional carriers) sign 25-year fiber-pair IRUs in 2025. Capex USD 386.5 million. Anchor IRU prices total USD 151.8 million, paid 30% at signing (USD 45.54 million) and 70% at ready for service (USD 106.26 million); the anchors also pay annual O&M contributions that cover the cable's operating costs. Equity USD 104.6 million. Construction debt = 386.5 - 104.6 - 45.54 = USD 236.36 million; at ready for service the USD 106.26 million IRU balance mandatorily prepays it, leaving term debt of USD 130.10 million. Term debt is serviced from uncontracted capacity sales forecast at USD 24.7 million a year (O&M contributions net to zero against opex). Over 8 years at 7.4% (annuity factor 5.8798) the debt service is USD 22.13 million, a DSCR of 1.12x on forecast sales. If lenders size term debt at 1.60x on the forecast, maximum term debt is USD 90.77 million, a gap of USD 39.33 million to be filled by more pre-sales or equity. Teaching point: on a cable, pre-sold IRUs do the work that senior debt does elsewhere; what remains for lenders is merchant capacity sales, priced like any merchant risk. Computed in Python.

Example 82.4. Sizing data-center debt against a long lease and against a short lease with an RVG (Illustrative). Setting: Bull Run Data Campus LLC (fictional), a 96 MW (IT) build-to-suit campus in Loudoun County, Virginia, financed in 2025 for a fictional investment-grade hyperscaler tenant. The campus costs USD 13.4 million per MW (USD 1,286.4 million, excluding tenant IT); rent USD 112/kW-month (USD 129.02 million a year); landlord costs under a triple-net lease 6% of rent; cash flow USD 121.28 million; sizing DSCR 1.15x (in line with the verified early-2025 NRF hyperscale range, t-market-norms); debt service USD 105.46 million; rate 6.45%. Case A, 15-year lease, debt amortizing over the lease: annuity factor 9.433; debt USD 994.8 million (77.3% of cost). Case B, 4-year initial lease with an RVG at 72% of cost (USD 926.2 million): debt sized at the same USD 105.46 million debt service on a 20-year amortization profile (USD 1,166.7 million) would leave a balloon of USD 1,033.6 million after 4 years, above the RVG; with debt service held at USD 105.46 million for the 4-year lease and the balloon capped at 85% of the RVG (USD 787.3 million), maximum debt = 787.3/1.0645^4 + 105.46 x 3.4297 = USD 974.8 million (75.8% of cost). Method stated so the auditor can check it; verified in Python. Teaching point: the two structures support similar debt; in Case A the lender relies on the tenant's lease payments, in Case B on the guarantor's RVG at renewal; both are the tenant's credit.

Example 82.5. Power cost and PUE (Illustrative). Setting: the Bull Run campus of Example 82.4 in its first full operating year (2027), buying power at a fictional fixed price; placed in ssec:82.5.3. 96 MW IT at a 78% average load factor and PUE 1.28: annual consumption 839,614 MWh; at USD 68.40/MWh, USD 57.43 million, of which facility overhead (cooling and losses) 183,666 MWh (USD 12.56 million). At PUE 1.40 the bill rises by USD 5.38 million a year. Teaching point: under a triple-net lease the tenant pays; under a gross or fixed-power lease the landlord carries PUE and price risk; the lender reads the lease to see which.

## 82.7 Walkthrough

Walkthrough: reading a hyperscale data-center lease for lenders (Section 82.10). Exhibit 82.5 (Illustrative lease summary).
1. Parties and guarantor: tenant entity, parent guarantee or RVG, credit ratings.
2. Term, commencement and renewal: commencement conditions (power energization), initial term, options, notice periods.
3. Rent: rate per kW-month, escalator, abatement for delay, commencement date risk.
4. Power: who contracts, who pays, PUE commitments, curtailment rights.
5. Termination and contraction rights: tenant early termination, partial give-back, casualty and condemnation.
6. RVG mechanics (Clause 82.1): threshold, decline, trigger, cap, remarketing.
7. Assignment and lender protections: estoppels, non-disturbance, step-in, cure rights.
8. Score the lease under Framework 82.1 and write the lender's summary (Example 82.4 logic).

## 82.8 Frameworks

Framework 82.1, the lease-life gap test (original; label fw:lease-life-gap). Purpose: expose mismatches between the lives that matter in a digital asset. Steps: (1) list four lives: firm lease term, debt tenor, technology life of what the tenant uses the asset for, and power-contract or interconnection life; (2) plot them on one timeline (Exhibit 82.2); (3) for each gap where debt outlives the firm lease, name what covers it (renewal economics, RVG, re-leasing market, amortization before the gap); (4) for each gap where technology life is shorter than the lease, test the tenant's incentive to renew; (5) set sizing, amortization and sweeps so that debt outstanding at each gap is covered by the named support. Demonstrated in Example 82.4, the walkthrough and Section 82.9.

## 82.9 Real cases and fact sheets

- hyperion-meta: hyperscaler build-to-suit JV with RVG; off-balance-sheet structure; comparators (opening, sec:82.9, ssec:82.6.2).
- t-frontier-data: IEA power-demand data and bottlenecks; investment estimates; FSB on private credit and AI concentration (ssec:82.4.2, ssec:82.5.4).
- t-market-norms: NRF data-center pricing and cover; CoreWeave; Applied Digital (ssec:82.4.4, ssec:82.5.2, ssec:82.7.1).
- t-digital-norms (placed in revision round 1): ssec:82.7.1 (Csquare data-center ABS), ssec:82.7.2 (SBA tower securities; Frontier, Tucows, Uniti and Shentel fiber ABS; indicative coupon ranges with their confidence label) and ssec:82.7.3 (the securitization template). Lesson in one line: once built and leased, digital assets are financed like whole-business securitizations, with cash traps and anticipated repayment dates in place of sculpted project debt.
- tower-carveout (IHS Towers and MTN; placed in revision round 1): ssec:82.2.2 (the carve-out template and the MTN take-private), ssec:82.5.2 (anchor concentration), ssec:82.6.1 (ground leases as right-of-use assets) and ssec:82.7.2 (ZAR acquisition debt). Lesson in one line: a tower carve-out turns an operator's network into rent sized to one anchor master lease agreement, and the anchor may come back as the buyer.
- ftth-altnet (CityFibre and the nexfibre and Substantial inquiry; placed in revision round 1): ssec:82.3.2 (the overbuilder's financing life cycle and consolidation), ssec:82.3.3 (Project Gigabit re-scope), ssec:82.5.4 (overbuild) and ssec:82.7.2. Lesson in one line: penetration is the credit, and an altnet short of capital exits by sale under competition-law control.
- t-cyber-infrastructure: one sentence in ssec:82.5.3 by cross-reference (R-037).

Open requests (the chapter can be taught without them):
- fact sheet requested: subsea-cable (coverage defect 9): one project-financed or consortium subsea cable with verified financing. Until it exists, ssec:82.3.4 is taught with Example 82.3 (Illustrative) and states no real cable's terms.
- The version-1.0 requests t-digital-norms, tower-carveout and ftth-altnet were delivered.

## 82.10 Running-case installment

Case R, story date 2024 to 2026; scene at Lattimer Infrastructure Partners, Houston, place and month "Lattimer Infrastructure Partners, Houston, November 2025" (writer may choose a month between December 2024 and 2026 and reports it, so that Ostrander's November 2024 upgrade is known; if the scene is set during refinancing preparation, it must be before the USPP pricing on October 21, 2025 or after the funding, and the writer says which). Characters: Rafael Quintanilla, Carmen Villarreal-Ochoa. Inputs (Annex TR R.2, book inputs): Ostrander Data Systems Inc. is a privately held colocation and build-to-suit data-center operator owned since 2018 by two infrastructure funds (never named); 11 data centers and about 240 MW of committed critical IT load with revenue of USD 0.71 billion in 2020; 21, about 610 MW and USD 1.52 billion in 2024; 34, about 1,050 MW and about USD 2.3 billion run rate in 2026; unrated in 2020, rated B+ on its 2022 debut notes and BB- (one agency) from November 2024; funded by secured term loans, data-center asset-backed notes and build-to-suit leases with hyperscale tenants whose terms Lattimer cannot see. R5 vPPA credit support: a USD 5.0 million standing LC from a bank rated A- or better; Mesa Corta's mark-to-market exposure to Ostrander above USD 10.0 million is collateralized by cash or LC, the threshold falling to nil if Ostrander is rated below B or defaults; Ostrander's exposure to R5 above USD 15.0 million is collateralized by an LC from the opco LC facility; no parent support; termination on failure to post within three business days.

Scene outline: Ostrander, the R5 virtual PPA counterparty (USD 27.85/MWh as generated, settled at West Hub, to June 30, 2033, with RECs; Case Bible 3.4), is growing fast. Rafael sees upside: a buyer that will want more power (and, in 2026, the repowered-R1 idea of Chapter 88). Carmen pulls the settlement data (her habit) and shows which way the settlements run: at base-case prices the strike is below the settlement value, so R5 mostly pays Ostrander and Ostrander's credit matters chiefly in the low price case (if numbers are shown, only the R-F02 rows for R5 vPPA settlements 2024 to 2026: USD 1.7 million, minus 1.3 million and minus 2.5 million). She raises the credit question: Ostrander's growth is funded by leases and debt whose terms Lattimer cannot see, behind a BB- rating, a USD 10.0 million threshold and a USD 5.0 million LC. Rafael's habit ("What's the shape?") appears once. The narration after the box teaches: a data-center operator as a power offtaker is a tenant-credit chain (its revenue comes from hyperscale or neo-cloud tenants; Example 82.4 and ssec:82.5.2), so the R5 vPPA's credit depends on Ostrander's own lease-life gaps; links to Chapter 20 for vPPA credit support and collateral. Figures displayed: Case Bible and Annex R.2 inputs (R5 vPPA price, term, hub; R5 capacity 182.4 MWac and P50 452.2 GWh; Ostrander profile and credit-support terms) and, optionally, the three R-F02 settlement values. The posted collateral amount at any date is not a ledger figure and is never printed.

## 82.11 Practitioner's notebook (outline)

Checklist: tenant list and credit; lease or master lease agreement terms (term, escalator, cancellation, renewal, FX reset); power arrangements and interconnection status; lease-life gap timeline; for towers, tenancy and amendment pipeline, ground lease terms; for fiber, homes passed, penetration curve, churn, competing networks, wholesale contracts; for data centers, MW energized against contracted, PUE, cooling water, RVG terms.

Red flags: debt tenor beyond firm lease without RVG or proven renewal; a single neo-cloud tenant on a short lease; penetration assumptions above the operator's existing footprint; power interconnection "expected" but not contracted; towers dependent on an operator that is merging.

Rules of thumb with limits: data-center debt is tenant debt (limit: multi-tenant colocation with diversified short leases); tower credit improves with each tenant; fiber is an equity risk until penetration is proven.

Common mistakes: lending to building life when the tenant's equipment lives five years; modeling power as a pass-through when the lease does not say so; counting signed letters of intent as leases; ignoring operator consolidation.

Questions experts ask: When does the firm lease end, and what is owed then? Who pays for power, and what if it is not available on time? What does the tenant use the building for, and how long will that technology last? Who else could build a competing network or campus here?

## 82.12 Judgment drill

The situation: you are a credit officer asked to lead a USD 1,163.5 million senior loan (Illustrative) to Abilene Compute Campus LLC (fictional) for a 120 MW AI data center near Abilene, Texas, leased for five years to Northvane Cloud Inc. (fictional), a single neo-cloud tenant (non-investment-grade, contract with a large AI lab behind it), April 2026. The sponsor asks for hyperscale terms: 1.15x DSCR and SOFR plus 250 bps, amortizing over 18 years with a balloon. Credit committee in one week.

Reasoning outline: the verified market (t-market-norms) prices neo-cloud-tenant deals wider (SOFR plus 325 to 425 bps early 2026; CoreWeave's own facility at SOFR plus 4%); apply Framework 82.1: the five-year lease leaves most debt outstanding at expiry with no RVG; the tenant's own revenue depends on an AI-lab contract the lender cannot see in full; the expert sizes to amortize most of the debt within the firm lease, requires a cash sweep, a DSRA, step-in and assignment of the tenant's downstream contract as support where possible, and prices wider.

What would change the answer: an investment-grade parent guarantee or RVG (moves toward hyperscale terms); a 15-year lease (reduces the gap); a second tenant pre-let for part of the capacity; evidence of strong re-leasing demand at the site because of scarce power (supports a balloon, but only with conservative sizing).

## 82.15 Glossary entries owned

Per the glossary canon: tenancy ratio, homes passed, average revenue per user and indefeasible right of use (sec:12.10), colocation (data centers), power usage effectiveness and build-to-suit (sec:12.11) (R-026, R-083), and residual value guarantee (ssec:21.9.2) are homed elsewhere and cross-referenced, not owned. "Master lease agreement" is never abbreviated (R-114). Terms owned here:

| Term | Abbreviation | Definition | Home |
|---|---|---|---|
| colocation (towers) | – | The addition of a second or later tenant's equipment to an existing tower. | ssec:82.2.1 |
| master lease agreement | – | A framework contract under which a mobile operator leases space on many towers from one tower company on common terms. | ssec:82.2.2 |
| penetration (take-up) | – | Connected subscribers as a share of homes passed. | ssec:82.3.2 |
| overbuild (networks) | – | Construction of a competing network over an area already served, splitting the customer base. | ssec:82.3.2 (central change: split from "overbuild (storage)", ssec:73.2.2) |
| hyperscale data center | – | A very large data center built for, and usually leased whole to, one major cloud or AI company. | ssec:82.4.1 |
| IT load | – | The electrical power consumed by computing equipment in a data center, the unit in which capacity is sold. | ssec:82.4.1 |
| triple-net lease | NNN | A lease under which the tenant pays rent plus the property's operating costs, taxes and insurance (and, for data centers, power). | ssec:82.4.3 |
| lease-life gap test | – | Framework 82.1: comparison of firm lease term, debt tenor, technology life and power-contract life to find unsupported debt. | ssec:82.5.1 |
| anticipated repayment date | ARD | The date by which a securitization class is expected to be refinanced, after which cash is swept and step-up interest applies; earlier than the legal final maturity. | ssec:82.7.3 (new; central change: confirm no earlier home in the canon) |
