## 83.1 Header

- Chapter: 83
- Title: Hydrogen and its derivatives, carbon capture and storage, and sustainable fuels
- File: `chapters/83-hydrogen-ccs-and-sustainable-fuels.tex`
- Target length: 13,000 to 15,000 words (three subsectors with first-of-a-kind structures; raised in revision round 1 per standards review defect 7). The target is a floor for the core teaching of each sector, not a cap.
- Dominant lens: an energy-transition project finance banker and DFI officer who has screened first-of-a-kind molecule projects, with the policy specialist's view of state support design.

## 83.2 Purpose and capability statement

Purpose. Hydrogen, captured carbon and sustainable fuels are products whose demand is made by policy and whose price has no deep market. A lender cannot size debt on a price curve that does not exist, so every financeable project in this chapter rests on a contract or a state instrument that creates the price: a long take-if-tendered offtake from a creditworthy sponsor (NEOM), a state that pays most of the capital and absorbs the cross-chain risk (Northern Lights), a licensed transport-and-storage business with revenue support (the UK clusters), a contract for difference (the UK's Low Carbon Hydrogen Agreements), an auctioned premium (the EU Hydrogen Bank), a tax credit, or a cheap, long public loan (Montana Renewables). This chapter teaches the cost economics of each pathway, the revenue models that make them bankable, the risks particular to first-of-a-kind molecule chains, and how to trace who really holds the market risk.

What you will be able to do:
- Compute a levelized cost of hydrogen and its sensitivity to capacity factor and power price, and convert hydrogen into ammonia volume and revenue (Capabilities 4, 13).
- Compare take-or-pay, take-if-tendered and CfD-style revenue support for a molecule project and size the risk each leaves with lenders (Capabilities 2, 5).
- Analyze cross-chain risk in carbon capture and storage and the state's role in absorbing it (Capabilities 8, 9).
- Trace who holds the market risk in any new-molecule project, and judge whether it is bankable (Capabilities 14, 15).

## 83.3 Concepts owned and assumed

Owned:
1. Hydrogen production routes at financier depth: electrolytic (alkaline, PEM) and fossil with capture; electrolyzer efficiency in kWh/kg; stack degradation and replacement; capacity factor and power supply shape.
2. Levelized cost of hydrogen (LCOH) and its drivers (home of the term and of eq:83.1, R-002; levelization arithmetic cited to ssec:5.3.3).
3. Hydrogen derivatives: ammonia, methanol, synthetic fuels; conversion ratios and losses; shipping as a derivative (cross-reference Chapter 76 for gas shipping).
4. Hydrogen revenue models: take-or-pay and take-if-tendered offtakes, sponsor-offtaker structures, and the three state support designs from t-hydrogen-support: the UK's negotiated two-way Low Carbon Hydrogen Agreement (HAR1: strike price against a reference price that is the higher of the achieved price and a gas-linked floor, 15 years), the EU Hydrogen Bank's pay-as-bid fixed premium per kilogram (up to 10 years, output-only), and the US §45V tiered tax credit (dated: construction must begin before January 1, 2028 after OBBBA, previously January 1, 2033; transferable under §6418; constraints cited to sec:67.10); double-sided auctions (H2Global) as a concept only.
5. Market state of hydrogen as of 2025 to 2026 from IEA (t-frontier-data).
6. Carbon capture and storage chain: capture, conditioning, shipping or pipeline, injection, storage; cross-chain risk; storage capacity utilization; long-term storage liability and its transfer to the state (EU CCS Directive, UK 2011 Regulations, US Class VI rules; t-ccs-liability).
7. CCS business models: state aid covering capital and operating costs (Northern Lights), regulated transport-and-storage company with revenue support, a government insurer of last resort and emitter contracts (UK model), storage-as-a-service contracts, US §45Q (dated: USD 17/t for storage, EOR and utilization alike and USD 36/t for direct air capture for facilities placed in service after July 4, 2025, before the prevailing-wage multiplier; recapture over a three-year lookback; transferable).
8. Sustainable fuels: pathways (HEFA, alcohol-to-jet, gasification with Fischer-Tropsch, power-to-liquid), feedstock constraints, the revenue stack of fuel price, green premium and policy credits; US §45Z and the SAF blenders' credit as changed by OBBBA (dated, from saf-project).
9. Framework 83.1, the market-risk holder trace (original).
10. Risks specific to new molecules: first-of-a-kind technology, policy dependence, offtaker concentration and role concentration (sponsor, offtaker and contractor in one party), power supply and shape, chain interfaces, cost overruns.
11. Sector financing terms from verified deals; why market norms do not yet exist.
12. Modeling specifics: hourly or shaped power against electrolyzer utilization, stack replacement, storage buffers, conversion to derivatives, CfD settlement, CO2 volumes against fixed costs, monetization of credits.

Assumed:
- Thermal, wind, solar and battery plants; electricity markets and capture prices: Chapter 11. Oil, gas and LNG chains: Chapter 12.
- CfDs (two-sided, strike and reference), RAB: Chapter 19. Corporate PPAs, shape and basis risk, proxy revenue swaps: Chapter 20.
- LNG tolling and SPAs; ammonia and gas shipping logic: Chapter 76 (and Chapter 21 for SPA form).
- EPC and technology risk; split contracts and wraps: Chapters 22 and 23.
- US tax-credit transfer and tax equity (dated): Chapter 32; US credit constraints after 2025: sec:67.10 (R-073). Blended and concessional finance, climate funds: Chapter 34.
- Levelization arithmetic: ssec:5.3.3 (eq:5.11). Federal loan programs as lenders: Chapter 29.
- ECAs and Islamic tranches: Chapters 29 and 33.
- Industrial ramp-up and yield; Framework 77.1 (margin protection ladder): Chapter 77.
- Merchant tails and bucket sizing: Chapter 36. Hedging construction-period interest: Chapter 37.
- Consolidation of a VIE by a sponsor: Chapter 66.

Forward references: Chapter 84 for carbon markets (compliance and voluntary, Article 6), taxonomies and green loan labels; one or two sentences in 83.3.2 and 83.9. Chapter 88 for evaluating new structures (one sentence in the close).

## 83.4 Locked table of contents

Opening (no heading). May 22, 2023: NEOM Green Hydrogen Company announces financial close at a total investment value of USD 8.4 billion, with approximately USD 6.1 billion of non-recourse financing from 23 banks and financial institutions, for a plant to turn up to about 4 GW of solar and wind power into up to 600 t a day of hydrogen delivered as up to about 1.2 million t a year of ammonia. Every tonne is sold to Air Products under an exclusive 30-year take-if-tendered agreement; Air Products is also a one-third sponsor and the USD 6.7 billion EPC contractor. Three years later Air Products has retreated from its own downstream hydrogen plans and agreed in July 2026 that Yara will market the ammonia it does not use, on commission. Source: neom-hydrogen. The puzzle: the plant has no market price; what did the lenders lend against?

What you will be able to do (unnumbered).

83.1 Selling a product that has no market price (sec:83.1)
- 83.1.1 Policy-made demand (ssec:83.1.1). Why green molecules cost more than grey ones and who pays the difference (offtaker, consumer, taxpayer, compliance market).
- 83.1.2 The hydrogen market in 2024 and 2025 (ssec:83.1.2). IEA (September 2025): global hydrogen demand almost 100 Mt in 2024, low-emissions production under 1%; potential 2030 low-emissions supply from announced projects cut from 49 to 37 Mtpa; operational or FID projects about 4.2 Mtpa by 2030, FID projects 9% of the 2030 pipeline; China about 65% of installed electrolyzer capacity; global installed water electrolysis 2 GW at end-2024. Stegra's repeated capital needs (medium confidence) as an industrial example. Source: t-frontier-data.

83.2 Hydrogen and its derivatives (sec:83.2)
- 83.2.1 Electrolysis and the levelized cost of hydrogen (ssec:83.2.1). Example 83.1; Equation 83.1 (LCOH; home equation, written as an instance of eq:5.11).
- 83.2.2 Ammonia, methanol, and fuels as carriers (ssec:83.2.2). Conversion ratios and losses; Example 83.2; shipping and cracking back to hydrogen as further costs (principle).
- 83.2.3 Offtakes, CfDs, auctions, and tax credits for hydrogen (ssec:83.2.3). Take-or-pay against take-if-tendered; sponsor-offtaker structures. The three support designs side by side as revenue models (t-hydrogen-support): (1) the UK's Hydrogen Allocation Round 1: 11 electrolytic projects (125 MW) selected on December 14, 2023 at a volume-weighted average strike price of GBP 241/MWh (GBP 175/MWh in 2012 prices), with over GBP 2 billion of revenue support expected; award planned for Q1 2024 but 10 projects signed Low Carbon Hydrogen Agreements only by July 23, 2025; the agreement pays strike minus reference on qualifying volumes, where the reference is the higher of the achieved sales price and a floor equal to the lower of the strike and the month-ahead gas reference price, with a 15-year term that erodes if the plant is late and a sales cap (Example 83.3); HAR2 shortlisted 27 projects on April 7, 2025 (no awards verified). (2) The EU Hydrogen Bank: pay-as-bid auctions for a fixed premium per kilogram paid for up to 10 years on certified output, funded from ETS revenues; the pilot's six signed winners bid EUR 0.37 to 0.48/kg (about EUR 694 million over 10 years); the second round selected 15 projects in May 2025 but only six signed (up to EUR 270.6 million) by January 20, 2026; the third selected 13 projects in 2026 at EUR 0.44 to 3.49/kg. (3) US §45V: up to USD 3.00/kg before inflation adjustment for 10 years, tiered by lifecycle emissions, with final rules of January 10, 2025 keeping incrementality, deliverability and hourly matching from January 1, 2030, and OBBBA moving the construction-start deadline from January 1, 2033 to January 1, 2028 (cited to sec:67.10). Lessons: a CfD removes price risk but not volume risk; a fixed premium leaves hydrogen price risk with the producer; a tax credit adds emissions-accounting and rule-change risk; headline awards are not financial closes. Do not state HAR1 awarded in Q1 2024, that all 11 HAR1 projects signed, HAR2 winners or prices, individual HAR1 strike prices, or that §45V was repealed. Double-sided auctions (H2Global): concept only.
- 83.2.4 Power supply and shape (ssec:83.2.4). Dedicated renewables, grid power and PPAs (Chapter 20); capacity factor against power cost; storage buffers; why "4 GW of renewables for 600 t a day" is a shape problem as much as a quantity problem.

83.3 The CCS chain and its business models (sec:83.3)
- 83.3.1 The chain and its interfaces (ssec:83.3.1). Exhibit 83.1 (TikZ chain: emitters, capture plants, ships or pipeline, terminal, injection well, storage); cross-chain risk; Example 83.4.
- 83.3.2 Who pays to store carbon (ssec:83.3.2). Emitter compliance costs and carbon prices (one-sentence forward reference to Chapter 84 for carbon markets); state aid; regulated revenue; §45Q as a transferable US credit: USD 17/t for storage, EOR and utilization alike and USD 36/t for direct air capture for facilities placed in service after July 4, 2025 (base amounts for 2025 and 2026, indexed from 2027; t-ccs-liability), with the parity for EOR and utilization new in OBBBA.
- 83.3.3 Three business models (ssec:83.3.3). Northern Lights state aid (80% of base investment, 50% of specified additional investment, 75% to 100% of eligible operating costs for ten years, capped; separate state-aid agreements with no bilateral contracts between capture and storage participants); the UK model (Ofgem economic licence for transport and storage; Revenue Support Agreement through LCCC; DPA for power and ICC contracts for industrial emitters; GBP 21.7 billion over 25 years committed October 4, 2024; Net Zero Teesside Power and the Northern Endurance Partnership at financial close December 10, 2024; Eni's HyNet network April 24, 2025); storage as a service (Northern Lights Phase 2: NOK 7.5 billion FID in March 2025, at least 5 Mtpa from the second half of 2028, EUR 131 million CEF grant, 15-year 900,000 t a year contract with Stockholm Exergi).
- 83.3.4 Long-term storage liability and its transfer to the state (ssec:83.3.4). From t-ccs-liability: under the EU CCS Directive (2009/31/EC) the operator stays responsible after closure until the authority accepts a transfer, which needs evidence of permanent containment, a minimum period of no less than 20 years (shorter only on evidence), a sealed site and a financial contribution covering at least 30 years of monitoring; financial security must be in place before injection; after transfer the authority can recover costs only for fault. The UK's 2011 Regulations copy this and pass "leakage liabilities" to the authority on licence termination; during operations the UK transport-and-storage business model makes the government insurer of last resort above commercial insurance limits or where cover is unavailable (Supplemental Compensation Agreement, December 2023 heads of terms). The US has no federal transfer: EPA's Class VI rule requires 50 years of post-injection site care by default and financial responsibility instruments, EPA says the Safe Drinking Water Act gives it no power to transfer liability, and primacy states may legislate transfers (Louisiana accepted in 2024; West Virginia, Arizona and Texas primacy in 2025). §45Q recapture applies only over a three-year lookback, and its measurement relies on EPA reporting that EPA proposed in September 2025 to remove (not final). Lender lesson: the transfer rule sets the length of the liability tail to be reserved for, and financial security instruments consume credit lines. Exercise 83.8 and the practitioner's notebook use this. Do not state that transfer is automatic after 20 years, that US law transfers liability after 50 years, state transfer periods, that subpart RR has been repealed, or the sums insured under signed UK agreements.

83.4 Sustainable fuels (sec:83.4)
- 83.4.1 Pathways and feedstocks (ssec:83.4.1). HEFA (waste oils and fats), alcohol-to-jet, gasification with Fischer-Tropsch, power-to-liquid; feedstock scarcity for HEFA; technology maturity for the others.
- 83.4.2 The SAF revenue stack and its policy credits (ssec:83.4.2). Example 83.5; fuel price, green premium from airline offtakers, policy credits. US policy as changed by OBBBA (saf-project): the SAF blenders' credit does not apply after September 30, 2025; for fuel produced after December 31, 2025, §45Z has no separate higher SAF rate; §45Z fuel must come from feedstock grown or produced in the United States, Mexico or Canada; §45Z is extended through December 31, 2029; proposed §45Z regulations were published on February 4, 2026. No credit values are stated. Mandates (for example ReFuelEU Aviation) as a concept only (fact sheet requested).
- 83.4.3 Feedstock contracts, airline offtake, and a DOE-financed SAF plant (ssec:83.4.3). Feedstock supply agreements, price indexation, traceability; airline offtakes as volume commitments with weak price protection (principle; saf-project verifies no offtake contract). Real case from saf-project: Montana Renewables LLC (MRL), an unrestricted, non-recourse subsidiary of Calumet, Inc. at Great Falls, Montana. Warburg Pincus bought USD 250 million of preferred units in its parent in August 2022; on January 10, 2025 MRL signed a USD 1.44 billion DOE-guaranteed loan for 15 years at the Treasury rate plus 3/8%, with debt service deferred until its MaxSAF expansion is commissioned and up to USD 232.8 million of interest capitalized; the first USD 781.8 million tranche (February 18, 2025) mainly refinanced costlier existing debt, with USD 150 million of new Calumet equity; DOE waived an event of default tied to a base cash equity reserve account twice in 2026 (February 10 and June 30); on September 1, 2026 the expansion was cut to about 200 million gallons a year of SAF by end-2028 for USD 137 million of remaining capex, reusing equipment from Calumet's adjacent asphalt refinery, and the remaining DOE commitment fell from up to USD 658 million to one USD 34 million draw, with the March 2029 first payment and December 2039 maturity unchanged. Lessons: refinance-first public loans leave most of the debt drawn before construction; covenant pressure was met by shrinking the project rather than enforcing; one law removed a revenue layer while the plant was in development. Do not state that USD 1.44 billion was disbursed, offtake contracts, airline customers, LCFS or RIN revenues, SAF prices, the reasons for the equity-reserve default, or other SAF financings.

83.5 Who holds the market risk (sec:83.5)
- 83.5.1 The market-risk holder trace (ssec:83.5.1). Framework 83.1 applied to NEOM, Northern Lights, a UK cluster project and Montana Renewables (Exhibit 83.2, Real case columns), with an illustrative HAR1-style electrolytic project as the fifth column (Illustrative).
- 83.5.2 Role concentration (ssec:83.5.2). One party as sponsor, offtaker and EPC contractor: interface risk concentrated, conflicts in enforcing contracts against a shareholder, consolidation of non-recourse debt by the sponsor under US GAAP (Air Products consolidates NGHC as a VIE while the debt stays non-recourse to it).
- 83.5.3 First-of-a-kind and cost risk (ssec:83.5.3). Stegra; the Brevik capture overrun and renegotiation mentioned in northern-lights (state only as the fact sheet frames it); why capture-side cost risk is where CCS projects break; MRL's re-scoping from a USD 1.2 billion megaproject to USD 137 million of remaining capex (company figures, saf-project); EU auction attrition as the hydrogen version of the same risk (t-hydrogen-support).

83.6 Offtake, electrolyzer, and CO2 storage contracts (sec:83.6)
- 83.6.1 Molecule offtake agreements (ssec:83.6.1). Volume, take-if-tendered against take-or-pay, specification, price formula and indexation, termination, credit support. Clause 83.1 (offtake obligation, ammonia sale agreement) with variants a (producer-friendly), b (lender-friendly), c (offtaker-friendly) and a landing paragraph as practitioner observation for 2020 to 2026 first-of-a-kind molecule deals (D-011 style, reviewer sign-off; do not attribute terms to NEOM beyond the fact sheet).
- 83.6.2 Power, electrolyzer, and integration contracts (ssec:83.6.2). Renewable supply and PPAs; electrolyzer supply with performance guarantees (efficiency, degradation); integration EPC (NEOM: Air Products as nominated contractor and system integrator; thyssenkrupp electrolyzers, Topsoe ammonia technology and Air Products air separation as announced in 2020).
- 83.6.3 CO2 transport and storage agreements and government support contracts (ssec:83.6.3). Exhibit 83.3 (TikZ contract map of a UK-style CCS cluster, Illustrative), showing the Supplemental Compensation Agreement and Discontinuation Agreement between the Secretary of State and the T&S company (t-ccs-liability, heads-of-terms structure only).

83.7 How molecule projects have been financed (sec:83.7)
- 83.7.1 Financing terms at NEOM, Northern Lights, and Montana Renewables (ssec:83.7.1). Exhibit 83.4: NEOM (total investment USD 8.5 billion at signing and USD 8.4 billion at close; senior USD 5,852 million plus mezzanine USD 475 million at signing; approximately USD 6.1 billion non-recourse at close, about 73% of project cost per Air Products; NDF/NIF USD 1,500 million; SIDF USD 1,250 million in riyals; a riyal facility at a stated 2.00% rate well below the disclosed floating riyal rate (the concessional inference must be labeled an inference); Euler Hermes-covered tranche; conventional and Islamic tranches; floating debt swapped to fixed in May 2023; green loan second-party opinion by S&P Global; USD 5.4 billion drawn by March 31, 2026); Northern Lights (equity and state aid; no project-finance debt identified); UK cluster financial closes (lenders and amounts not verified); Montana Renewables (DOE-guaranteed USD 1.44 billion at Treasury plus 3/8%, 15 years, deferred debt service, amended September 1, 2026; saf-project); EU Hydrogen Bank grants as output-only revenue, not construction funding (t-hydrogen-support).
- 83.7.2 Contracted offtake and state support in place of market norms (ssec:83.7.2). t-market-norms has none; use Cho's 2026 view that 25% to 30% merchant revenue is the practical maximum before financing becomes difficult (US renewables context) to explain why molecule projects need contracted offtake or public support; no D-011 ranges are offered for this chapter, by design.

83.8 Modeling electrolyzers, conversion, and support payments (sec:83.8)
- 83.8.1 Power profile and electrolyzer utilization (ssec:83.8.1). Hourly or shaped power against electrolyzer minimum load and ramp limits; cross-reference Chapter 45 for energy-yield inputs.
- 83.8.2 Degradation, stack replacement, and conversion (ssec:83.8.2). Efficiency loss over stack life; replacement capex (Example 83.1 includes one replacement); hydrogen-to-ammonia conversion and losses.
- 83.8.3 Support payments, credits, and chain volumes (ssec:83.8.3). CfD settlement on qualifying sold volume with the gas-linked floor (Example 83.3); credit monetization at a discount (cross-reference Chapter 32); CCS fixed-cost recovery against utilized capacity (Example 83.4).

83.9 NEOM Green Hydrogen and the offtaker as the credit (sec:83.9)
Real case from neom-hydrogen. Lesson: with no market price, lenders lend against the offtaker; risk transferred to an offtaker does not disappear, as Air Products' 2026 retreat and the Yara commission arrangement show; concentration of roles has consequences for interface risk, enforcement and accounting. Do not state loan tenors, the offtake price, mezzanine lenders, the full lender list, completion support terms, completion of construction as fact, production achieved or first-ammonia date; "world's largest" only as the sponsors' description. One-sentence forward reference to Chapter 84 for the green loan label.

83.10 Northern Lights and the state as chain integrator (sec:83.10)
Real case from northern-lights. Sequence: conditional decision May 2020; FID December 2020 after the Storting's approval; Longship estimated at NOK 25.1 billion (2021 money, whole chain including ten years of operation; state share NOK 16.8 billion); facilities opened September 2024; first CO2 from Brevik injected August 25, 2025; Phase 2. UK contrast. Lesson: the state can make a chain financeable by absorbing interface risk and most capital and operating cost; storage becomes a service business once third-party contracts appear. Do not state project-finance debt, lender names, customer prices or the JV establishment date; storage depth "about 2,600 metres".

83.11 Walkthrough: marking up a hydrogen offtake term sheet (sec:83.11)
Artifact: an Illustrative ammonia offtake term sheet (Exhibit 83.5). Steps under Walkthroughs.

83.12 Case R: Lattimer passes on Marlowe Gulf Hydrogen (sec:83.12)
Small installment.

83.13 Practitioner's notebook (sec:83.13). 83.14 Judgment drill (sec:83.14).

83.15 Someone must agree to pay before anyone can lend (sec:83.15)
Close. Crystallizes LCOH, the market-risk holder trace, chain risk and the role of the state. Final paragraph poses the next problem: much of the money for these projects arrives labeled "green" or "sustainability-linked", and some of the revenue depends on carbon prices; what do those labels and markets require, and how can a lender tell a real green credential from a claim (Chapter 84).

83.16 Exercises (sec:83.16). 83.17 Solutions to exercises (sec:83.17). Sources.

## 83.5 Opening

NEOM's financial close and the three hats Air Products wears, then the July 2026 Yara arrangement. Facts from neom-hydrogen only; first ammonia "expected in 2027" only as trade reports say.

## 83.6 Worked examples

Example 83.1. Levelized cost of hydrogen (Illustrative). Setting: Pilbara Hydrogen Pty Ltd (fictional), a grid-connected electrolysis plant near Karratha, Western Australia, buying firmed renewable power under a fictional PPA; banking case of 2025; amounts in USD. 384 MW electrolyzer; installed system cost USD 2,215/kW (USD 850.56 million); efficiency 53.0 kWh/kg; capacity factor 58%; power at USD 34.60/MWh; fixed O&M 3% of capex a year (USD 25.52 million); stack replacement of 25% of capex in year 10 (PV USD 97.59 million at 8.1%); water 9 liters per kg at USD 2.10/m3; WACC 8.1%; 25 years (capital recovery factor 0.09448). Output 36,812 t a year. Annualized capital USD 89.58 million; power USD 67.51 million; O&M USD 25.52 million; water USD 0.70 million. LCOH USD 4.98/kg: capital USD 2.433, power USD 1.834, O&M USD 0.693, water USD 0.019 (components shown to three decimals so they sum). Sensitivities: capacity factor 42% gives USD 6.17/kg; 70% gives USD 4.44/kg; power at USD 24.00/MWh gives USD 4.42/kg; at USD 48.00/MWh, USD 5.69/kg. Equation 83.1 (home equation) as an instance of eq:5.11. Teaching point: capital and utilization dominate; cheap power at low capacity factor can cost more than dearer power at high capacity factor. Recomputed in Python (revision round 1; the version-1.0 400 MW and USD 900.0 million inputs were replaced).

Example 83.2. From hydrogen to ammonia (Illustrative). Setting: the Pilbara plant of Example 83.1 adds an ammonia loop in 2026 for export to a fictional Japanese buyer. Using Example 83.1's 36,812 t of hydrogen and a conversion of 0.1795 t of hydrogen per tonne of ammonia (stoichiometric 3/17 = 0.1765 plus losses): 205,080 t of ammonia a year. At USD 640/t, revenue USD 131.3 million. Teaching point: the derivative's price, not hydrogen's, sets revenue; ammonia's existing market gives a reference price but not a premium for low emissions. (Cross-check for the reader: NEOM's up to 600 t a day of hydrogen and up to about 1.2 million t a year of ammonia are consistent with a similar ratio, since 600 x 365/0.1795 is about 1.22 million t; the writer states this only as a consistency check, not as a NEOM fact beyond the fact sheet.) Recomputed in Python.

Example 83.3. A Low Carbon Hydrogen Agreement and volume risk (Illustrative; rebuilt in revision round 1 on the HAR1 mechanics in t-hydrogen-support, simplified). Setting: Wharfedale Green Hydrogen Ltd (fictional), an 18.5 MW electrolytic project in West Yorkshire, England, with a HAR1-style agreement signed in 2025 and a fictional industrial offtaker; amounts in GBP per MWh of hydrogen (higher heating value). Strike price GBP 238.70/MWh (CPI-indexed; about GBP 9.41/kg at 39.41 kWh/kg, computed); qualifying sales 54,300 MWh a year (about 1,378 t); achieved sales price under the offtake GBP 52.00/MWh. Rule: reference price = higher of the achieved price and a floor, where the floor = lower of the strike and the gas reference price; difference payment = (strike - reference) x qualifying volume. Gas reference GBP 31.40/MWh: reference 52.00; payment GBP 10.14 million; total revenue GBP 12.96 million (strike x volume). Gas reference GBP 48.90: unchanged. Gas reference GBP 76.20: the floor (76.20) exceeds the achieved price, so the reference is 76.20; payment GBP 8.82 million; total GBP 11.65 million, GBP 1.31 million less, because the producer sold below a gas-linked floor. If the offtaker takes only 70% of volume (38,010 MWh qualifying), revenue at the low gas price falls to GBP 9.07 million. Teaching points: a CfD removes price risk only for qualifying volume; the gas-linked floor penalizes selling cheaply; lenders still need an offtaker for the molecules (CfD mechanics cross-referenced to Chapter 19). Computed in Python.

Example 83.4. Cross-chain stranding in CCS (Illustrative). Setting: Dogger Store T&S Ltd (fictional), a transport-and-storage company serving a fictional Humber-area cluster in England, testing its 2026 budget; amounts in USD for comparability. A transport-and-storage system with fixed costs of USD 94.7 million a year and variable costs of USD 6.80/t, built for 1.5 Mtpa: unit cost USD 69.93/t. If one emitter's 0.8 Mtpa capture plant fails and only 0.7 Mtpa flows: unit cost USD 142.09/t. Teaching point: under cost-of-service pricing remaining emitters pay double; under fixed tariffs the T&S owner loses; someone (often the state) must absorb the stranding risk; this is why Northern Lights' state aid and the UK's revenue support exist.

Example 83.5. A SAF plant's revenue stack (Illustrative). Setting: Bayou Renewable Fuels LLC (fictional), a HEFA plant near Lake Charles, Louisiana, budgeting in 2025 before the OBBBA changes took effect (saf-project); the credit value is a single illustrative number, not any law's value. HEFA plant processing 350,000 t a year of used cooking oil at USD 1,120/t; product yield 0.83 t per t of feed; processing cost USD 265/t of feed; conventional jet value USD 742/t; airline green premium USD 610/t; policy credit value USD 690/t of fuel (illustrative, not a statement of any law's value). Margin per tonne of feed: with credits USD 309.86 (USD 108.45 million a year); without credits (USD 262.84) (USD 91.99 million loss); jet value only (USD 769.14). Breakevens: feedstock at USD 1,429.86/t with credits; credit value of USD 316.67/t of fuel. Teaching point: the plant is a spread business (Framework 77.1) whose spread is mostly policy; lenders size only on contracted premium and credits with a term matching the debt.

## 83.7 Walkthrough

Walkthrough: marking up a hydrogen offtake term sheet (Section 83.11). Exhibit 83.5 (Illustrative term sheet for a 15-year green ammonia offtake).
1. Volume: annual contract quantity, take-or-pay or take-if-tendered, make-up and carry-forward.
2. Specification and certification: product spec, emissions-intensity certification, consequences of failing either.
3. Price: fixed, indexed (to what), floor; who bears the green premium if policy support changes.
4. Delivery and shipping: point of delivery, shipping responsibility, demurrage.
5. Credit support: parent guarantee, letter of credit, rating triggers.
6. Termination: offtaker's rights (change in law, project delay), termination payments and whether they cover debt.
7. Assignment and direct agreement for lenders (cross-reference Chapter 28).
8. Mark up as lenders' counsel (Clause 83.1b logic) and apply Framework 83.1 to the result.

## 83.8 Frameworks

Framework 83.1, the market-risk holder trace (original; label fw:market-risk-trace). Purpose: find who ultimately bears the price and volume risk of a new molecule. Steps: (1) state the product and its nearest liquid market; (2) list each contract or instrument between the plant and the end-user, and for each say whether it fixes price, volume, both or neither; (3) identify the party that bears the residual (offtaker, state, consumer, lenders, equity); (4) test that party's credit and its incentive to keep performing if the market moves against it; (5) test the term of the protection against the debt tenor; (6) decide bankable volume and price. Demonstrated on NEOM, Northern Lights, Example 83.3 and the Case R installment. Distinct from Chapter 88's new-structure framework, which evaluates whole structures; this trace answers the narrower lender question for molecule projects.

## 83.9 Real cases and fact sheets

- neom-hydrogen: offtaker as credit; role concentration; concessional state funding; ECA and Islamic tranches; sponsor retreat (opening, sec:83.9, ssec:83.7.1, ssec:83.5.2).
- northern-lights: cross-chain risk; state aid; UK model; storage as a service (sec:83.3, sec:83.10).
- t-frontier-data: hydrogen market state; Stegra (ssec:83.1.2, ssec:83.5.3).
- t-us-tax-credits: §45V, §45Q, §45Z existence and transferability (ssec:83.2.3, ssec:83.3.2, ssec:83.4.2), cited with sec:67.10.
- t-market-norms: absence of norms and the merchant ceiling (ssec:83.7.2).
- t-hydrogen-support (placed in revision round 1): ssec:83.2.3 (HAR1, the EU Hydrogen Bank, §45V final rules and the OBBBA deadline), Example 83.3 (LCHA mechanics), ssec:83.5.3 (auction attrition), ssec:83.7.1 and the Case R narration (the §45V deadline in 2024 and after July 2025). Lesson in one line: support schemes are revenue lines with their own price, volume, accounting and rule-change risks, and an award is not a financial close.
- saf-project (Montana Renewables and its DOE loan guarantee; placed in revision round 1): ssec:83.4.2 (OBBBA's SAF changes), ssec:83.4.3 (the real case), ssec:83.5.1 (trace column), ssec:83.5.3 and ssec:83.7.1. Lesson in one line: a SAF plant's spread is mostly policy, and its public lender restructured by shrinking the project when the policy and the cash moved.
- t-ccs-liability (placed in revision round 1): ssec:83.3.2 (§45Q amounts and recapture), ssec:83.3.4 (the liability regimes), ssec:83.6.3 (the UK government support agreements) and Exercise 83.8's discussion. Lesson in one line: who holds stored CO2 after closure, and for how long, sets the tail lenders must reserve for.

Open requests (the chapter can be taught without them):
- fact sheet requested: a specific SAF mandate (for example ReFuelEU Aviation) with dated obligations; until it exists mandates are taught as a concept.
- fact sheet requested: H2Global double-sided auctions (dates, mechanism, results); until it exists they are taught as a concept.
- The version-1.0 requests t-hydrogen-support, saf-project and t-ccs-liability were delivered; t-us-45q-45v is superseded by t-ccs-liability (§45Q) and t-hydrogen-support (§45V).

## 83.10 Running-case installment

Case R (small), story date March to May 2024 (Annex TR R.9); place and month "Lattimer Infrastructure Partners, Houston, May 2024" (the investment committee month; writer may set the opening of the scene at the March 2024 approach). Character: Rafael Quintanilla. Inputs (Annex TR R.3, book inputs): Marlowe Gulf Hydrogen LLC, a venture-backed developer planning a 120 MW electrolyzer near Corpus Christi, approached Lattimer in March 2024 for power from R2 (Sandoval Hills, coastal, South Hub) after the Orchard PPA ends. Proposed terms: 12 years from July 1, 2029; 100% of R2's as-generated output; USD 39.00/MWh flat, settled at South Hub, with hourly-matched RECs; conditional on Marlowe's final investment decision and on its qualifying for the federal clean hydrogen production credit; credit support a USD 4.0 million LC and no parent guarantee. Lattimer's IC passed in May 2024: the offer was conditional, the credit thin, and the price did not compensate for giving up R2's merchant upside after 2029.

Scene outline: Marlowe's proposal for R2 (248.4 MW coastal wind, P50 785.5 GWh; current physical as-generated PPA with Orchard Power Retail at USD 31.40/MWh flat to June 30, 2029; Case Bible 3.3 and 3.4). Rafael asks his habit question ("What's the shape?") and the scene turns on it: the electrolyzer wants steady power and hourly-matched certificates; the wind farm's output is shaped; Marlowe's offtake for its hydrogen is a memorandum of understanding; its §45V eligibility depends on beginning construction before the deadline then in force (in 2024, before January 1, 2033; the narration notes that OBBBA moved it on July 4, 2025 to before January 1, 2028, and that the final rules of January 2025 require hourly matching only from 2030; facts from t-hydrogen-support only). Rafael passes. Narration after the box applies Framework 83.1: the PPA's credit would rest on a developer whose own market-risk holder is not identified, behind a USD 4.0 million LC; teaches that a renewable seller to a molecule project inherits that project's offtake and policy risk. Figures displayed: Case Bible and Annex R.3 inputs only (R2 capacity, P50, current PPA price and term; Marlowe's proposed term, price, volume share and LC). The writer does not compare USD 39.00/MWh with model capture prices unless the ledger gives R2's post-2029 node price (R-F06). State at end: no change.

## 83.11 Practitioner's notebook (outline)

Checklist: identify the nearest liquid market and the price gap; list every price- or volume-fixing contract and its counterparty credit; check policy support terms against debt tenor and change-in-law protection; check power supply shape against electrolyzer or plant needs; check technology guarantees (efficiency, degradation) and integrator wrap; for CCS, map every link's owner and who absorbs stranding; for SAF, check feedstock contracts, certification and credit eligibility.

Red flags: memoranda of understanding presented as offtake; offtake shorter than debt; a sponsor who is offtaker and contractor with no independent enforcement path; CCS links contracted bilaterally with no party absorbing interface risk; revenue that depends on credits with a policy deadline inside the construction period.

Rules of thumb with limits: no contract, no debt (limit: state capital grants and regulated revenue); capacity factor beats power price for LCOH within the normal range; in CCS the capture plant is the weakest link.

Common mistakes: treating a CfD as removing volume risk; modeling electrolyzers at nameplate with flat power; ignoring stack replacement; assuming a green premium persists after policy changes.

Questions experts ask: Who pays if the green premium disappears? What is the bankable volume if the offtaker takes only the minimum? Who absorbs the cost if one link of the chain fails? When does the support end compared with the debt? What did the last first-of-a-kind plant of this type cost against its budget?

## 83.12 Judgment drill

The situation: you are a DFI investment officer (Illustrative), March 2026, considering senior debt for Sokhna e-Methanol SAE (fictional), a 120,000 t a year e-methanol plant in Egypt's Suez Canal Economic Zone. A container shipping line offers a 10-year offtake for 50% of output at a fixed price with CPI indexation; the remaining 50% would be sold on spot to bunkering markets. Debt tenor requested 15 years; sponsor asks for 70% gearing on a USD 1,214.6 million cost. Board in three weeks.

Reasoning outline: apply Framework 83.1: the shipping line holds price risk for half the volume for ten years; the rest and the tail are merchant in a market without depth; t-market-norms' practitioner ceiling of 25% to 30% merchant suggests the merchant half is not bankable on its own; size on contracted volume over the contract term, treat the rest as equity upside or require a second offtaker, consider blended finance (Chapter 34) for the tail, require completion support given first-of-a-kind integration, and test the shipping line's credit and termination rights.

What would change the answer: a second offtake for the remaining volume; state support (CfD or auction) covering the tail; a sponsor with operating experience of the technology; a shorter tenor matching the contract.

## 83.13 Exercise set

Tier 1 (4):
- 83.1: Explain take-or-pay against take-if-tendered from the lenders' side.
- 83.2: Explain cross-chain risk in CCS and how Norway handled it.
- 83.3: Explain why a CfD does not remove volume risk.
- 83.4: Apply the market-risk holder trace to a SAF plant with an airline offtake and a policy credit.

Tier 2 (5):
- 83.5: Compute LCOH for a given electrolyzer at three capacity factors and find the capacity factor at which LCOH equals USD 4.00/kg.
- 83.6: Convert a hydrogen output into ammonia and compute revenue at two ammonia prices, with a 3% conversion loss sensitivity.
- 83.7: Compute CfD payments and producer revenue under three reference prices and two volume outcomes.
- 83.8: Compute CCS unit costs at four utilization levels and the state top-up needed to hold a fixed tariff.
- 83.9: Draft the lender-friendly variant of an ammonia offtake obligation clause and annotate each paragraph.

Tier 3 (4):
- 83.10: Model task: build an hourly-to-annual electrolyzer model with a given wind and solar profile, minimum load and stack degradation; compute LCOH and annual hydrogen (blank workbook; profile data supplied with the exercise).
- 83.11: Case analysis: NEOM. Using neom-hydrogen facts only, write a memo on what changed for lenders between May 2023 and July 2026 and which protections they had.
- 83.12: Case analysis: compare Northern Lights' state-aid model with the UK's regulated T&S model for a lender considering Phase 3 debt.
- 83.13: Case R extension: write Rafael's two-paragraph note to the investment committee explaining the pass decision, using only Case Bible inputs and Framework 83.1.

## 83.15 Glossary entries owned

| Term | Abbreviation | Definition | Home |
|---|---|---|---|
| electrolyzer | – | Equipment that splits water into hydrogen and oxygen using electricity. | 83.2.1 |
| levelized cost of hydrogen | LCOH | The constant price per kilogram of hydrogen that recovers a plant's capital, operating and power costs at a stated cost of capital over its life. | 83.2.1 |
| stack replacement | – | Periodic replacement of an electrolyzer's cell stacks, whose efficiency degrades with operating hours. | 83.2.1 |
| green ammonia | – | Ammonia made from hydrogen produced by electrolysis powered by renewable electricity. | 83.2.2 |
| take-if-tendered | – | An offtake obligation to pay for product only when the seller tenders it for delivery, without a payment obligation for product the seller cannot deliver. | 83.2.3 |
| carbon capture and storage | CCS | Capturing CO2 from an emitter, transporting it and injecting it into a geological formation for permanent storage. | 83.3.1 |
| cross-chain risk | – | The risk that failure or delay in one link of a multi-party chain (capture, transport, storage) strands the investment in the other links. | 83.3.1 |
| transport and storage company | T&S | The entity that owns and operates the CO2 pipelines or ships and the storage site in a CCS chain. | 83.3.3 |
| storage as a service | – | A CCS business model in which a storage operator contracts with several emitters to take their CO2 for a fee. | 83.3.3 |
| sustainable aviation fuel | SAF | Jet fuel made from non-fossil feedstocks or captured carbon that meets jet fuel specifications for blending. | 83.4.1 |
| hydroprocessed esters and fatty acids | HEFA | A SAF and renewable-diesel pathway that hydrotreats waste oils and fats. | 83.4.1 |
| green premium | – | The extra price a buyer pays for a low-emissions product over its conventional equivalent. | 83.4.2 |
| market-risk holder trace | – | Framework 83.1: identifying, link by link, which party bears the price and volume risk of a new molecule and whether that party can carry it for the debt's life. | 83.5.1 |
| role concentration | – | One party acting in several project roles at once, such as sponsor, offtaker and EPC contractor. | 83.5.2 |

- CR-u16-2. Demand-risk sharing instruments for user-pay PPPs (minimum revenue guarantees, revenue-sharing bands, least-present-value-of-revenue concessions) are not assigned in the architecture. If the u12 brief for Chapter 58 already teaches them, Chapter 79 should cross-reference and keep only the sector application. Editor to reconcile.
